• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accreditations are valid for 12 months from date of issue. Any changes to the project or programme that was provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected within this timeframe.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 30 pages. Materials exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under the GCF’s Openness and Transparency Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

## Climate change in Bosnia and Herzegovina (BiH) has been observed through continuous rise of the mean annual temperature and significant seasonal and spatial changes in precipitation, that have become more pronounced over time. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are also increasing and becoming more common. The Third National Communication (TNC) shows that the temperature is expected to increase further until 2050 and the variability of rainfall regime and extreme precipitation will further intensify under warmer climate conditions.

### Approximately 26% of the BiH population is highly vulnerable to climate change induced flood risk. The increasing intensity and frequency of extreme weather events, coupled with a growing population in flood-prone areas, has resulted in significant impacts on people, their property and critical infrastructure. The most important economic sectors affected by flooding are agriculture, energy, transport and tourism. In the last 25 years, floods accounted for 62% of damages from natural disasters in most of the BiH territory. In 2010, the second largest flood disaster in Bosnia and Herzegovina experienced its worst flooding in 150 years which affected 23% of the territory and resulted in 28 deaths and 4,976 people displaced. The floods of 2014 were the most severe since 1993. In 2014, Bosnia and Herzegovina experienced its worst flooding in 150 years which affected 23% of the territory and resulted in 28 deaths and 4,976 people displaced. The floods of 2014 were the most severe since 1993.

### In order to adapt to the intensifying climate induced flood risk, BiH needs to move towards a more proactive disaster risk management approach that prioritizes risk reduction, risk prevention and preparedness through enhanced use of climate information, establishment of a national flood forecasting and early warning system (EFWS) and strengthening of institutional capacities.

### Objective of the project: Scaling up climate-resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina (hereinafter referred to as the Project) is to address increasing vulnerability of BiH communities and livelihoods to climatic extremes, including floods. The Project will contribute to the implementation of a National Climate-resilient flood risk management (FRM) approach, strengthen institutional, technical and financial capacity to implement FRM in Bosnia and Herzegovina, and promote climate-resilient development through integrated and ecosystem-based approaches. The project will strengthen the use of climate information, flood forecasting, early warning systems and disaster risk reduction measures at national level.

### The project is aligned with the new UN multi-partner initiative "Early Warning for All (EW4All)" announced by the UN Secretary General in 2017. It aims to scale up the implementation of EFWSs across vulnerable countries and contribute to the implementation of EWGSM Executive Action Plan developed in March 2020.

### The project will scale-up successful FRM solutions and technologies tested through a UNDP-SDC project in Bosnia and Herzegovina, and will leverage these lessons learned for scaling up climate-resilient flood risk management (FRM) sector by sector. The development of the project is based on the results of the UNDP-SDC project and the experience with sector partner models. The willingness of the BiH private sector to engage with the intervention is recognized through the successful implementation of the UNDP-SDC project in Bosnia and Herzegovina, which has completed the project design phase (Annexes 2 and 7). The project has two Outcomes, and three Outputs as follows:

#### Outcome 1: Climate-resilient flood response governance informed by FRM and increased generation and use of climate information.

- **Output 1**: Fully integrated multi-sector Based Flood Forecasting and EFWS facilitates timely preparation and response to climate-induced flood risk.
- **Output 2**: Vulnerable communities are less exposed to climate-induced flood risks through a combination of structural and non-structural measures.

#### Outcome 2: Non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions mainstreamed in sectorial policies and plans and effectively contribute to protection of people and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risk.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Output 3: Climate proof flood protection measures scaled up through new and improved national and local investment frameworks increasing resilience of most vulnerable groups to climate change

### Socio-economic risk modeling undertaken to assess potential adaptive benefits of the proposed project intervention for baseline and climate change (2050) scenarios for each of the five most vulnerable sub-basins in Bosnia and Herzegovina (BiH). The results show that 799,445 people and 272,811 households are currently at risk from floods (Section 2.3 and Annex E of FS).

### User cost estimates for flood damage were calculated based on the following assumptions:
- Total number of flooded households: 60,000
- Total number of flooded buildings: 306,907 hectares.
- The total damage to household assets is expected to increase from US$2.8 Billion (baseline) in 2015 to US$4.4 Billion in 2050.
- The total damage to building assets is expected to increase from US$2.3 Billion (baseline) in 2015 to US$3.4 Billion in 2050, of which US$3.7 Billion is related to infrastructure.
- Currently, 2,825 business sector assets across the five sub-basins are at risk from flooding and would sustain US$249 Million in asset damage in an extreme flood event even!

### The project targets 799,445 direct beneficiaries by the end of the project (25% of the total population). It will prevent all of the above-mentioned risks to people’s livelihoods, economic assets and will build resilience to flooding in the most vulnerable communities. It will achieve this by:
1. Developing institutional capacity to generate climate risk information for long-term strategic climate-resilient flood management.
2. Scaling up successfully innovative FIRM technologies and implementing a nationwide impact-based Flood Forecasting System (IFS) at basin scale.
3. Implementing local community-based approaches to understand local flood risk reduction and response measures, including community-based early warning systems (CBEWS).
4. Enhancing the capacity of local communities to undertake local flood risk reduction and response measures, including CBEWS.
5. Promoting innovative EBA strategies and nature based FIRM solutions, which enhance the environmental and social benefits of flood management.
6. Enhancing long-term flood risk investment planning and enabling environment for climate risk financing to achieve sustainable development goals and objectives.
7. Engaging with public sector institutions and private sector services and FIRM activities and facilitating an increased use of climate risk information in their operations.

* *NFC for Bosnia and Herzegovina. See also Section 3.1 of the FS.
* Based on flood socio-economic risk modeling undertaken for the proposed project (See Section 2.3, Table 2.6 of FS). In addition, the 2014 food security survey was used to estimate user costs.
* Based on up-to-date flood event information recorded by HMs and WRA in Bosnia. See Sections 2.2.1 and 2.2.2 (and tables 2.4 and 2.5 of FS).
* * Technology transfer for climate resilient flood management in Vitasi River basin (Vitasi project, 2015-2020)

### B. Project/Programme Information

#### B.1. Background (1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

**Climate Change Problem**

**Geophysical and Developmental Context**

* **Bosnia is a sovereign state with a decentralized political and administrative structure consisting of two Entities: The Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina and the Republika Srpska.**
* **The country has a population of approximately 3.5 million people, making it one of the most densely populated countries in Europe with an estimated 3,531,500 inhabitants and total surface area of 51,292 km². Agriculture remains the largest sector of Bosnia's economy, employing about 48% of the workforce.**
* **The Bosnian War (1992–1995) had a devastating impact on human, social and economic resources of the country, leading to enormous physical destruction and loss of life. The war also left behind a legacy of environmental degradation and severely weakened management capacities of the country through the direct damage and lack of maintenance of flood control facilities.**
* **The Bosnian economy has been in transition since 1995 from a centrally planned economy to a market economy, which has been further compounded by the transition towards a market economy requiring structural reforms and privatization.**

**There are two main river basin systems and seven major rivers in Bosnia:** 
- **Save (Sava, Una-Sana, Una, Limnica, Bosna and Drina)**
- **Adriatic Sea Basin (Neretva and Trebišnjica rivers)**

* **The Save is the largest river of the country and the second longest river in Europe. It flows through Bosnia from north to south and empties into the Adriatic Sea.**
* **Save sub-basins are highlands of mainly fluvial, peatland and forested lands.**
- **Una-Sana sub-basin is a large basin with many rivers flowing into it. The Una river is one of the longest rivers fully contained within it, stretching from central Bosnia to its source near Sarajevo to Sava in the south.**
* **The Neretva River flows through southern Bosnia and empties into the Adriatic Sea at Neum. It is the largest river in the country by discharge volume. The Trebišnjica River is a tributary of the Neretva, the longest karst river in the Dinaric Alps, the main river of Herzegovina and the very long river in Bosnia.**
* **The Drina River flows through northern Bosnia and empties into the Danube at Sremska Mitrovica. It is one of the 18km length flows underground. Both Neretva and Trebišnjica are heavily modified by HPP dams. These Adriatic Sea sub-basins are characterized mainly from ground due to their karstic geology.**

**Observed and Projected Climate Change in Bosnia**

* **All B&H National Communications to UNFCCC (INC, CNC, TNC) have identified that climate is affecting agriculture and water resources during the remainder of the twenty-first century. According to the TNC, meteorological observations show a significant increase in temperature over the last 50 years.**
* **The entire territory of B&H trends in annual temperatures on all analyzed stations were found to be statistically significantly positive with an average increase of +1.2°C per decade since 1961. The mean annual temperature ranges from 0.4–1.0°C, while the increase in temperature during the growing season (April–September) reaches 1.7°C.**
* **The number of days with extreme heat has increased significantly over time and there are more positive trends, while indices of cold temperature extremes have negative trends. The most significant change in this period is a decrease in the number of frost-free days.**
* **In meteorological stations the number of cold days (FD) has a negative trend. In the central mountain areas the number of days with snow cover (SD) has an increasing trend, while in the lowlands it has a decreasing trend and less than 5 years per decade. The number of warm days (SD) has a positive trend and is less than 20 years per decade.**

* **The period from 1961 to 2014 most of the B&H territory was characterized by a slight increase in the amount of observed annual rainfall (TNC). Analyses of historical records carried out for the FNC(IV) draft show that, compared:**

To the reference period (1961-1990), there was an increase in annual maximum rainfall in the Sava Basin of 16.4%, 22.5% and 22.8% for the periods of 1991–2010, 1991–2014 and 1991-2018 respectively (the largest flood was in 2013). The increase is also observed in the annual maximum rainfall over the entire territory of Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the reference period, the records also show that changes in the amount of rainfall are more pronounced by seasons than by months. The most significant increase in the amount of rainfall occurred during winter (from 20% to 35%) and spring (from 18% to 27%), while during autumn the increase in rainfall was observed, especially in the north-western part of Bosnia and Herzegovina (from 4% to 20%). The most significant decrease in annual maximum rainfall occurred from 2010-2019: MAM (2004, 2016, 2018); JJA (2014; 2019); SON (2014; 2015). Prior to this, the floods were recorded in 2003 and 2007. The most significant increase in annual maximum rainfall occurred during winter (from 6% to 18%) and spring time (20%). The results of observed flow data trend analysis (FDC) demonstrate strong changes in seasonal regime of river discharge, especially for the period from 2004-2019.

### Analyses (TNC: FNC)

Show that there has also been a reduced amount of snowfall in winter months due to increased daily winter temperatures. Hence, lower precipitation falling as rain instead of being stored as snow, is considered to be one of the main reasons for the observed increase in annual maximum rainfall and decrease in spring snowmelt runoff.

Furthermore, late snowfalls have been observed, which resulted in flooding in spring months (March–May) over the entire territory of Bosnia and Herzegovina. These observed changes are the precipitation regime, including increasing maximum annual rainfall, decrease in winter snowfall and increase in spring snowmelt runoff. The observed changes in precipitation regime increased likelihood of floods due to the increased runoff and an increasing intensity and frequency of rain events.

According to the TNC: FNC analysis, the most significant increase in annual maximum rainfall occurred during winter (from 20% to 35%) and spring time (20%). The results of observed flow data trend analysis (FDC) demonstrate strong changes in seasonal regime of river discharge, especially for the period from 2004-2019.

### B.3.1 TNC projections

Show that extreme precipitation will intensify under warmer climate conditions. Scenarios RCP8.5 and A2 anticipate an increase in annual precipitation for the time horizon 2011–2040 in relation to the reference period (1971-2000) (+3% for most of the B&H territory). Precipitation is projected to increase in winter, spring and autumn. The scenario RCP8.5 anticipates an increase in annual precipitation by 6–10% for all time horizons. Scenarios A1B, A2C and RCP8.5, downscaled for Bosnia, also anticipate further increase in variability of precipitation regime with a higher frequency of extreme events (e.g., heavy rainfall) and changes relative to the reference period.

For all four scenarios (RCP8.5, RCP 2.6, RPS 4–10, A1B), the projected change is an increase in annual maximum rainfall for all time horizons relative to the reference period.

### B.3.2 TNC projections

While climate change projection from the TNC and the FNC for the periods beyond the reference show that there will be a positive change in rainfall amounts for the near future (up to 2050), this is not associated with an increase in annual rainfall amount, but rather an increase in rainfall amount on annual and seasonal basis at all seasons except summer, contributing to increased flood risk. Furthermore, changes in index of evapotranspiration.

See Section 2.1.4 for FFS.

The observed trends in precipitation and drought (Section 1.4.4) point out that the last eight decades will not be decisive as the decade with the largest number of 8 summners, which indicates that the last decade saw an increase in frequency of summer droughts. The last decade also had a higher frequency of extreme events compared to another with a period with the longest number of wet summers.

In addition to this summary, the last decade did see an increase in annual maximum rainfall and decrease in winter snowfall and spring snowmelt runoff. These observed changes are the precipitation regime, including increasing maximum annual rainfall, decrease in winter snowfall and increase in spring snowmelt runoff.

## Climate Change Impacts

### Historical flooding in B4M

15. The increased intensity, frequency and variability of rainfall due to climate change observed in the past decades has led to more frequent and intense and more geographically extensive floods. Years with floods have become increasingly common. In the last two decades, Bosnia and Herzegovina has had six very severe events, with 2014 being the most extreme year on record.

16. Major flood events in the preceding 75 years (1938 to 2013):

* In April 2004 significant floods affected 48 municipalities and destroyed or damaged 1,200 houses.
* In May 2004, floods caused damage to 6,000 agricultural land and 300,000 people were endangered by floods of which several hundred families were evacuated. The total economic loss was estimated at €5 million.
* In June 1987, the flood in Bosnia and Herzegovina affected 24 municipalities, destroyed or damaged 650 houses, and caused damage to 3,000 agricultural land. In the Republic of Srpska about 100,000 hectares of land were flooded.
* 48 houses were destroyed, 1,513 people evacuated, 10 bridges were damaged; and 196 bridges were recorded**.

17. In May 2014, floods in Bosnia and Herzegovina affected the entire region with up to three times the normal May total and half the annual average rainfall falling in a 5 day period**. The country was hit by the most intense flooding since records began in 1938.

18. The events of the last decades show that floods have been escalating in B4M due to strong regional precipitation, which has caused catastrophic floods. The flood risk has also been increasing due to mild winters with scarce snow cover and a longer period of snowmelt. This is consistent with climate change projections.

19. Of the two main river basins in B4M – Sava (Vrbas, Una-Gračanica, Una, Vrbas, Drina and Trebižat) and Adriatic Sea Basin (Sana-Bosna and Trebižat rivers) - Sava river basin is at highest risk of flooding, while Adriatic Sea Basin

# B

## Social and economic vulnerability

### Climate change is of significant impact on the most important economic sectors including agriculture, water management, and hydropower, and BMI already low adaptability capacity.

As outlined above, changes in rainfall patterns are expected to increase the frequency and intensity of floods and increasing economic damage from floods have been significant, especially in the water agricultural and hydroelectric sectors. The economic impact of the 2014 floods is believed to have caused losses equivalent to about 3% or 15% of BMI's GDP (see Section 2.3 of FIS).

### Agriculture employs approximately 20% of the total work force and 60% of land use in BMI and is the most important economic sector. The agricultural sector can be severely affected depending on the timing of the flood during the growing period (see Section 2.2, Annex E and Section 18.5 of FIS). In addition, agricultural land can be damaged by floods through erosion, sedimentation, and salinization. Furthermore, the sediment load and debris carried by flash floods to agricultural land result in significant losses and abandonment of productive land (see Section 3.2.4 of FIS).

The most vulnerable areas are located on the coast of BMI, in the Sana Basin, Voioba, in the Sava basin, and Nereina and Treblelinda in the Adriatic Sea basin), a single extreme flood event can cause significant damage to agricultural land and infrastructure, resulting in major changes in the agricultural sector. This increases to 150,694 ha and $27.71 million USD under climate change (2050).

25. The hydropower potential of BAH is estimated at 8,000 megawatts (MW), with technical potential of 6,800 MW and commercial potential of 3,800 MW, placing the country among the leading hydropower producers in Southeast Asia. Large hydropower plants are part of 3 existing power companies in BAH, which are joint stock companies. The construction of large dams on rivers with high sediment loads can lead to increased sedimentation events that lead to increased runoff and increased peak river flows could result in reduced power production and damage to the infrastructure. In addition, the release of water from these reservoirs is not always synchronized with demand and may need to adjust their discharge before the events, and a result significantly aggravated the flood situation. The Adrijac Bok Reservoir in Bosnia and Herzegovina experienced an extreme variability in generation due to climate change. While generally designed and licensed to operate at full capacity during dry periods, the reservoir was unable to meet demand for power production when forecasting to minimize climate change impacts on their operations (Annex C of GFS). In the Sava basin sub-basins in Bosnia and Herzegovina, HPPs are vulnerable to increased sedimentation due to climate change. The increase in sediment load can lead to reduced capacity of HPPs in HPFPs. While increased flood risk could result in damage to HPPs and the type and size of flood events that may be more frequent or intense than those experienced historically, there is no evidence for protection of population. Hence HPPs are included in the project in order to optimise their essential dual role in flood management and power production while also taking into account climate change impacts on sediment load and forecasting. In addition, the risk from a falling dam, although of low probability, would result in significant damages to property and infrastructure as well as loss of life. The average annual production for the basin is 152 GWh, with positive impact in the near period on the level of average annual production for the baseline at an increase of 44.6 million kWh (30%). However, the losses and those that may result in the downstream area could be significant.

26. BAH has the highest proportion of forests and the largest variety of forest species in the Western Balkans. According to the latest data from the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of Bosnia and Herzegovina 11% of its forest cover, or around 25,000 hectares. However, taking into account the total area of land under forest management (including areas outside the forests), this figure increases to 34%. Between 1986 expansion) during 1990-2005, BAH lost 4.4% of its forest and woodland habitat. The increase in average temperature has led to a significant decrease in snow cover duration (by up to 70 days per year), compared to the communal communities. The assumed changes in forest ecosystems indicate that drastic changes would be expected by 2100, with an estimated loss of 35% of total forest area and 48% of woodland habitat in four macro regions in BAH (vegetation ecology areas). The Drina Region, a very important wetland and centre of endemic species, is particularly vulnerable to climate change. This region has experienced significant fires as deforestation and land degradation" which will result in shifting water supplies and distribution of agricultural pests. In addition, the increase in temperature and decrease in precipitation are expected to lead to more frequent events and impact on people and the assets in river basins and sub-catchments (See FS Section 10.2). Also increasing temperatures can lead to a significant reduction in snow cover duration which will have an adverse effect on TNCs) have suggested a number of mitigation measures including increase in forest productivity and management or increased use of agroforestry systems. However, the impacts of climate change are complex and multiple actions are needed to address any climate-induced changes that will impact the structure and density of the forest cover, which is also affected by other factors such as land-use change and deforestation. The most significant threat is a noticeable lack of integration of climate change and FIRM considerations into forestry policies and strategies.

These developments do not take account of flood risk nor do flood zones include flood resilient land use and construction rules. As a result, many buildings are located on flood-prone lands and in flood-prone areas. ## Institutional, Legislative and Policy Context

28. BiH is a sovereign state with a decentralized political and administrative structure. BiH consists of two Entities (the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (FBiH) and the Republic of Srpska (RS)) and one Brčko District. The Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina is subdivided into 10 Cantons, Decision-making processes in the cantonal level are based on a system of checks and balances between the three branches of government: executive, legislative and judicial. The State of BiH has responsibility for environmental issues through policies, regulations and standards. The State of Bosnia and Herzegovina as the central authority is responsible for foreign policy, defense, border security, and international relations. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs of BiH is responsible for coordinating the inter-entity relationships between the Entities (FBiH and RS) and the Brčko District. The Brčko District has its own authorities with responsibilities in water sector including adoption of new legislation and implementation of EU directives. The lack of a State-level framework and the confederal character of BiH and RS, and the different levels of responsibility for water management issues at the cantonal level, create significant discrepancies between the Entities (Ministry for Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of RS and Ministry for Agriculture, Water Management and Spatial Planning of FBiH) in their management of shared water resources across the borders. The State-level authorities therefore have no responsibility for regulating these inter-entity relations.

29. The two Entities and the Brčko District have relevant political, administrative and legal jurisdiction in their own territory. The Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina is subdivided into 10 Cantons which have their own authorities (ministries) with responsibilities in the water sector, including adoption of new legislation and implementation of EU directives. The lack of a State-level framework and the confederal character of BiH and RS, and the different levels of responsibility for water management issues at the cantonal level, creates significant discrepancies between the Entities (Ministry for Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of RS and Ministry for Agriculture, Water Management and Spatial Planning of FBiH) in their management of shared water resources across the borders. The State-level authorities therefore have no responsibility for regulating these inter-entity relations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## B

**30. The Law on Water of Republic Srpska was aligned with the EU Floods Directive in 2017 to provide the legal basis for better flood risk management and defines the method of flood risk assessment and management, in accordance with the EU Floods Directive.**

The law has been officially submitted to the FSB for adoption by the end of 2017 and is not yet passed.

**31. The FBaH Environmental Protection Strategy (2012-2025) is an integral part in water management planning, which includes a number of projects that are currently being implemented. These include climate change adaptation but do not include concrete adaptation measures to address it. These entity strategies require coordination and harmonization with the national strategy.**

**32. BaH’s Climate Change Adaptation and Low Emission Development Strategy (Adopted in 2013) is of key importance to defining and planning adaptation action. As one of the worst impacts of climate change in BaH are the increased intensity and variability of rainfall, which leads to flooding and damage to agriculture and forest ecosystems caused by changes in rainfall and extreme weather. The lack of reliable hydro-meteorological data and monitoring systems is a major obstacle for effective adaptation strategies.**

The Strategy also identified the need for improvements in infrastructure (both flood protection and water storage infrastructure) as well as the development of new technologies and practices to reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts.

Key priority areas were identified as priority research projects: Strengthened hydro-meteorological monitoring, establishment of the early warning system, and adaptation measures. The Strategy was updated in 2016 with a focus on flood risk management and Low Emission Development. The Strategy was updated in 2020 and includes adaptation priorities for 2020 to 2030, with 4 implementation priorities: (i) Strengthening the early warning system; (ii) Improving climate change data collection and quantified and effectively communicated, supporting evidence-based policy development. Outcome 2: An effective institutional framework is in place to support adaptation planning and decision-making processes that address climate change risks and opportunities across sectors. Outcome 3: Adaptation approaches are mainstreamed into decision-making processes at all levels of government and by key stakeholders. Outcome 4: Climate change adaptation is effectively implemented, monitored and evaluated.

A well-resourced Adaptation Strategy being implemented on time, effectively, and delivering the expected outcomes.

**In 2017, BaH submitted Third National Communication (TNC) to the Conference of Parties to the UNFCCC**, which summarizes information on vulnerabilities to climate change, steps taken to adapt to climate change impacts, and mitigation actions. The TNC highlights that “due to the increased intensity of rainfall and its greater variability, as well as due to the increase in temperature, there is a significant risk of flooding in urban areas and agricultural land in the north-eastern part of BaH.” BaH’s Climate Change Adaptation and Low Emission Development Strategy is of key importance for defining and planning adaptation action.

**The Government has initiated the development of the National Adaptation Plan which should lay out specific activities with timelines, indicative costs and implementing entities necessary to combat climate change consequences. The plan will be submitted in 2018 as a response to the UNFCCC’s call for countries to submit NAPs by 2020.**

**Key strategic documents in BaH including water management strategies and rural development strategies point out that climate change is one of the major challenges facing operations, but these strategies do not propose specific mitigation future risks or steps to adapt to climate change.**

At the national level, the system for flood protection is protected by the Ministry of Security through the Sector for Protection and Prevention of Natural Hazards policy design. However, because of the decentralized nature of the system, the responsibility for flood risk management lies with the competent authorities at the municipal, cantonal and entity levels. The District Ministry of Security and the Coordinating Body of BaH (composed of the Council of Ministers, five representatives from the municipalities, and five representatives from the cantons) are responsible for coordinating in the event of a large-scale accident with trans-boundary effects which cannot be resolved at the entity level. Local governments have responsibility to ensure that flood risk management is integrated into their development plans on municipal, cantonal and entity levels. There is an emergency warning center being organized on BaH country/entity level, but it is still not in operation. Split responsibilities in the past have already led to artificially triggered floods.

# Baseline Projects

Chapter 7 of the FS provides a description of the baseline projects and detailed discussions of the baseline project being scaled up. It is summarized in the next paragraph.

## Technology Transfer for Climate Resilient Flood Risk Management (Vibas project)

USD 3 million SCCF grant provided by GCF was used to scale-up the prototype Vibas project for the proposed GCF intervention. The SCCF funds were used to enable the communities of the Vibas basin (12% of BAH) to adapt to flood risks through a combination of structural and non-structural measures. These included policy amendments, upgrading and rehabilitation of the hydrometric monitoring network and the hydrodynamic models and GIS tools for the Vibas River Basin incorporating climate change predictions and projections. The project also strengthened the capacity of the communities in flood risk management forecasting and enhancing the existing early warning system within the VTB; development of emergency response plans; and training of 100 community members on disaster preparedness, response and recovery.

The project has resulted in a significant increase in the capacity development of over 100 practitioners and decision makers. Further, an institutional capacity development plan was developed to ensure that the lessons learned from the Vibas project are scaled up across BAH. The project also worked with affected communities to introduce climate resilient community based non-structural measures and provided technical assistance for the development of flood risk management plans in line with national planning regulations and recommended revisions to strengthen the legislation that govern activities influencing flood risks.

The project has resulted in a significant increase in the capacity development of over 100 practitioners and decision makers. Further, an institutional capacity development plan was developed which focuses on the promotion of flood zoning as a means of efficient flood risk management, or using land use planning to reduce flood risk. The project also worked with affected communities to introduce climate resilient community based non-structural measures and provided technical assistance for the development of flood risk management plans in line with national planning regulations and recommended revisions to strengthen the legislation that govern activities influencing flood risks.

## Main Emergency Response Project (Sub-Activity 2.1.3, not WBP loan)

### Objectives of the project to restore functionality of critical infrastructure and public services and reduce risk after 2014 floods.

#### - capacity building for disaster management
#### - emergency disaster recovery goods
#### - rehabilitation of key public infrastructure
#### - capacity building

### Upgrade
The Project will build on lessons learned in recovery using non-structural measures approach and will use the following approaches:

- **Gap filling:** Climate hazard and risk modeling data was not available for the design of reinforcement measures for this critical infrastructure.
- **Capacity building for disaster management**
- **Emergency disaster recovery goods**

The Project will develop the hazard and risk modeling which will be used to identify areas where additional capacity is needed, particularly in critical infrastructure (Activity 1.2). The Project will develop risk reduction measures for identified critical infrastructure (Activity 1.3) and will also develop climate proof future structural investments and risk reduction.

The Project is developing floodplain zoning policy and legislation which will be used to identify areas where vulnerable land use can be zoned away from high-risk areas (Sub-Activity 2.1.3.1). The project will also develop a methodology for the identification of critical infrastructure that has to be placed in high-risk areas, the project is embedding the capacity building and risk reduction measures into the rehabilitation of all critical infrastructure (Sub Activity 2.1.3. FS pp. 209-215).

Through sub activity 1.3A (FS pp. 220-221), project will develop a methodology for the identification of critical infrastructure embedding flood hazard risk and risk modeling approaches and FFEWS and food management.

Through Activity 1.4 (FS pp. 221-222) project will develop and implement protocols and SoFs on data generation, data management and analysis, and climate impact based FFEWS and food management.

Through Project activity 2.2 (FS pp. 240-247) project will integrate flood hazard risk reduction measures into the disaster reduction and climate change adaptation methods (which integrates flood hazard risk reduction with other risks such as water risk), while through Activity 3.3 (FS pp. 260-265) project will develop a methodology for the identification of critical infrastructure embedding flood hazard risk, and risk modeling approaches and FFEWS and food management.

The Project will also study economic impacts of climate change on the hydropower sector in Republica Srpska, Bosnia and Herzegovina (Sub Activity 3.1). The project will develop system operations and will provide essential input observation data to support the development of flood hazard risk reduction measures.

Knowledge gained on CC impact on HPP electricity production and agriculture will be of help for modeling and early warning systems.

Monitoring and evaluation system will set a base for data exchange protocols.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Barriers to climate resilient flood risk management in B&H

### 1. Limited institutional capacity (technical, legislative, and financial) for nation-wide Flood Forecasting and Early Warning System (FFEWS)

#### 42. Use of FFEWS: The information network and capacities: Financial and human resource constraints coupled with a severely reduced monitoring network since the 1992-1995 wet period limit B&H’s ability to monitor important river basins, which are critical for flood risk management in B&H. This limits the use of FFEWS services.

#### 43. Gaps in flood hazard and risk modeling and mapping capacities: There is, as yet no comprehensive flood hazard, risk or vulnerability assessment at a national level. The development of such assessments has been severely delayed and will be done for selected high-risk areas within river basins. Technical capacities related to hazard and risk modeling and mapping are weak across institutions and governance levels. Hence climate risk information for effective risk management is limited.

#### 44. Gaps in FFEWS capabilities: FFEWS for most of the main watercourses are under development, but these systems do not cover tributaries or torrents. Existing FFEWSs are not integrated into a country-wide system, and there is no harmonization between them. The lack of an effective early warning system has led to ineffective response after natural disasters are cumbersome and inefficient. The regulatory frameworks for FFEWS is incomplete as it lacks provisions on the responsibilities of different stakeholders in the development and implementation of the protocol on early warning systems.

#### 45. Lack of a strategic integrated FFRM approach, insufficient technical knowledge, and capacities on ecosystem-based and non-structural approaches to climate resilient flood risk reduction:

There is a lack of strategic integrated flood-risk management approaches in B&H that identify the best combination of structural and non-structural measures to address flood risks. The current flood risk management approach in B&H currently relies on limited structural measures that are becoming increasingly ineffective in terms of the Standards of Protection they provide. There is also a lack of technical knowledge and capacities for ecosystem-based approaches, which lack basin scale appraisal-led approach to the identification of flood needs, and lack detailed assessment of risk and vulnerability at the local level. The current flood risk management approach in B&H is informed from a pre-war communist system; there is insufficient knowledge about non-structural measures and limited capacity to implement them. There are also no specific institutional arrangements for flood risk management approach in B&H that identifies the best combination of structural and non-structural measures to address flood risks.

### 2. Lack of a strategic integrated FFRM approach, insufficient technical knowledge, and capacities on ecosystem-based and non-structural approaches to climate resilient flood risk reduction:

There is a lack of strategic integrated flood-risk management approaches in B&H that identify the best combination of structural and non-structural measures to address flood risks. The current flood risk management approach in B&H currently relies on limited structural measures that are becoming increasingly ineffective in terms of the Standards of Protection they provide. There is also a lack of technical knowledge and capacities for ecosystem-based approaches, which lack basin scale appraisal-led approach to the identification of flood needs, and lack detailed assessment of risk and vulnerability at the local level. The current flood risk management approach in B&H is informed from a pre-war communist system; there is insufficient knowledge about non-structural measures and limited capacity to implement them. There are also no specific institutional arrangements for flood risk management approach in B&H that identifies the best combination of structural and non-structural measures to address flood risks.


---
**Page 24**
---

### B

#### 3. a) Outlining a climate-risk-informed approach to flood defense design and b) lack of capacity, knowledge and information for enhanced climate-proof public and private investments into flood risk reduction and community resilience

At the national and community levels, there is a lack of knowledge, tools, and investment to systematically respond and build resilience to flood risk. The population, in particular in rural areas, is lacking general awareness about flood risks and their consequences. There are also limited institutional arrangements and capacities among local communities to apply climate resilient livelihoods and adaptive strategies for coping with climate change impacts. In addition, there is a lack of investment into flood risk reduction measures as well as a lack of risk information building such awareness and capacities.

#### 4. Lack of financial resilience of communities due to a lack of climate-risk-informed risk transfer instruments in the public and private sectors

There is a lack of engagement with productive sector stakeholders, particularly in the agricultural and productive sectors in climate responsive FIRM. In order to ensure sustainability of the FIRM practices, particular attention should be given to the maintenance of water resources, the maintenance of flood defenses, and all other flood risk management activities; there needs to be an engagement with large-scale investors and private sector stakeholders as well as a greater awareness among the public at large. Interest on the in interest is and willingness to pay is currently lacking, as are the mechanisms, instruments and tools for such investments.

#### 8.2.2. Theory of change narrative diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The Project has been developed to address the barriers to integrated climate-informed flood risk management and to support the development of a national FIRM strategy in order to avoid loss of lives and to reduce economic and infrastructure losses caused by climate-induced flood disasters.

#### 5. In the baseline scenario, without the Project

In the baseline scenario, without the Project the lack of technical, institutional, and financial capacities to implement an integrated FIRM strategy will continue to be a barrier to reducing flood risk. The limited time horizon of accuracy, skill and spatial extent of flood warnings not reaching the communities who are most affected by flood risks, coupled with the absence of a national FIRM strategy, will continue to be a barrier. Furthermore, legislative and policy framework will continue to exacerbate weak land use, spatial planning and climate risk management practices. The lack of institutional arrangements and capacities for climate change adaptation withoutロックed capacities and adaptive strategies to adapt to climate change and the capacity to manage and monitor flood risks will continue to be a barrier. Without an integrated FIRM strategy and investment in FIRM, the BAHM population and economy will face increasing pressures from more frequent and severe climate change impacts, including increased frequency of extreme weather events, which will result in greater flood disasters and associated impacts on GDP. In addition, a continued lack of financial resilience will further limit recovery capacity, enabling and limiting further sustainable development gains. Scaling up of tested integrated FIRM approaches at the national level will require additional investment into climate change adaptation and required investments as well as an underdeveloped national capacity for flood hazard risk knowledge.

#### 6. In the adaptation alternative

In the adaptation alternative, this Project will support the commitment of the BAHM government to avoid losses of lives and reduce economic and infrastructure losses caused by climate-induced flood disasters through scaling up a Theory of Change described below. The Project will scale-up technical, institutional and financial investment into an integrated FIRM strategy.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Theory of Change

### Project goal statement: IF flood risk management in BAH is transformed towards a more proactive, integrated and climate-informed system supported by gender responsive climate risk informed FRM policies, strategies, and practices, then the frequency, intensity, duration, and spatial extent of flooding will be reduced, as well as the intensity of climate-induced floods and will face reduced loss of assets, infrastructure and livelihoods BECAUSE OF:

1. The implementation of a set of integrated flood risk management measures supported by gender responsive policies and plans and effectively contribute to protection of people and livelihoods from climate induced flood risk.
2. The implementation of a set of integrated flood risk management measures supported by gender responsive policies and plans and effectively contribute to the resilience of vulnerable communities in BAH towards climate-induced flooding.

### As outlined in the goal statement, the project will catalyse a paradigm shift in flood risk management in BAH by transforming it from a reactive, sectoral approach to an integrated, multi-sectoral approach. The project will also provide an opportunity for an ex-ante, i.e., informed preparation approach based on adequate climate information and risk knowledge.

### These paradigm shifts are further described in Section D.2 below.

#### To achieve the GCF paradigm shift and deliver the project goal, the project will:

1. Strengthen the implementation of integrated flood risk management (FRM) and enhanced generation and use of climate data reduce vulnerability to flood related disasters; and Outcome 2: Vulnerable communities in BAH including women and children will be better protected from climate-induced flooding through a combination of structural and non-structural flood risk reduction solutions. Through these outcomes, the project will contribute to the achievement of the following GCF objectives:

1. Scaling up the use of state-of-the-art technologies in flood monitoring, modeling, impact based forecasting and early warning systems, as well as the implementation of cost-effective EAS measures to complement traditional structural solutions.
2. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the new UN initiative EWS4All announced at COP 21.

#### While pursuing these Outcomes, the project will also leverage several important social co-benefits:

- Improved health and reduced health risks in communities due to the reduction in flood risk.
- Reduced economic losses from flooding.
- Increased access to clean water for women and children who are disproportionately affected by floods.
- Enhanced female participation in all aspects of FRM and establishing gender-responsive risk assessments and FRM frameworks.

### The project will be guided by a socio-economic risk model which will guide the identification and prioritization of risk management, planning, preparedness and response measures in order to ensure that the most vulnerable communities are protected.

#### The outputs of this project are:

1. Fully integrated multi-sectoral flood forecasting and EWS initiatives timely preparation and response.
2. Gender responsive policies and plans and effectively contribute to protection of people and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risk.
3. The implementation of a set of integrated flood risk management measures supported by gender responsive policies and plans which will be achieved through a set of four interconnected and sequenced activities required to deliver a

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## B

functionally impaired impact-based FFWEs. First, The project will significantly improve the hydrometeorological network which was damaged during the 1992-1995 war and currently cannot provide adequate spatial and temporal resolution of hydrological variables. This will be achieved by implementing a new generation of hydrometeorological monitoring networks to cover a greater range of hydrologic variables that climate change is expected to impact, including rainfall, snowmelt, runoff, river flow, water levels, sediment transport, flash floods, fluvial and pluvial flooding, groundwater flooding, floods from dam operations, and torrential floods (Activity 1.1). Further, the project will strengthen the capacity of institutions at all levels to identify climate change risks and vulnerability by enhancing technical capacities related to hazard and risk identification, assessment, modeling and monitoring, as well as decision-making and planning for climate-resilient flood risk management through a hazard undertake model making for forests, include HPPs and groundwater modeling in flood hazard, risk and vulnerability assessments (Activity 1.2). To address the lack of a country-wide flood forecasting and early warning system and lack of emergency response capacity to respond to extreme events, the project will implement a countrywide FFWEs based on an impact-based forecasting approach and following the WMO standards for flood warnings and early warning systems (Activity 1.3). The project will also strengthen the implementation of EWTs for the highest risk communities (Activity 1.3). Finally, the project will strengthen climate risk information sharing through the development of a Climate Risk Information Sharing Platform (CRISP) with appropriate Communication Protocols and Codes of Conduct to be developed for each of the institutions responsible for impact-based flood forecasting and early warning systems (Activity 1.4).

58. The project’s Outcome 2 will be achieved through the Outputs 2 and 3 which together will enhance the capacity to identify, plan and implement long-term flood risk management strategies at the basin and sub-basin scale by implementing climate-resilient land use planning and integrated water resources management (Activity 2.1).

59. More specifically, the Output 2 will mainstream climate informed integrated flood risk management into key sectoral policies and plans, enhance institutional technical capacities in all aspects of FIRM, and facilitate scaled up implementation of climate-resilient land use planning and integrated water resources management through decision support tools, maps and assessments resulting from Output 1 as the basis. Through the application of nature-based solutions (NBS) to address flood risk at the basin scale, the project will enhance the capacity for sustainable land use and restoration of ecological flood protection functions. This output will be delivered through four complementary activities: Activity 2.1 will mainstream climate-informed integrated flood risk management into key sectoral policies and plans; Activity 2.2 will implement nature-based solutions to address flood risk at basin scale, including hydropower, critical infrastructure and spatial planning (Activity 2.1). Then, the project will promote and upscale non-structural solutions for flood risk reduction in high-risk basins through the implementation of integrated soil conservation and sediment management measures (Activity 2.2). These solutions will complement structural measures to address climate change impacts on flood risk by mainstreaming climate-resilient land use planning into sectoral policies, plans and decision-making processes as well as promoting a solution to climate-resilient food management and protection. In addition, mainstreaming climate induced flood risk considerations in the design of new infrastructure projects will be promoted through the development of guidelines for impacts of climate change and promote reforestation, afforestation, and sustainable forest use. The long term project goal is to reduce flood risk by 20% at basin scale and improve water quality and ecosystem services and other biological functions. The project will thereafter result in improvements in watershed ecosystems and restoration of ecological flood protection functions through the implementation of nature-based solutions (NBS) for climate change adaptation, as well as improved livelihoods and food security due to reduced effects of catchment degradation and enhanced livelihoods of rural communities. To fully embed these new (SA) practices into sectoral policies and plans, the project will mainstream climate-informed integrated flood risk management in key sectoral policies and plans and protocols (Activity 2.3). Similarly, the project will work with institutions to enhance institutional capacity and embed combined practices developed through the project for flood risk management. The project will also conduct country wide training programs on best practices in flood risk management, including climate-informed integrated flood risk management, emergency responders based on the new SA practices (Activity 2.4). Implementation of the Output 2 will address the gaps in FIRM sectoral policy in BAH by, the lack of a strategic framework for mainstreaming climate change into land use and water resources planning and capacity and knowledge on system-based and non-structural solutions for climate resilient flood risk reduction.

60. Finally, Output 3 will support the enhanced long-term flood risk investment planning at the national and local levels, engage private sector in risk management, and implement priority climate-proof structural flood protection

## B

### GOAL STATEMENT

Without the management of GB's traditional livestock & crop systems, integrated disease informed research supported by gender-disaggregated climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies will not be possible. This project will support the development of a national strategy for the sustainable management of GB's livestock and crops in order to reduce GHG emissions and will also contribute to the achievement of goals, commitments and frameworks related to the reduction of the country’s climate impact through the implementation of integrated disease informed research supported by gender-disaggregated climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies.

### OUTCOMES

Outcome 1: Fully integrated disease informed research supported by gender-disaggregated climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies will be developed and implemented in GB.

Outcome 2: Non-traditional, broad-spectrum livestock and crop systems will be established to reduce GHG emissions and increase resilience of smallholder farmers and their families.

### PROJECT ACTIVITIES

Activity 1.1: Development of a national strategy for the sustainable management of GB's livestock and crops in order to reduce GHG emissions.

Activity 1.2: Implementation of integrated disease informed research supported by gender-disaggregated climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies.

#### 71. WBFIP project developed EFUSD flood hazard risk maps for all basins in BAH. These maps were completed using a basin approach and only cover the main river floods and not the smaller tributaries or torrential floods. The maps pose a significant risk to populations, particularly where there is torrential flash flooding, but there is currently no funding available from any source to develop flood risk management plans for all basins in BAH that include torrential floods. In addition, the IPAD15 has allocated funds for development of flood risk management plans for all basins in BAH that include torrential floods. However, the current EFUSD flood hazard and risk mapping requires the EUFIP with respect to flood hazard and risk mapping for BAH. It will not fully enable the strategic assessment and decision-making for climate-induced flood management at basin level. The WBFIP has developed a framework which is a government priority to support and guide local municipalities to wisely and rationally manage risk exposure.

The project will enhance the WBFIP EFUSD hazard and risk modeling and map by (a) extending the WBFIP models to ensure whole basins approach and not just modeling of high risk areas; b) modeling Category II tributaries and not only main river floods, including torrential torrents flooding mechanisms; c) extracting hydrological modeling; and d)

### Introduction to flood operations and flooding from potential dam failure: The modeling will include future climate change projections. An essential task of flood management in HPP modified catchments is determination of an effective operating regime for the hydropower plant that balances the need for reliable power generation, safety within reasonable limits and optimizing water availability for power flow. To minimize disastrous impact due to flooding from potential dam failure, it will be necessary to develop a comprehensive framework for flood risk management which includes adequate representation of HPP operations in the basin hazard and risk modeling (to strengthen the basis for decision making) as well as development of an operational regime that can be implemented during a developing flood. To this end, the project will incorporate HPP operations into flood hazard and risk modeling (to strengthen the basis for decision making) as well as develop an operational regime that can be implemented during a developing flood.

The project will also develop the institutional capacity of HPP operators to use climate risk information produced by the project in their day-to-day operations. The project will focus on the rivers and Tributaries sub-basins of the Afric Basin where there are large numbers of HPPs.

### 3.1.2. Project Activities

The project will also develop the institutional capacity of HPP operators to use climate risk information and strengthen their ability to manage flood risks in a manner that is consistent with the needs of VPP-based operations. In addition, the ability to assess flood risks posed by dams and reservoirs in the case of overtopping or breaching will be improved through development of an enhanced hazard model for all main basins (category I rivers), incorporating HPP operations at relevant basins.

This activity will also build on the baseline GIS-based socio-economic flood risk and vulnerability model, which has been developed as part of the project. The model will be used to develop a future climate change scenario for VPP-based operations. The developed socio-economic flood risk model will be enhanced with improved socioeconomic and hydrological datasets that are important to flood risk and vulnerability modeling and assessment.

# Article 17: Develop an Integrated Impact-based Consolidated and Community-Based Flood Forecasting and Early Warning System (FEWS)

(BSC Financing - €2,156,040; GCF Financing - €1,938,438)

## Background

Bajir is a member of WMO and implements all of its activities in line with WMO standards and guidelines. Forecasting and early warning within Bajir currently includes the key principles of GFCS: Hydro Meteorological Information System (HMIS), Water Resources Management Information System (WRMIS) and Climate Change Perspective, tailoring and delivering science-based climate predictions and services for sectors like agriculture; and flood management. The project will build on the experience gained from the implementation of the integrated water information system (server and cloud computing) and forecasting platform server which, in addition to cloud infrastructure, has been developed under the UNDP/UNICEF project. A number of other platforms have also been developed under UNDP’s Vitros project. The platform will be built on the FEWS which was developed for the Vitros and Una Sara River Basins and will enhance the ICT systems to support an integrated flood forecasting system (FFS) in Bajir. The FFS will cover the entire basin of the Bajir River, while the nation-wide impact-based FEWS will cover the two high-energy inter-basin watersheds of Nencha and Tetepeyra, and the three low-energy inter-basin watersheds of Mba, Kafu and Lomami. The FFS will be based on the hydrological forecasting system for Sara River Basin (Phase I: Baina Basin project) implementing the Vitros platform. The FEWS will be based on the hydro-meteorological information system (HMIS) and climate change perspective. The forecasting system to be implemented will update and integrate all existing and new FEWS models covering the entire basin of the Bajir River, while the FEWS will cover the entire basin of the Bajir River and implement and integrated with the upgraded forecasting platform based on two different Numerical Weather Predictions (NWP) models. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with WMO regional technical centers and implemented within the upgraded forecasting platform based on two different Numerical Weather Predictions (NWP) models. The project is supported by the World Bank, GCF, UNDP, and other donors. The project will also respectively the WRF and the COSMO local implementations.

## Objectives

The project aims to support the development of a flood forecasting system for Bajir River Basin and its application and annual fee, cost of data and access to cloud computing for the project duration (Ministry of Water Management) will pay through the service co-financing letter - Annex XIII (Sub-activity 1.3.1 pgs 2021 and 2051). The project will be implemented in close collaboration with WMO regional technical centers, UNESDOC ICT4G and other sector partners. The project will also support the development of a socio-economic risk model for Bajir River Basin which will be done through partnerships with WMO regional training centers, UNESCO ICT4G and other sector partners. The socio-economic risk model will provide information about the actual impact that each event is going to cause in particular locations, and for particular sectors (e.g., agriculture, livestock, fisheries). It will also provide information on the potential economic and social impacts of flooding events, including damage and loss of infrastructure and livelihoods, likely damage and loss of critical infrastructure and failure of protective hazard defences. The components of the socio-economic risk model will be developed in close collaboration with local communities.

## Components

The project will support the development of a flood forecasting system for Bajir River Basin and its application and annual fee, cost of data and access to cloud computing for the project duration (Ministry of Water Management) will pay through the service co-financing letter - Annex XIII (Sub-activity 1.3.1 pgs 2021 and 2051). The project will be implemented in close collaboration with WMO regional technical centers, UNESDOC ICT4G and other sector partners. The project will also support the development of a socio-economic risk model for Bajir River Basin which will be done through partnerships with WMO regional training centers, UNESCO ICT4G and other sector partners. The socio-economic risk model will provide information about the actual impact that each event is going to cause in particular locations, and for particular sectors (e.g., agriculture, livestock, fisheries). It will also provide information on the potential economic and social impacts of flooding events, including damage and loss of infrastructure and livelihoods, likely damage and loss of critical infrastructure and failure of protective hazard defences. The components of the socio-economic risk model will be developed in close collaboration with local communities.

## Implementation

The project will be implemented based on the following considerations: relative risk and vulnerability, lead time of the extreme event, potential technical constraints for the central system to effectively service the community (e.g., due to remote location), and potential technical constraints for the local system to effectively service the community. The project will be coordinated at the level of municipalities. The components of the FEWS is described in Section 10.1 (Pgs. 202 to 205) of the GCF FS. The project will also develop sector specific FEWS products for the agriculture and hydro power sectors.

## Conclusion

The socio-economic risk model activity will examine opportunities to enhance and support the capacities of the agricultural, livestock, fisheries and hydropower sectors in Bajir River Basin to better access and use forecast information and advice to farmers. Through this activity the project will implement ICT-based innovations in the communication of forecasts and advisories, improve the use of historical data and derivatives, improve models

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

The project will engage the hydropower sector in the flood risk management of the basins in which they operate through agreement on operations of their systems during flood events. This agreement will be between hydropower operators and other stakeholders, including government agencies, water users, agricultural businesses, agribusinesses, farmers' groups and other clients to deliver timely forecasts and advisories to farmers who will be affected by meteorological hazards.

The project will also develop a last-mile communication system (Sub-activity 2.3.2, P153/2 of FIS), which includes the design of last-mile communication models, working with the government to update the communication protocol for flood risk management and water resources management, and developing a mobile application that will provide real-time weather forecasts and warning information that will feed into HPP operations. In parallel, the project will provide support to the hydropower sector in the development of an early warning system (EWS) for hydropower operators and other stakeholders. This EWS has been carried out during the project design, and co-financing leveraged from the hydropower component of the project will be used to develop this EWS.

The project will also support the development of a flood risk management strategy that will address how farmers can be affected by meteorological events and hence climate change, on both the demand and supply side. Thus, the project will provide technical assistance in the design of such strategies for hydropower operators and other stakeholders to develop operations and will develop enhanced tools and systems that provide decision-makers with the ability to analyze and develop flood risk management strategies.

The project will also support the development of a mobile application that will provide real-time weather forecasts, products and services which will help improve efficiency and reduce risk associated with hydro-meteorological hazards affecting hydropower operations.

UNDP/SBA established a cooperation with the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC) in order to introduce Forecast based Financing (FbF). Based on jointly developed selection criteria, FbF was presented as an innovative approach for disaster risk management. The project will support the enhancement of DRR activities within the municipal plans. For the first time in BAH the FbF approach was presented and discussed with stakeholders from different sectors. The project will also provide technical assistance to obtain data from hydrological stations to define thresholds for action and danger levels necessary for triggering FbF mechanisms.

The conclusion of municipal representatives was that implementation of FbF would be a very useful tool in disaster risk management, but they highlighted the need for further efforts are needed in order to ensure reliable and timely forecast information, define triggers for action and perform risk analysis. The project will support the development of a mobile application which will provide real-time weather forecasts and FbF mechanism is triggered.

The project will also support the development of a mobile application that will provide real-time weather forecasts and warning information that will feed into HPP operations in parallel, to identify the thresholds and impacts of flood events. These thresholds will include flood levels under which water will be released from dams, flood levels at which evacuation orders are issued, and other forecast thresholds that will trigger the release of resources for early action will be developed as part of the development of the EWS.

The project will also support the development of a mobile application which will provide real-time weather forecasts and FbF mechanism is triggered. The project will leverage existing relationships with Red Cross Society of Bosnia and Herzegovina (RCS BH) to develop an early warning system for hydropower operators, including action based on in-depth forecast information and risk analysis.

The project will also support the development of a mobile application which will provide real-time weather forecasts and FbF mechanism is triggered. The project will leverage existing relationships with Red Cross Society of Bosnia and Herzegovina (RCS BH) to develop an early warning system for hydropower operators, including action based on in-depth forecast information and risk analysis.

To consolidate and sustain the significant technical capacity development that will be achieved under this activity, the project will develop and implement a capacity development plan for embedding flood hazard and risk modelling approaches in BAH. The plan should include training needs assessment, prevention, risk reduction, transfer, preparedness, climate risk management and climate change adaptation strategies. A revised institutional building plan will be developed under the project to ensure that the required human resources are available within the project period. This will also define the institutional arrangements for FFWEs by implementing the recommendations from the VFM project which identified a multi-stakeholder functional arrangement for an integrated, people-centred impact-based FFWEs (all of BAH) (See Section 6\.5 of FIS).

83\. Gender Mainstreaming Approach: The Project will secure equal participation and active role of women and men, including vulnerable groups on all levels of the project. To ensure that the project addresses the specific vulnerabilities of gender and socially excluded groups, the Project will secure at least 40% participation to identify and address their needs and concerns in planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation and reporting. This will be achieved through:

* **Participation and Representation:** Involve women, men, and diverse social groups in the design and decision-making processes of the project. Ensure that women and men have equal access to information, resources, and inclusion in planning, implementation, and evaluation stages. This can be done through engaging local communities, including women’s groups, youth groups, and other marginalized groups.
* **Gender-Responsive Communication:** Design systems that use gender-responsive and culturally appropriate communication strategies. Use a variety of media (e.g., radio, television, print materials) in multiple languages, channels, and formats to reach diverse groups. Tailor the messages to specific needs and preferences of women and men.

## B

### Output2: Non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions mainstreamed in sectoral policies and plans and effectively contribute to protection of people and livelihoods from climate-induced flood risks

#### Activity 2.1: Mainstream climate induced flood risk reduction into sectoral planning (agriculture, hydropower, forestry, environment), critical infrastructure and spatial planning (Co-Financing = $396,000; Co-Financing = $50,000)

The project will enhance the existing legislative and regulatory FFRM framework by mainstreaming climate-based flood risk management into sectoral planning for agriculture, forestry, environment, hydropower, critical infrastructure and spatial planning. At the state level, a cross-sector approach will be taken to integrate flood risk reduction measures in sectoral plans. The project will also support the development of an intersectoral agreement between two entities. This requires the involvement of key stakeholders, the improvement of coordination, and the implementation of joint actions. The intersectoral agreement will be reviewed every five years. The FFRM framework will be reviewed and adjusted to account for the impacts of climate change induced flood risk. The project will help the entity to mainstream climate-based flood risk reduction into sectoral planning by developing a strategy on climate- based flood risk impact, vulnerability and adaptation assessments, analyses of the costs and benefits of flood risk management measures.

### In the Hydropower sector the project will support the development of climate risk informed sector strategies, policies and plans for the flood safety of dams and will examine the specific role of the existing and planned dams in flood risk reduction. The project will also develop a Climate Change Adaptation Plan (CCAP) for BAFD dams.

This will include development or enhancement of guidelines for the risk-based categorization of dams, dam performance monitoring, and climate change adaptation strategies. The CCAP will address the risks associated with dams, including risk of overtopping, exposure to landslides and sediment inundation, and the potential impacts on downstream communities. The project will also support the development of a framework to develop dam operating rules that ensure sufficient flood storage is provided at the start of large events, to protect communities from flooding in downstream areas. This will be achieved through the involvement of dam owners and operators in the development and eventual implementation of the overall Flood Risk Management Plan (FRMP) for BAFD dams. The CCAP will also include a strategy on climate change adaptation and flood alleviation. The policies and strategies will ensure that dams are maintained and operated in a manner which is consistent with the changing conditions of flooding where possible, and which takes account of the increasing risks they pose due to climate change.

### With respect to the agricultural sector the project will ensure that climate change is taken into account in the design and development of flood risk management measures. The project will also include the consideration of risks and opportunities for food management in the design and development of flood risk management measures. This will be achieved through a series of workshops which integrate climate risk and adaptation priorities into the agriculture sector plans, investments and budget frameworks. These workshops will also provide an opportunity to review existing flood risk management in the sector model; trade off analysis and cost-benefit assessments for a range of plausible adaptation options in agricultural systems; and identify opportunities for the development of new tools and technologies which will catalyse adoption of proposed FFRM agriculture measures and will consider a range of different instruments for either direct or indirect support to farmers. The project will also develop a strategy on climate change for the agricultural sector on flood risk assessment and management and the use of climate change induced flood risk information.

### In relation to the forestry sector, the project will develop catchment management strategies for climate resilient catchment management to reduce the exposure of communities and their physical assets to climate-induced flood risks. The project will also support the development of a strategy on climate change (climate change adaptation management) to develop and mainstream basin afforestation/reforestation and agro-forestry strategies and other forestry strategies to enhance flood risk management site of forests, as well as flood risk adaptation priorities into

## Section B

### In the area of land use and spatial planning:

The project will scale up and embed nationwide flood risk zoning policy based on soil and hazard maps (to be produced under Activity 1.2) and develop the relevant guidelines, standards, and tools for implementation.

### With regard to the critical infrastructure project:

The project will review climate proofing design standards, methodologies, procedures and practice and will introduce climate risk screening methods and embed climate risk reduction criteria into the planning process of all major projects (e.g., urban development plans, water supply and sanitation facilities, protective dikes, dams, highways, and railway bridges). The intervention will embed the systematic use of climate risk information in the design and construction phase of critical infrastructure projects. At the municipal level, the project will also introduce climate risk criteria into the prioritization process and include them as a criterion for investment decisions.

### Gender mainstreaming activities:

The Project will ensure participation of both men and women but also recognize that women are disproportionately affected by climate change and have limited access to resources. The Project will promote gender equality in flood risk reduction into sectorial planning. Conduct gender mainstreaming training and build capacity of national stakeholders (including local authorities) on gender-sensitive approaches.
