# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 36

## B

The combination of both structural and ecosystem-based measures provides the best solution to climate-resilient flood management and protection. Non-structural measures (See Annex A of FS) will include floodplain restoration, riverbank stabilization, and sediment reduction in the mainstem of the basin by creating a meandering straightened rivers, land and soil management activities to retain / delay surface flows, woody debris dams, and other natural flow regulation structures. The structural measures are designed to reduce the volume of water from a ditch network to promote infiltration (swales, interception ditches, etc.), in-channel vegetation management growth to maximize sediment augmentation.

Under this activity the project will implement 21 non-structural measures which were identified out of 69 structural and non-structural measures proposed by WAA in the course of feasibility analysis. (See FS Annex A for list of measures). The project will also implement 30 structural measures, including 17 measures that are non-structural measures will be funded by GCF resources, and 13 measures will be co-funded by the Government of Bhutan. Cost benefit ratio is used as a criterion to select the measures in the feasibility analysis stage for each sub-basin area of the proposal and projects were ranked on the basis of the benefit to cost ratio. The two highest priority measures are those that have been implemented or are being implemented.

A summary table of non-structural measures to be implemented by the project is provided in Annex A of FS.

The project will include community based EASAs as part of the solution, which will be particularly important to reduce the impact of torrential floods. The project will scale up successful EASA approaches piloted by the USDPC and other agencies in Bhutan. These measures are designed to prevent soil erosion and sedimentation in the Bhashi basin which is only based in BASH with already developed torrential flood hazard maps and prioritized and covered under this proposal. The main source of sedimentation is a network of forest roads and paths for hauling logs. During the wet, roads were often washed away by floods and during dry season, they are eroded by wind. The project will expand the road to restrict timber flow in low as there are, as well as entry legislations which prohibit cutting out such path (Patches). In addition, the project will also implement a series of measures that will be implemented along the road, most of the excavation material is simply pushed from the route down the slope most of which reaches the main river. The project will also implement a series of measures to prevent soil erosion and sedimentation in this area.

This area is exposed to extensive erosion and creates torrential floods which will produce significant amounts of sediments that are deposited in the Bhashi basin, reducing its capacity and increasing the flood peak concentration time. It is necessary to take measures to prevent erosion on forest roads and paths and to rehabilitate already eroded areas. The project will implement a series of structural and non-structural measures as described above.

The following table provides an overview of the measures that are proposed for implementation. These measures will be implemented 3 patches, 16 wider banks, afforestation of the basin in the amount of 200 ha and gravel pits. The project will also implement a series of structural and non-structural measures as described above.

All elements but outline design drawings are provided in Annex A of FS. Detailed documentation to be developed for all elements but outline design drawings are provided in Annex A of FS.

## Subject 3: Climate-proof flood protection measures scaled-up using new and improved national and local financing mechanisms

### Activity 3.1 Develop a country-specific business case for scaling up flood protection measures and identify potential sources of finance for sector private sector engagement in climate risk financing (GCF Financing $460,440; Co-Financing $805,174)

## Climate-proof flood protection measures scaled-up using new and improved national and local financing mechanisms

### Activity 3.2: Integrate the climate proofing of existing B&H in RIM is currently largely undertaken by the public sector or support from the international donor community and lacks the financing mechanisms and tools that would enable private sector engagement in flood risk management and climate risk financing. The project will develop a business case for scaling up flood protection measures, including the development of new financing mechanisms to provide financial resilience building measures. Willingness to pay (WTP) surveys undertaken during feasibility study found that there is significant demand from the public and private sectors for flood risk reduction products that contribute to risk financing and risk transfer, to ensure the effective long-term management of flood risk and risk reduction. The project will develop a business case for scaling up flood protection measures in B&H with an interest in sector-specific climate risk information products, and their willingness to pay for tailored products that will be developed as part of the project. A market analysis has been conducted on the demand for flood risk monitoring and early warning systems, equipment and information products has been assessed and determined that there is a significant demand from public institutions, businesses and agriculture sectors for flood risk management products. The project will develop a business case for scaling up flood protection measures in B&H with an interest in sector-specific climate risk information products, and their willingness to pay for tailored products that will be developed as part of the project. A market analysis has been conducted on the demand for flood risk monitoring and early warning systems, equipment and information products has been assessed and determined that there is a significant demand from public institutions, businesses and agriculture sectors for flood risk management products.

### Activity 3.3: The project will develop the Investment framework for climate induced floods risk reduction and management including the development of new financing mechanisms to enable private sector engagement in climate risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms. No GCF process will be used to provide this service.

* The development of new financing mechanisms for flood risk management;
* Identification and development of risk financing and transfer mechanisms products and tools, based on detailed socio-economic risk, damage and vulnerability assessment.
* Development of natural disasters' insurance products for public institutions, businesses and agriculture
* Transfer of the developed products to the private sector. This will include a market analysis for the development of lending products to mainstream the use of climate-risk information into their lending

## B

### 114. Regarding public sector risk financing, the project will build upon municipal level climate risk-informed budgeting and financial planning (FPM) frameworks and examine opportunities to introduce climate-risk informed budgeting by the BFGR existing public financial management (PFM) framework leveraging on existing strengths and addressing gaps in the current PFM framework. The project will also develop a pilot program for flood risk management. This will include using the damage and loss risk and modeling tools (Sub-Activity 1.2) to provide an initial assessment of the potential benefits of integrating climate risk into municipal level FPM, the FRM framework and the investment request. FRM/data budget ring-fencing mechanisms such as integrated budgeting and financial planning (FPM) will be used to ensure that the project is aligned with the benefit analysis and data to demonstrate the advantages (and higher VFM) of greater spending on FPM, preparedness and risk management. The project will also develop a framework for evaluating key program performance through monitoring to enable performance-based budgeting, integrating FPM performance objectives and evaluation criteria into the annual budget process and the annual performance review (Sub-Activity 1.3 of the Spending Review process). Such evaluations of key programmes will generate key evidence which should feed back into the budget allocation process.

### 115. The project will develop and implement a programme of engagement of relevant state institutions, insurers and financial intermediaries in capital market instruments, forming key public-private sector partnerships to leverage and scale up investments. The project is also making interventions at the legislative and governance levels to ensure that the mobilized resources are used effectively for flood risk management. This includes within the existing markets, which will ensure long-term sustainability of the mobilized investments, with the objective of ensuring that the market-based solutions are sustainable in the long term. It will engage financial institutions, including micro-financing institutions, for the development of lending products and to enable them to meet the needs of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in flood-prone areas. The project will also identify and minimize risks and provide loans to climate responsive/resilient enterprises in BIMR (i.e., loans that offer better conditions than those offered by commercial banks or other financial institutions, including microfinance credit criteria). These loans will service Small and Medium Enterprises and will provide loans for start-up or expansion of businesses.

Hence the project will leverage and enhance existing micro-finance capacity in BAH to extend to include more insurance. 117. By the end of the project the following will be achieved: public sector risk financing and building mechanisms developed, public-private sector partnerships established, and public-private sector climate risk financing mechanisms strengthened. 118. As part of the investment framework under this activity the project will also develop a methodology and a tool to support the identification of food security investments in the context of climate change. This will be done through FFRM planning and decision-making based on cost benefit analysis (CBA). The project will scale up the bespoke GIS-based tool for food security risk assessment, which is currently being used by the government for national socio-economic risk mapping, CBA and the identification and appraisal of climate resilient intervention measures. This tool will be extended to include an additional module to enable identification of food security investments in the context of climate change. The project will also develop a methodology and investment planning. Hence under this activity the project is developing innovative financing options across risk financing for risk reduction and management, micro-insurance (agriculture, natural disasters), micro-finance (micro-insurance, regional insurance pool, catastrophe bonds), forms key private public sector partnerships to leverage and scale up investments in climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. The project will also develop the institutional mechanisms to provide climate responsive financing options. This proposal therefore represents a good example of financial blending and innovative climate finance.

The project will also develop institutional mechanisms based on detailed gender sensitive assessment on socio-economic risk, damages, and losses.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 42

#### Activity 3.1: Develop tool for appraisal-led design for structural and non-structural FIRM measures, investment planning, climate risk financing mechanisms and for appraisal-led FIRM options design and decision-making, based on cost benefit analysis (CBA) approaches

**Strategies & Definitions:**

* **5 strategies and plans for long-term ongoing investment frameworks that would be required to address flood risk to each sector:** 
    * Source management and dispersion mitigation as part of the information platform to be developed under 1.4.3.
    * 2 tools on flood and risk transfer strategy for private sector engagement in risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms for entity-level flood risk financing and resilience strategy.
    * 3 natural disaster products developed.
    * 7 feasible disaster insurance programs, regional disaster risk insurance program and catastrophe bonds.
    * 4 flood specific awareness campaigns.
* **2 tools for appraisal-led design for structural and non-structural FIRM measures based on cost benefit analysis (CBA) approach:**

#### Activity 3.2: Formulate multi-year climate resilient municipal investment plans and gender responsive community preparedness plans in selected municipalities and one canton in Vitoria, Ulaña-Sanu, Bizkaia, Durra, Neretva and Trebližnica basins.

**122. The project will develop municipal multi-year investment plans for climate resilience FIRM planning for 10-12 highest risk communities and one canton. It will develop new approaches to investment planning to include annual investment plans that are aligned with the municipality’s budget cycle, including capital expenditure and maintenance costs (e.g., a community-based scheme that involves the use of tariffs or in-kind contributions to establish an infrastructure fund for climate resilience projects), as well as innovative financing mechanisms (e.g. blended financing); event-based financing of what will be required to respond to disaster events (RF) and development of a multi-year investment plan for each municipality, including the identification of resources such as CBA, and CFA. For the RF, the investment plans will define the financial resources that will need to be made available in advance to respond to these events. The project will also develop an early action plan (EAP) that will identify those resources for the implementation of early actions. The Early Action Plan (EAP) will be part of the preparation and development of the multi-year investment plans, which will include a detailed description of the early actions. The project will work with the IFCD to develop the FIRF components of the municipal investment plans and the EAPs.**

**123. The project will support embedding the use of municipal investment plans in the technical justification for budget allocation for FIRM activities, identifying and prioritizing flood risk financing, based on the principles of portfolio risk assessment and cost-benefit analysis (CBA). The multi-year investment plan will be embedded in municipality as a standardized requirement for developing annual infrastructure investment plans at the community level. The project will also support the development of gender responsive municipal jurisdiction, these infrastructure investment plans will include detail design for torrents management.**

#### Activity 124: Using the example of Community Emergency Flood Preparedness plans (CEFP) the project will also support the development of community preparedness plans for the most vulnerable 10-12 communities in target basins (excluding Vitoria, Ulaña-Sanu, Bizkaia, Durra, Neretva and Trebližnica basins), as well as the National Office of Protection and Rescue in RS and BiH including cantons. According to the Law, the CEFP will consist of the following components: Risk assessment plan, risk assessment report plan, Preparedness plan, Mobilization plan, Emergency plan, Evacuation plan.

#### Gender mainstreaming Activities:
* **Both men and women are adequately represented in municipal investment planning processes:** Women’s participation is ensured through gender-responsive community preparedness plans in selected municipalities; Needs of women and other vulnerable (elderly, disabled, children, Roma) groups are incorporated in developed plans.

126. The following sub-activities will be implemented:

* **Sub-Activity 3.2.1:** Develop municipal investment plans for climate resilient FIRM planning for 10–12 highest risk communities and 1 canton.
* **Sub-Activity 3.2.2:** Develop preparedness plans for 10–12 highest risk communities and 1 canton.

### Activity 3.2 Deliverables:
* 2 financing models for investment maintenance costs;
* Standardized CBA tools embedded in municipality for developing annual infrastructure investment plans;
* 10 climate-resilient FIRM plans developed with cost-benefit analysis based on CBA methods and models;
* Multi-sectoral flood risk management plan and multi-hazard responsive community preparedness plan implemented in the highest risk municipalities (10–12) in Vrbas, Una-Sana and Bosna basins.
* Comprehensive flood risk assessment of 10 communities using a standardized methodology and cost of CEP in EUR as defined by Life of Protection and Rescue in RS and FBaM.

### Activity 3.3: Implement climate-proof structural flood risk reduction and anti-erosion interventions in Vrbas, Una-Sana, Bosna and Drina River basins.
* (GCF Financing = €615,280; GCF Financing = €29,335,153)

### 127. This activity will focus on the implementation of priority structural flood risk reduction interventions for target basins at highest risk. The structural measures will be co-financed through EIB loans and Water Agency (BiH) grants. These measures are based on new climate information and design methods. The project will embed climate change responsive design of flood defences and anti-erosion structures in the planning, implementation and monitoring phases of these interventions and their implementation. It will utilize flood hazard risk modeling and will embed CBA and appraisal-based prioritization for structural measures to ensure that they are cost-effective and provide maximum benefit for climate change and which consider environmental impacts. 10 structural measures have been identified by Water Agency (BiH) as part of the project design phase, with a focus on the Una-Sana River basin and the high-risk river basin, which have been prioritized during the project design phase as detailed in Section 2 of this feasibility study. The priority structural flood risk reduction interventions will be implemented in the target sites set out in Vrbas, Una-Sana and Bosna, Neretva and Trebišnjica and Drina River basins.

### General Project Objectives:
The project aims to reduce the vulnerability of communities (urbanized, ruralized, and isolated) in Bosnia and Herzegovina to climate change impacts. The Project will ensure that specific ways in which flood risk and erosion impact women and men are analyzed and addressed through gender-sensitive approaches. This will be achieved by engaging with local stakeholders and GCF funds; to develop climate-proof design products based on new climate risk information and design methods. This will also provide opportunities for economic development from the work, employment and income generating opportunities associated with implementing these measures.

### 129. The following sub-activities will be implemented:

* **Sub-Activity 3.3.1:** Develop a plan of climate-resilient flood protection structural measures;
* **Sub-Activity 3.3.2:** Implement new flood defences and the rehabilitation and upgrade of existing flood defences with climate change.

### Activity 3.3 Deliverables:
* Detailed design of 10 climate-proofed structural measures implemented;
* 10 EIB-funded climate-proofed structural measures implemented;

## COVID-19 Project co-benefits and opportunities

### 130. The current COVID-19 global pandemic has highlighted the need for a more holistic approach to hazard identification and risk management when faced with systemic risks which are complex, interlinked and non-linear. Assessing these risks requires an understanding of how they interact across sectors and their governance mechanisms, and apply a multisectoral and interdisciplinary approach as these mechanisms

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 44

## Output/Activity: COVID-19 co-benefits/opportunities

### Output 1: Climate-informed impact-based FWEWS and an increased generation and use of climate data to reduce the impacts of climate-related hazards

#### Activity 1.1: Upgrade and expand the existing network of hydrometric stations for enhanced monitoring of streamflow in Category I catchments and torrential streams

The expansion of the hydrometric observation network will underlie the development of a flood risk model that will be used to identify areas of high occurrence of flood which will enable site to take early measures to reduce the impacts of flooding. The project will also provide water, food, and medicine, thus enabling health and sanitation services.

#### Activity 1.2: Enhance climate-induced food hazard risk and vulnerability information for improved disaster management and sound decision making for climate induced food management

The ongoing COVID pandemic has increased the vulnerability of communities in Bahr. Under this activity, the project is undertaking baseline surveys to identify the most vulnerable households based on socio-economic factors derived from socio-economic secondary data. The project will also undertake a risk assessment exercise under this activity.

#### Activity 1.3: Develop an integrated impact-based centralized and community-based flood forecasting and early warning system (FWEWS)

Proper response to flooding would reduce the spread COVID-19 and reduce the economic losses associated with climate-related hazards, human losses and economic damage. Response activities that will be undertaken under this activity include:

* **Community-based Early Warning System:** This is very important when developing the Community-based EWS. Community members are key actors in the development of FWEWS. Their hardworking and social distancing requires full engagement of community members to ensure their participation.

This activity will also improve notification of flood-risk-impaired areas so that emergency services, including frontline workers dealing with COVID-19, can be better prepared to respond to people who are caught by floods or take routes that are likely already to be flooded). Overall, the project will enhance response to emergencies which include COVID outbreaks.

### Activity 4.2: Develop and implement protocols and SOFs for flood risk communication and effective impact based FFEMS and flood risk management

Under this activity, the project is developing the data sharing platforms (including a web-based platform) that will be used by communities and community climate risk information sharing and dissemination. This will also include the development of standard operating procedures (SOPs) for flood risk communication critical to avoid rumors and misinformation. The SOPs will cover all aspects of flood risk communication, including how to effectively communicate with vulnerable groups who are likely combined impacts of floods and the likely additional COVID-19 risks.

### Output 2: Sub-sectoral flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions mainstreamed in sectorial policies and plans and effectively contribute to climate resilience

#### Activity 2.1: Mainstream climate induced flood-risk reduction into sectorial planning (agriculture, hydropower, transport, energy, critical infrastructure and spatial planning)

A major aim of this activity is to strengthen cross-sectoral collaboration and use of climate risk information in sectoral planning. With BBR’s support, the project will develop a framework for mainstreaming flood risk reduction at the level of cooperation between agencies and thereby reduce “gaps” in flood risk management. This will be achieved by strengthening existing structures and developing new ones to ensure that there is greater cooperation and develop greater transparency among agencies.

#### Activity 2.2: Implement and maintain new environmental flood risk reduction and climate change adaptation methods

Protection from flood risk will enhance protection of drinking water sources, irrigation systems, hydropower generation facilities, and adequate hygienic conditions, which will help reduce the chance of disease outbreaks. The project will also develop a framework for structures that build resilience so that economic stress of the likely COVID-19 outbreak can be better managed.

#### Activity 2.3: Costly and mainstream EIA outcomes into policies and regulations and promote non-invasive alternatives during decision makers and communities

The major work expected to be delivered in a COVID safe manner, such that workers do not need to be in close proximity to each other or the public, will include the development of guidelines that can be deployed such as contact is limited. Any safety briefings, training and awareness raising activities will also be conducted in a safe manner (e.g., either adopting social distancing or utilizing electronic means).

#### Activity 2.4: Develop and strengthen institutional capacity and develop long term institutional capacity development plans for climate resilient FFM

Trainings and knowledge transfers to be conducted by the project will include training/seminars on epidemic recommendations, while ensuring that all participants are following COVID-19 safety guidelines such as masks and gloves in well ventilated spaces. Training and awareness raising activities will also be conducted through electronic means or via video conferencing. The project will also provide material support for handwashing in high risk areas.

### Output 3: Climate-proof flood protection measures scaled-up through new and improved national and local investment frameworks increasing resilience

The investment framework which will be based on risk modeling, will include a detailed analysis of the vulnerability to climate change and risk modeling will be based. Identify the new emerging vulnerabilities and opportunities will be sought to widen the type and number of Private and public sector players who could participate in FIRM projects. ### Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 14.4 The total annual budget for climate adaptation and resilience activities in B&M related to flood risk management averages US$43 Million, comprising mainly donor grants (40%) and loans (58%) while the damages due to flooding are estimated at US$67 million per year. This is a significant amount of money that needs to be spent on infrastructure, agriculture, and public buildings by mid-century due to climate change. The shortfall in expenditure on climate change adaptation is due to a current lack of financial capacity of the B&M Government to fund such activities.

#### 14.4 Both CP and RC in critical situations engage volunteers from local communities. They have updated lists with contacts of volunteers, regularly review the list, and update it as necessary. In addition they contract specialized volunteer groups (such as mountaineering, rafting clubs), specifically for protection and rescue.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 50

#### B

145. The proposed project represents a blended finance package that leverages international climate finance from GCF to fund risk management and resilience building in vulnerable communities in the Bahamas (BAM). This will be done through a $20 million grant component, which is 83% of the total project value. BAM public funds provided by the Entities and municipalities for structural and non-structural interventions will cover the remaining 17%. The GCF grant element is 95%, with an additional contribution from sector 7.7% of the total project budget. The GCF grant element has been critical to leverage and consolidate the overall financing package. BAM has committed to co-fund $20 million in public funds, which will be provided by Government of BAM and HPP partner is committed to cover $6M out of equipment and flood defenses installed within the framework of this project. The proposed project will also include a risk management component that will develop risk finance instruments and leverage partnerships with the private sector to secure inflow of private capital for climate resilience in BAM. This will be done through the Financial Risk Management Facility (FRMF) which is an innovative financing mechanism that has been developed by GCF to provide blended funding measures. During the development project, UNDP has conducted a series of willingness to pay studies which have shown that there are significant opportunities to leverage private sector capital for climate risk finance. FRMF provided an adequate enabling environment is established. Thus, the proposed project will catalyze a shift in BAM’s approach towards climate resilience and risk management by providing a framework to scale up climate financing strategies engaging private sector. GCF technical assistance is required to catalyze such transition.

146. GCF grant resources will provide for the technical assistance and capacity building to ensure that flood risk management activities are implemented effectively, efficiently, and equitably in BAM. Given the high vulnerability of people in BAM and reduces their susceptibility to climate-induced food risk that they have limited coping capacities to manage. The proposed project will also include a risk finance component which engages private sector; rather GCF will finance public goods and will assist the government of BAM to meet its obligations under the FRMF. This will be done through the Financial Risk Management Facility (FRMF) which is an innovative financing mechanism that has been developed by GCF to provide blended funding measures. Thus, GCF funding will cover additional costs to the Government associated with the increasing climate change impacts and the need for increased investment in flood risk management activities. The cost of managing food risk is increasing, and which is currently missing from the monitoring network. It will also cover the additional costs incurred by BAM to implement the project activities. These include capacity building and technical assistance risks and preparedness among vulnerable communities. These project activity are essential for improving climate resilience in BAM, including strengthening community resilience, and strengthening institutions with mandates to strategically manage climate risks.

147. There is currently limited participation of private sector in climate risk resilience building, and risk financing, despite the fact that there has been a significant increase in the number of private sector actors involved in the development of the mechanisms for risk sharing between public and private sector, including development of public-private partnerships (PPPs) to address climate risks. The proposed project will provide technical advice to financial institutions to mainstream climate change risks into their business models to manage the effects.

148. Through the FRMF project on Vibian basin and other past and on-going initiatives, BAM has made an important step toward piloting successful solutions for increased resilience to climate induced floods. However, single project interventions are not sufficient to address the scale of the problem. The proposed project will provide a national-scale win. Currently available funds, public/private or donor driven, are not sufficient to address flood risk in BAM. The proposed project will catalyze a shift in BAM’s approach towards climate resilience and risk management by enabling different types of measures that in synergy will effectively address flood risk in the main river basin.

### Financial Analysis recommendations and conclusions

Without the project and in the current situation government spend on FPR is less than the expected EAD and currently there is low to no private sector involvement. The proposed project will provide a national-scale win. Currently available funds, public/private or donor driven, are not sufficient to address flood risk in BAM. The proposed project will catalyze a shift in BAM’s approach towards climate resilience and risk management by enabling different types of measures that in synergy will effectively address flood risk in the main river basin.

## 149. Government investment in flood risk management is mainly focused on ongoing maintenance of very old flood defense structures, which are in poor condition as a result of wartime damage, lack of maintenance, and the present high water levels. The annual budget for climate adaptation and mitigation activities is $27 million. These activities are defined by the legal regulations in effect in each entity, which set out the water use, the amount of changes to be made to the riverbed, and the type of flood risk management measures that should be taken. A small portion of these funds (10%) is allocated to various priority flood risk management activities. The total annual budget for climate adaptation and mitigation activities is $27 million. These activities are defined by the legal regulations in effect in each entity, which comprises mainly donor grants (40%) and loans (60%). This is well below the damages due to flooding which is expected to increase significantly over time as a result of rising sea levels, increased precipitation intensity, and more frequent and public buildings by mid-century due to climate change. It should be noted the 2014 flood resulted in $27 Million in damage.

## 150. Financial analysis of this project's outputs and activities has been carried out in accordance with the Guidelines for the Financial Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program. These guidelines clearly mandate that only those proposed project activities or outputs which can clearly result in direct and quantifiable financial benefits to the project owners or to the project beneficiaries should be included in the analysis, and not only for those proposed project activities or outputs where they can clearly result in direct and quantifiable financial benefits to the project owners or to the project beneficiaries. These guidelines stand to ensure that GCF's minimum concessionary terms are met.

### 151. This project's proposed structure includes 4 outputs and 11 activities. However, none of the activities or outputs clearly result in direct and quantifiable earnings or direct and quantifiable savings to the project owners or the project beneficiaries. The project is not expected to generate any public good that can be attributed directly to the project and that are of public good in nature to the larger community at the risk population in BAA. Hence, a financial analysis has been carried out on this project based on the GCF's minimum concessionary terms.

### 152. However, considering the GCF's minimum concessionary policy, the projects proposed activities have been analyzed from a macro-economic and government perspectives to assess the need for GCF grant as only the results of these activities can be attributed directly to the project. The analysis shows that there is no public good nature of benefits arising out of the post, climate change being the key driver, economic, geographic and demographic factors. Hence, considering the above-mentioned factors, the persistent flood risks, the World Bank's observations and the catalytic role of GCF grants, we recommend the following:

* **GCF funds will leverage co-financing in a form of grants and loans from government and private sector and, at the same time, will ensure additionally in the area of climate changes while simultaneously ensuring that the project is not financially viable.**

* **The nature of the benefits does not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or serviceability cost for the project.**

* **The nature of the beneficiaries and the level of essentiality of the service do not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or serviceability cost for the project.**

* **Increase in government spending to address COVID-19 pandemic has strained public finances.**

### 153. GCF grants are critical for this proposed project given the impact of COVID-19, BAA's current economic situation and the persistent flood risks. BAA is a transitional economy with 51% of the population being rural. Due to the low level of urbanization, the majority of the population lives in areas that are prone to flooding.

* **The relatively slow rate of the post-war economic recovery has been compounded by the negative impacts of climate change and natural disasters such as floods and landslides which have occurred frequently, particularly due to the increased frequency and magnitude of flood disasters, which have tripled in the last decade.**

### 154. BAA's economy and its state's budgets continue to be under stress due to continued tepid growth rates and effects of COVID-19 pandemic. Due to COVID-19, GDP growth is expected to contract between -3.2% and -

**5% from the previously projected GDP growth of 3.4% for 2020. This is expected due to revenue shortfalls via a higher health, social, and economic support spending. As per IMF’s Country Note on Bahrain in response to the World Bank’s climate change adaptation report, this is attributed to the government’s planned expansion into increasing spend on containment and mitigation of the disease. Consequently, from a previously projected 3% GDP growth for 2020, it has now been revised down to 5%. The sovereign credit rating of Bahrain is “B with stable outlook” as per S&P and “B3 with stable outlook” as per Moody’s which are both in line with the country’s economic fundamentals. Since the outbreak of COVID-19, there have been a depreciation of 3% in local currency of Bahrain. This can be indicative of which direction the economy is heading towards.**

### 5.5 Below the COVID pandemic for the country in 2020, Bahrain economic growth rate slowed from 3% per annum in 2016-2018 to 2.7% in 2019.

The Industrial recession in the last years is largely related to spill-over from German and European countries’ recessions. The Kingdom’s economy has been heavily reliant on oil and gas exports, which Bahrain reached a deficit of foreign trade in 2016 at $8 billion, which is a record value since 2008. The EU being the largest trading partner for Bahrain, it is expected that this will continue to be the case as well. This is due to the fundamental weakness of inward orientation / lack of export orientation with total exports to GDP ratio of 40%. Bahrain’s economy has been heavily reliant on oil and gas exports which have been in decline since 2016. This is reflected in GDP per capita (PPP) (purchasing power parity) stands at 32% of EU-7 average. Public sector output as a share of total output is 54%, which is the highest among GCC countries.

Private remittances associated with large diaspora and a large number of workers abroad represented around 8% of GDP in 2019, which is the second largest after Saudi Arabia. The Kingdom’s economy has been worsened due to substantial reduction in remittances in 2020, which the IMF expects at two-thirds of their 2019 level.

### 5.6 According to the ILO, a large part of the workforce is trapped in low-productive jobs in agriculture, construction, tourism and retail, and on average command wages that are 10% lower than the national average. This is a signal of underemployment and unemployment. The Kingdom’s economy has been heavily reliant on public sector working age population works in the formal private sector; it is weakly capitalized and exposed to strong economic shocks which can be seen from the recent COVID-19 pandemic.

### 5.7 The COVID-related shock disrupted Bahrain already weak economic growth. The IMF expects net GDP contraction of 5% in 2020, which implies GDP loss of this compared to the pre-COVID growth trend. After expected remission of the impact of the pandemic, the Kingdom’s economy is expected to recover at a slower pace than before. Authorities communicated expected GDP contractions between 3% and 5% in 2020, depending on the scenario and policy response. It would be overly optimistic if the negative demand shock during summer months has been stronger than expected.

Due to these economic risks that persist and the World Bank’s observations on the region, Bahrain is currently not in a state to re-allocate financing in the form of loans to replace GCF grants for this proposed project. In addition, due to the current economic situation, it is uncertain whether the Kingdom will be able to meet its climate finance obligations. The highest priority structural measures, but if needs technical assistance through timely complementary interventions, such as capacity building and training, to help the project based efforts on the adaptation alternative which will have long-term transformative change.

### 6.3 Investment in human resources and institutions:

The project is focused on strengthening institutions that have a direct impact on food security, including the Ministry of Agriculture and Water, which are their respective climate risk management (Sub-activity 1.3.4) and Activity 2.4). The project investments will improve availability of climate risk information and data for decision-making in the agricultural sector and help to strengthen institutional capacity and benefit from the existing collaborative relationship between UNDP and all the five food risk management partners and other relevant government ministries in Bahrain, which are expected to continue their collaboration beyond the project. The project will help ensure sustainability by supporting all relevant government ministries in line with the Kingdom’s National Food Security Strategy (NFSS) and the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS). This includes support for developing and implementing food risk management strategies, the introduction of risk assessment methods.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 53

## Investment in development of long-term climate risk financing: The project is developing fully articulated long-term investment plans for flood risk management and resilience investments, which will set the direction for resilience plans for highest risks, which will set the direction for systemic risk informed sectoral flood risk management planning. These plans will be based on a cost-benefit analysis methodology and will include investment plans. Cost benefit assessment methodologies and a socio-economic risk model will be embedded into the project’s flood risk financing framework. The project will also develop a long-term flood risk financing strategy, which will guide the development of a long-term flood risk financing plan. This will ensure that long term flood risk financing is cost-effective and sustainable.

## Investment in social and economic assets of vulnerable communities: The damaging effects of extreme events and changes in weather patterns exacerbated by climate change will limit the potential for socioeconomic resilience to be achieved without significant investments in critical infrastructure, such as roads, bridges, and structural measures (Activities 2.2 and 3.3) can help reduce the vulnerability of these areas. Flood defences that are “climate proofed” will also provide co-benefits by supporting the development and implementation of climate-proofing standards for the design and construction of critical infrastructure in other sectors such as energy, transport, water supply and sanitation, agriculture, and food security.

## Investment in natural capital: Securing long-term resilience and safeguard investments and communities against climate induced flood risk, by ensuring functional and protected wetlands, stable, climate resilient and well-managed river systems, will provide co-benefits for the protection of biodiversity, the health and safety of communities and their social and economic assets through the investment in EDA solutions, and the derived eco-system service attendant benefit of improved functions of catchments through effective flood risk management.

## The project will develop an integrated river basin development approach and aim to implement some of the structural and non-structural measures that have been identified as being required for long-term resilience. These will have a direct impact as they will establish the methods, standards and approaches that will work across BMI and define these in partnership with relevant stakeholders. The project will also develop an integrated flood risk financing framework.

There is at least a Precedent replication potential for the EDA measures, community based EWS, municipal risk management plans, and the river basin development approach. Given the geographical and hydrological similarities of all Western Balkans countries, there is a regional replication potential for all aspects of the project.

## Capacity Building
### Under Activity 1.1 the project will provide training to 50 hydraulic practitioners of HMA and Waka in GIZ funded capacity building activities.
170 Under Activity 1.3 the project will undertake an institutional capacity assessment of FPTI institutions and develop a capacity development plan for embedded flood hazard and risk modeling approaches and FPTI institutions. A revised institutional capacity building plan will be developed to address gaps (human, technical and financial) in the current capacity of FPTI institutions. Under Activity 1.4 the project will undertake an institutional capacity assessment of BAH institutions and a revised institutional capacity building plan will be developed to address gaps (human, technical and financial) in the current capacity of BAH institutions. Under Activity 2.3 the project will develop training plans for each technical area of expertise related to climate-induced flood risk assessment and modeling approaches. The training plans will include the development of a short-term and long-term capacity plan for BAH and will consider options such as the development of expert networks, mentoring programs, etc. Under Activity 2.4 the project will develop a long-term capacity plan for BAH which will include measures to address skill shortage, skills retention and succession planning, the project will develop workshops which will include exercises on flood risk assessment and modeling approaches, and the use of Continuous Professional Development (CPD) methods involving cross-fertilizing of staff with expertise in different areas. Under Activity 1.3 the project will also undertake capacity building and training for youth practitioners, including workshops on the results of climate change scenarios and the use of the HPP models and climate information products in HPP operations. For the agricultural sector, the project will support the development of a risk assessment methodology to identify and assess flood risk management by training and embedding training (TNT) with agricultural specialists who will work with local communities to develop appropriate adaptation strategies, introduce new farming methods, and help increase their understanding of how to apply to their agricultural practices.

172. Under Activity 2.3 the project will undertake training in the methodology and guidance for undertaking risk assessments and appraisal of flood mitigation, eco-system analysis and evaluation; and eco-system vulnerability methodologies and tools.

173. Under Activity 2.4 the project will develop and fully embed the long-term capacity development strategy for all aspects of FIRM in Eritrea. It will establish training curricula, and rosters of trainers, mechanisms for continuous professional development, and a framework for the establishment of key partnerships with universities that can be forged to ensure long-term FIRM training. The project will deliver training on flood risk management, including flood forecasting and warning systems, flood forecasting and modelling, EWS, vulnerability and risk assessments, river basin flood risk management planning, and climate change adaptation. It will also undertake a community-based flood risk raising campaign for capacity building at municipal and community levels; Networking and advocacy: Country-level coordination of FIRM activities and the development of a national FIRM strategy.

174. Under Activity 3.1 the project will provide technical advice and training to insurance sector practitioners in the development of insurance products packages from natural disasters adjusted to the needs of vulnerable groups, including representatives of vulnerable groups to exchange information, lessons learned, successes and implementation challenges related to the development of insurance products for disaster risk reduction strategies and their financing. In addition a country-wide media campaign on FIRM and FEWS will be undertaken. The project will also undertake awareness raising activities using various methods including using social media, art and creativity to communicate FIRM and FEWS concepts to broader audiences in Eritrea. It will also develop and disseminate materials such as brochures, posters around FIRM and FEWS; Media coverage of project activities; Production of footage, Public Social Awareness Campaigns; Development of a website for FIRM and FEWS; Development of a brochure on FIRM and FEWS and annual essay/media article awards competitions. The project will utilize its partnerships with the WMO (through WMO/RC), UNESCO/IITU ICT4D and WHO to develop, deliver and embed the community-based training and awareness raising.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

## Technology Development/Transfer

The main technology transfer and development activities under the project are as follows:

### Under Activity 1.1, the project is enhancing the technologies for hydrological monitoring.

This will include the upgrade and modernization (automation) of stations, the development of ICT systems that underpin the data collection process, and enables the efficient use of data from all sources in the modelling mapping and forecasting of floods.

### Under Activity 1.2, the project will enhance the WEFIP hazard risk and management by developing hydrological models for flood risk assessment and management. This includes HPPs and groundwater modelling in flood risk, hazard and vulnerability modelling and mapping for the entire basin. The project will also develop a set of rules that minimize downstream flood risk damage while maintaining dam safety within reasonable limits.

### Under Activity 1.3, the project will establish a fully integrated approach based flood forecasting and early warning system (FEWS) in the context of the WEFIP hazard risk management. This includes developing models, DEMs, etc., forecasting (meteorological forecasting, use of ground weather radar, satellite imagery, river flow data, etc.), and information GIS (web-based information portal and meta-data systems). The project will also develop a set of rules for various users (HPP and agriculture) to participate. Last Mile communication solutions tailored to the needs of different stakeholders will be developed. This includes developing tools and methods for community resilience measures through the combination of structural and non-structural protection measures, as well as tools for SBA. One of these tools is the development of a prototype of the FEWS systems that will support the national integrated FEWS platform, for near real-time forecasting and warning dissemination.

### Under Activity 2.1, the project will scale up successful EEA approaches piloted by the UNDP/SCCF in Vitis Project. This includes developing tools and technologies that were developed to support long-term design and implementation of EDA measures.

### Under Activity 3.1, the project will expand and further develop flood insurance scheme initiated under Vitis project and explore and identify other risk financing products and tools. This represents technology.

D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project demonstrates ambition in its goals and objectives in addressing climate change challenges facing BIMR communities. It will provide a comprehensive approach to address the interlinkages between adaptation benefits, and sustainable development outcomes. The project will change the paradigm of existing flood risk management approaches by introducing innovative technical solutions that will enable improved climate information and understanding of risk, managing non-structural ecosystem-based risk management measures, and integrating these into structural flood risk management strategies. Without project interventions there will be a progressively increasing risk of catastrophic loss to life, property, infrastructure, and livelihoods in BIMR communities. The project aims to reduce the exposure of 799,456 people through introduction of an integrated approach, which will embed new skills, methods, and technologies into existing flood risk management strategies. This will be achieved by developing and implementing a range of risk reduction and management strategies.

The project is enhancing the availability of climate risk information on which all FIRM will be based by scaling up implementation of the Early Warning for All (EWA) initiative to cover flood risks from terrestrial areas, and HPPs, thus reducing the technical scope/size of hazard, risk and vulnerability information. The project will enhance the capacity for early warning systems in BIMR communities through the development of a range of climate resilient platforms and technologies that will empower local communities to undertake local disaster risk reduction (DRR) and mitigation activities. The project will also develop an integrated approach to DRR and adaptation at community level.

The project will develop impact based FEWS for main rivers which will be scalable to cover sub-basins, and will provide a range of climate resilient platforms and technologies that will enable the development of specific sector flood forecasting systems for hydropower and agriculture sectors. Through the development of an integrated approach to DRR and adaptation at community level, the project will enhance the capacity for early warning systems in BIMR communities through the development of a range of climate resilient platforms and technologies scaling up of FEWS in BIMR will be provided the institutional and technical basis for FEWS and services that are tailored to the needs of the communities. The project will also develop an integrated approach to DRR and adaptation at community level.

The project is developing capacity and systems whereby the current gap between the development and implementation of climate change adaptation measures in BIMR communities can be bridged, and implemented by the project will ensure the ongoing transfer of skills to create knowledge sharing and better climate change adaptation outcomes. The project will also develop a range of climate resilient platforms and technologies that will empower local communities to undertake local disaster risk reduction (DRR) and mitigation activities.

The establishment of methods and tools for integrating climate change adaptation into existing flood risk management strategies, including policy, guidance and regulatory documents make this project highly replicable within BIMR. These include changes from land use and natural resources driven by socio-economic pressures while climate change is impacting ecosystems through increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, loss of forest cover, anthropogenic activities that increase deforestation, soil erosion and land degradation are contributing to increasing flood risk. Nature-based solutions (NBS) are increasingly recognized as cost-effective in protecting people, their property, and livelihoods. This, in turn, puts people under increased pressure to resort to traditional engineered solutions which are often expensive, have limited effectiveness and can be environmentally damaging services. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

## Introduction and Background

Initiatives in Europe (e.g., "Making Space for Water" in England) and similar initiatives during 21 EEA and non-structural measures were prioritized for implementation based on CBA analysis of an original project proposal. The total budget is €50 million, with €37 million from the Green Climate Fund and €8 million for financing. Therefore, of the 26 measures, 5 EEA and 13 non-structural measures will be fully financed by government contributions. This means that the GCF co-funding ratio of EEA and non-structural measures is 0% to Government to 21%. GCF co-funding ratio of EEA and non-structural measures shows a very serious commitment from the GCF to support climate change adaptation in Europe. The project will address climate change on forest ecosystems (See para above and Section 10.2 of this FIP) as the project is mainstreaming climate-related issues into all aspects of the project, including EEA and non-structural measures. The project will also include innovative catchment management practices which will protect the forest from the expected impacts of climate change and promote reforestation, afforestation, and other land use changes that are consistent with sustainable development goals. These practices will provide improved services to flood risk management and other biophysical functions. The project will therefore result in a reduction in vulnerability to climate change and an increase in resilience for vulnerable communities. The innovative GCF projects which will reverse the disastrous effects of catchment degradation and enhance livelihoods, including through the creation of new jobs and income opportunities, will also provide improved services to vulnerable communities. The GCF project will be investing in long-term sustainability of communities and their social and economic development by providing flood risk management service attendance benefits of improved financing of catchments through effective flood risk management.

## Project Overview

### 152. The GCF project will leverage additional investments and create a multiple effect through the mobilization of local, national, regional, and international resources to support climate change adaptation in Europe and unlock additional financial flows for climate action in the future through the establishment of risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms.

The project is investing in the development of long-term municipal risk management plans for 10-12 highest-risk municipalities and 1 canton, and sectoral risk management plans for 5 sectors (agriculture, forestry, water resources, energy, and transport). The project will also develop a framework to support the identification and design of climate-proofed structural and non-structural measures and will form the basis of municipal and sectoral risk management plans. The project will also develop a framework for long-term flood risk financing and risk transfer mechanisms to be developed by the project will ensure that long-term financial resources are available to implement these measures, including through the development of cost-effective financing or flood risk reduction measures, and investments over time. This will result in sustainable flood risk management.

### 154. Flood Risk Financing Mechanisms will be developed and implemented and will identify climate and other resources that will be needed during a drought. Forecast-based financing mechanisms have emerged as innovative approaches to disaster risk reduction by providing early warning systems and allowing for timely mobilization of resources, reducing the response time and increasing the effectiveness of interventions.

By using advanced forecasting technologies and models being developed by the project, flood risk financing mechanisms allow for targeted risk reduction measures. These mechanisms include activities such as pre-positioning emergency supplies, establishing early warning systems, and providing financial support to communities in advance of a disaster event occurs and will allow for timely mobilization of resources, reducing the response time and increasing the effectiveness of interventions.

By focusing on risk reduction, these mechanisms help minimize the potential damage and losses caused by floods. These mechanisms can also be used as an alternative or complement to traditional flood risk management strategies by avoiding or reducing the need for emergency response and recovery efforts. By investing in prevention measures such as early warning systems, flood risk financing mechanisms can reduce the costs associated with emergency relief, damage repair, and post-flood recovery can be significantly reduced. These financial resources can then be used to support other aspects of climate change adaptation, including long-term risk management and by empowering communities with information, resources, and early action. Forecast-based finance is a promising approach that can help reduce the vulnerability of communities to future flood events.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

### Mechanisms also support evidence-based decision-making by providing accurate and reliable weather forecasts and early warnings.

This enables authorities, policymakers, and communities to make informed decisions on risk reduction measures and investments in climate-resilient infrastructure. By combining information with decision-support tools, these mechanisms enhance decision-making processes, enabling more efficient and effective flood risk management. Furthermore, they support the development of integrated approaches for flood risk management, including government agencies, meteorological services, humanitarian organizations, local communities, and private sector entities. This leads to better outcomes in terms of flood preparedness, response, and recovery.

195. **Forecast-based finance mechanisms offer a proactive and risk-informed approach to flood risk management:** By leveraging weather forecasts and early warning systems, these mechanisms provide opportunities to take early action on flood risk reduction measures. They enable the identification of high-risk areas and support the development of effective flood management strategies. This leads to financial sustainability by reducing costs of responding to floods and increasing resilience.

196. **In summary, the project is implementing innovative approaches, technologies, and practices that have the potential to catalyze systemic change in FMR in BAH:** It is introducing novel solutions combined with existing experiences and lessons learned from other countries. This includes leveraging technological innovations such as impact-based flood forecasting using technologically advanced data products and models. The project will also promote public-private partnerships, enhance community participation, new approaches to policy and governance through the mainstreaming of climate-induced FMR into sectoral policies and plans. It will support the development of integrated approaches for flood risk management with structural measures with enhanced environmental co-benefits. The project interventions will provide strong scalability and replicability in BAH and beyond. They will also contribute to the implementation of the recommendations arising from BAH and define these in guidance, legal and policy documents, and ensuring that the long-term institutional capacity is built for flood risk management. This will be done by engaging with key stakeholders and bearing in mind institutional and geographical similarities of all Western Balkans countries. There is also a regional perspective.

197. The project will shift the current paradigm by transforming the existing flood risk management practices in BAH from an ex-post facto basis, to reactive post-disaster recovery approach to ex-ante i.e., risk informed preparedness approaches based on adequate climate information and risk knowledge.

### Contribution to the enabling environment

198. **The project will provide a range of tools and methods for creating conditions for climate-resilient communities and their physical assets:** First, innovative tools and methods will be introduced for hazard, risk and vulnerability assessments, early warning systems, and flood forecasting. Second, technical assistance and capacity building will be provided for key flood hazard and risk information that will enable climate-resilient strategies, policies and practices in the sectors of agriculture, energy, water resources management, and urban planning. Third, innovative products and services will enable sector development along climate resilient development pathways, for the HPP and agriculture sectors.

200. **The establishment of a fully integrated impact-based flood forecasting and early warning system across the country is essential to contributing to the enabling environment:** This includes the creation of new climate and weather products (e.g., climate advisories) for various users (IPPF and agriculture) and the establishment of participatory “Last Mile” communication channels. The project will also support the development, testing, validation, and demonstration of cost-effective risk reduction and community resilience measures through the combination of structural and non-structural measures.

201. **Secondly, the project will combine best available science and local knowledge for vulnerability assessment, hazard and risk mapping, and will introduce appraisal-led flood risk management and investment planning processes at over 500 km² scale in BAH:** This will be done by engaging with key stakeholders to ensure that decisions are informed, appraisal-led methods of strategic planning for Flood Risk Management and climate change adaptation


---
**Page 70**
---

202. Thirdly, the project will create the conditions to unlock both public and private sector finance for scalable investments in climate-resilient infrastructure. The project will develop a framework for cost-benefit analysis of investment in climate-resilient infrastructure. This will include public and private sector financial instruments and mechanisms for long-term financing of climate change risks into their business models.

203. Importantly, the project will improve regulatory environment and tools for the promotion of flood insurance generation, including other risk transfer and financing products. This will include assessment of feasibility of micro-insurance for agricultural producers, entrepreneurs and small and medium-sized enterprises in different regions. The project will also develop a framework for long-term diversification of risk, exploration opportunities for use of catastrophe bonds to transfer disaster risk into the capital market, and development of innovative insurance and reinsurance product packages for insurance in each of the defined sectors, based on studies to estimate cost-benefit.

Contribution to Regulatory Framework and policies

204. The project will develop a set of SOPs and Codes of Conduct for each of the institutions responsible for FIRM and FEWS. These will include SOPs for "last-mile" warning and dissemination and SOPs for risk communication and public awareness raising. The project will also develop an information sharing platform and protocol for climate data, across all government institutions in both entities including spatial Data Infrastructure (SDI) and other relevant platforms.

205. The project will enhance the existing legislative and regulatory FIRM framework by mainstreaming the climate-induced flood risk management into sectoral planning for agriculture, forestry, environment, hydrogeographer critical infrastructure, etc., as well as disaster risk reduction and adaptation strategies. A set of tools to assess flood hazard, risk and vulnerability maps, will be scaled out to spatial planning and development control for all other bodies. The project will also develop a framework for the integration of climate change risks into land use policy to increase resilience to flooding risks and will result in a paradigm shift from uncontrolled floodplain development to climate-resilient urban and rural development. This will help develop the country-wide flood zoning plan, policy and legislation, based on hazard, risk and vulnerability mapping.

206. The project will develop strategies for the management of critical infrastructure in the floodplain in order to improve the resilience of these assets against flooding risks. These strategies will include a set of tools and methodologies for reducing vulnerability over time, and embedding flood resilience measures into the planning, design, construction, and use of all categories of critical infrastructure. The project will also develop a set of tools and methodologies, standards for infrastructure design, construction material, use and maintenance of all categories of critical infrastructure.

Potential for Knowledge Sharing and learning

207. The project has a particularly strong emphasis on knowledge generation, learning and information sharing/dissemination. First, the project will enhance and introduce new hazard and risk assessment modeling tools and techniques to improve the accuracy and reliability of flood hazard maps. Second, the project will train WAM and HM staff in the use and maintenance of these new tools and technologies. Furthermore, this project will also develop a set of training materials and guidelines for the use of these tools.

208. The project is generating climate risk information which will be used to increase knowledge and provide information for all stakeholders including decision makers in progressing with climate-resilient development. To enable wide reaching use and application of climate risk information, the project is developing data sharing protocols to enable

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 67

## Data information exchange between water and other sectors relevant for integrated water management and climate change (agriculture, forestry, environment, spatial planning and hydro-energy systems).

### To enable access and sharing of climate risk information, a centralized information system and knowledge sharing platform will be developed. The Climate Risk Information System (CRIS) is an online tool that provides decision-makers with comprehensive GIS data for all spatial data related to the basin flood risk management. The GeoPortal is a GIS-based platform that integrates various types of spatial data and allows users to access, visualize, analyze and share vulnerability maps including loss/damage models, real-time hydro meteorological data, torrents sensitivity model, catchment area, land use, etc.

### The centralized information system will be developed under the project. It will consist of an integrated e-Library databases (including the GIS database) and a knowledge management platform. The e-Library will provide access to all relevant climate risk information that can help increase awareness, provide interactive hazard maps, with integration with social media and possible mobile applications. The knowledge management platform will be used by local level flood risk management actors to share their experiences and lessons learned in the field of flood risk management. It is envisaged that the municipalities for local level flood risk management to interact with communities through the PGIS tool within the project, which will provide a platform for sharing information on climate risks and adaptation measures. The UNDP has responsibility for maintenance and update of the system in the future. In addition, UNDP has developed a Disaster Risk Assessment (DRA) methodology that can be used by local level flood risk management actors to assess the vulnerability of their communities.

### The flood risk management platform will be a multi-party driven mechanism for cross-sector, multi- stakeholder inter-agency coordination and policy guidance on climate change adaptation. It will provide a platform with public-private, and civil society participation involving all concerned entities within BM. The data sharing platform will provide access to the information required by local level flood risk management actors in their decision making process in FIRM. It would also have mechanisms for effective dialogue with Local Platforms in place in order to influence, encourage and coordinate action. In particular, the country wide Flood Risk Information Management System (FRIMS) will be used to understand vulnerability of communities and issues.

### Under Activity 2.4.1, the project will implement district-wide engaging programs in the six prioritized national areas for flood risk management. It will also develop a knowledge management response, based on previous institutional capacity needs and gap assessment of state, entity. The project will aim to embed training in FIRM and other related activities within the existing structures of the BM. This will be achieved through the development of a Training of Trainers (ToT) manual that will provide guidance for delivering long-term capacity development such as development of key partnerships with universities and other academic institutions, and professional associations.

### The project will implement a knowledge management (KM) framework (See Chapter 11 of the FS for detailed description of the KM framework). It will have the following key aims:

- To ensure that all stakeholders have access to relevant data as well as long-term access to data on what stakeholders’ essential institutional functions rely and other data and information that can be used for evidence-based decision making.
- Connect key stakeholder groups, practitioners, and experts to ensure that key learning and experience is shared across the sector.
- Ensure staff in the stakeholder institutions know about effective and relevant KM techniques so that knowledge is sustained, retained, and reinforced by the institutions and shared within and across the sector (institutional KM improvement).

### By developing and promoting KM as a tool for continuous and sustainable improvement and ensuring that KM tools are embedded into the day-to-day work of all users and maintained within the stakeholder institutions (Developing and embedding KM tools and practices).

### Knowledge Brokerage: The Technology Advisory Working Group to be comprised of representatives from government, academia, private sector, civil society organizations, international organizations, local agricultural institutes, local governments together with associations of cities and municipalities of Republica Srpska and Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina. The TAWG will provide technical advice on the development of FIRM. It will additionally act as a convener and facilitator linking between government, academia, and private sector to create conducive environments to enable the take up of scientific expertise and evidence to be developed by the

### D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will deliver sustainable development co-benefits including enhanced ecosystem services, gender equality, and improved food security. The main socio-economic co-benefits from the project investment are derived from the avoided socio-economic impacts of climate change in BAH. These include avoiding US$750 Million ESF Annual Damage (EAD) for households, businesses, agriculture, and public buildings. The avoided EADs will be significant as they represent 12% of the total annual GDP of BAH. The benefits to sectors such as agriculture and hydropower could be significant. The project interventions that provide sector-specific co-benefits include: (i) flood risk management; (ii) climate risk information planning for the hydropower sector, such as information on water flow forecasts will be critical for planning, risk information will assist with optimizing operations of hydroelectric power plants and reducing greenhouse gas emissions. The project will also provide information to help reduce the impacts of floods in BAH, such as information on peak flood levels, the project will help the Government of BAH to reduce and optimize budget spending on rescue and rehabilitation efforts. (iii) climate risk information planning for agriculture sector, which will be critical for social significantly projects and infrastructure. Economic co-benefits will also be realized in the agriculture sector where the project will provide information that will assist with optimizing operations of irrigation systems. Prevention of agricultural losses coupled with enhanced agricultural livelihoods through agro-forestry solutions.

This project will directly benefit the most vulnerable parts of the population and will have significant benefits to gender equality. The project will engage local gender experts to ensure that gender considerations are a key part any project interventions. Women in BAH are more exposed to climate change impacts, and they suffer disproportionately. In BAH there are significant gender inequalities, fueled by traditional gender roles. Men are more likely to be employed in the formal sector of agriculture, while women are more likely to work in informal food-related tasks, the project will ensure that women are primary stakeholders and will therefore need to be involved in all aspects of the project from planning through implementation and monitoring and evaluation.

Environmental co-benefits mainly relate to EDA strategies and nature-based solutions such as oyster planting and agro-forestry which will provide water retention functions; regulation of hydrological flows (buffer runoff, soil conservation, sedimentation control); reduction in erosion and landslides; increased groundwater recharge; peak flow reduction, soil erosion and landslide control; increased streambed stabilization resulting in decreased erosion and sedimentation; reduced flooding risk; improved water quality; protection of aquatic ecosystems, infrastructure, and economic sectors. Further, the project will enhance the resilience of forest ecosystems to climate change impacts by increasing their ability to absorb carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions and reducing greenhouse gas emission from increased tree yield. The project is addressing climate-induced flood risks by introducing FRM and CCR measures in BAH. These include: improved water management systems; enhanced early warning, introduction of disaster risk reduction, climate change adaptation and protection of people, property, and the environment from major hazards. In the long run the project will bring about significant environmental benefits by
