D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

218.BAH is a middle-income country with an estimated 3.5 million inhabitants. The 1992-1995 war had a devastating impact on the economy and infrastructure, resulting in prolonged reconstruction and economic social recovery. This challenge has been further compounded by the transition towards market-based economies, which have not yet fully materialized due to limited resources and with limited market reforms. The economy relies heavily on the export of metals, energy, textiles, and furniture as well as on remittances from expatriates. The country's economic performance is also constrained by its weak policy coordination and reform, while excessive bureaucracy and a segmented market discourage foreign investment. BAH has been able to achieve a 5.8% annual growth rate since the beginning of the decade in 1998 - it pegged the euro through a currency board arrangement, which has maintained confidence in the country's economic performance. The government has also implemented a social safety net program and a pension program, but it has struggled to meet the economic reform benchmarks required to receive all future lending instruments. Implementing these reforms has been further complicated by political instability, high unemployment and unemployment to soar, but the economy made progress until 2008, when the global economic crisis caused a downturn in BAH's economy. BAH has posted positive economic growth, though securing funding in 2014 significantly hampered the recovery.

219.Due to the war time devastation and a slow transition of economy, a large part of BAH's population still lives in poverty. The country's Gini coefficient (a measure of income inequality) increased from 38 percent in 1995 to 60 percent in 2014, an increase of 22 percentage points. Human inequality coefficient for BAH is equal to 15.3 percent. BAH has a Gender Inequality Index value of 0.149, meaning that women are 14.9% less likely than men to have access to education and health care services. The country's vulnerability to climate change is exacerbated by the negative impacts of climate on key sectors such as agriculture, energy (hydropower), the economy, and water resources. BAH has experienced a number of natural disasters in recent years, including the second largest flood record on record causing damages of US$ 200 million or approximately 1% of GDP in 2013. The country also recorded its worst flooding in 159 years which resulted in 26 deaths and USD $7.1 Billion worth of damages (15% GDP).

220.The most damaging floods have had devastating impacts on the most vulnerable groups including the rural poor, women, children, and the elderly. The majority of BAH's population lives in areas that are highly susceptible to flooding; the flood victims belong to one of these vulnerable groups. For example, in several municipalities in the Vizas basin of the country, 80% of the households live below poverty line and more than half of them have no access equipped to cope with and recover from floods. This has led to a deepening of poverty in flood-affected areas. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts as well as agriculture, water management, hydropower, and limits country's already low adaptive capacity. Please refer to Annex A (Country Profile) for further information on this vulnerability and needs.

221.Aims of the FS provides a detailed assessment of the institutional capacity in all key aspects of flood risk management. BAH institutions and professionals do not have adequate experience in identifying hazard and managing risks, nor are they equipped with the necessary skills to implement effective policies and strategies for institutional structure, and a lack of coordination and cooperation hinders successful implementation of those actions. The FS aims at strengthening the institutional capacity by improving the intergovernmental (national state, entity, cantonal and municipal levels) communication within the water management sector further limits effectiveness of flood risk management in BAH. The FS also aims to improve the technical and financial resources to strengthen the hydrologic monitoring on which the strategic assessment and forecasting of flood risks relies, developing a national flood risk management strategy, and improving the institutional capacity by implementing technical and financial human resources to develop and implement a range of modern flood risk management strategies. These include: (i) strengthening the institutional capacity for flood risk management in BAH by implementing FFWEs according to revised and agreed institutional arrangements; roles, and responsibilities for FFWEs; (ii) improving the intergovernmental communication within the water management sector through coordination and eliminate institutional fragmentation; (iii) implementing a number of state-of-the art technologies, equipment and tools, developing a capacity building plan and framework and undertaking training.

D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

222 The overall development is initiated at the request of the Government of BiH and builds upon a very strong national ownership. BiH became a party to the UNFCCC on 6 December 2008, and since then has worked to enter into several international agreements related to climate change adaptation and low emission development.

B&H NDC adopted the Internationally Determined Contributions (INDCs) under the Paris Agreement in October 2015. The B&H NDC is a comprehensive document that includes a section on climate change adaptation, and the updated section on contribution to climate change mitigation. It covers all three dimensions of sustainable development: economic growth, social inclusion, water resources, forestry, energy, tourism, biodiversity and sensitive ecosystems, and human health with the two main objectives: (i) reducing greenhouse gas emissions; and (ii) increasing resilience to climate change.

The importance of detailed planning of short, medium and long-term adaptation measures, development of climate risk financing mechanisms, and capacity building for effective implementation is highlighted in the B&H NDC. The B&H first Climate Change Adaptation and Low Emission Development Strategy (CCALEDS), adopted on 12 December 2013, sets out a comprehensive framework to address climate change adaptation and mitigation priorities and details measures for adaptation to climate change and capacity building needs with the aim of promoting sustainable development. The CCALEDS covers all three dimensions of sustainable development: economic growth, social inclusion, water resources, forestry, energy, tourism, biodiversity and sensitive ecosystems as well as agricultural sector and HPPs to climate-induced risks. The CCALEDS was updated in 2020 and includes the priority areas for adaptation and mitigation measures with a focus on climate change impacts on ecosystems as well as agricultural sector and HPPs to climate-induced risks.

1: Climate change risks, vulnerabilities and opportunities are reliably identified, quantified and effectively communicated to all relevant stakeholders. The CCALEDS is supported by a comprehensive regulatory framework supporting adaptation, coupled with delivery capacity, is addressing climate change risks and vulnerability in the context of sustainable development. The CCALEDS has been fully developed and implemented and their objectives and planned delivery are understood by an informed society. Outcome 4: A well-resourced and coordinated institutional framework for climate change adaptation is established. The proposed project directly contributes to the stated outcomes of the CCALEDS 2020-2030 strategy. In particular, Outcome 1: Climate change risks, vulnerabilities and opportunities are reliably identified, quantified and effectively communicated to all relevant stakeholders. The CCALEDS is supported by a comprehensive regulatory framework supporting adaptation, coupled with delivery capacity, is addressing climate change risks and vulnerability in the context of sustainable development.

223 The European Floods Directive on the assessment and management of flood risks (2015/34/EU) (EFD) was adopted to address flood risk which was not included in the European Water Framework Directive (2000/60/EU) (WFD). The EFD is a comprehensive framework for assessing, managing and reducing flood risks. It focuses on the main aim of reducing the risk of floods and taking into account the impact of future climate change on flood risk. The EFD provides a framework for flood risk assessment and management, and requires public authorities to publish flood risk maps at least every 6 years. The EFD also sets out requirements for flood risk management plans (FRMPs). The EFD is fully aligned with the WFD and other EU legislation such as the Natura 2000 Directive of 1992, the Habitats Directive of 1992 and the Water Framework Directive of 2000. The EFD also sets out requirements for flood risk management plans (FRMPs). The proposed project activities under Objective 3: Flood risk assessment and management are fully aligned with Steps 1-4 of the EFD, which includes: Step 1: Assessing flood risks; Step 2: Developing flood risk maps; Step 3: Developing flood risk management plans (FRMP); and Step 4: Implementing FRMPs. The proposed project activities under Objective 3: Flood risk assessment and management are fully aligned with Steps 1-4 of the EFD, which includes: Step 1: Assessing flood risks; Step 2: Developing flood risk maps; Step 3: Developing flood risk management plans (FRMP); and Step 4: Implementing FRMPs. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 71

## Background

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is working on a project to advance the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process for medium-term planning and long-term investment in climate change adaptation, with a focus on structural approaches.

### Project Objectives

- Implementing non-structural measures recognized by PFMR as well as long-term institutional capacity development plans for climate-resilient FIRM.
- Strengthening the knowledge base of national authorities to support NAPs through the implementation of the CALEADS 2013 and advancing adaptation planning in BMI with a focus on sectoral approaches, upgrading the knowledge base of national authorities to support NAPs.

### Project Components

- Developing a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for medium-term planning and long-term investment in climate change adaptation.
- Demonstrating innovative ways of financing adaptation at the central, local, and rural government levels.

## Climate Change Impacts on Food Security

As a response to devastating 2014 floods, the Council of Ministers in November 2014 adopted an Action Plan for Flood Protection and River Management (2015-2021). The project is a direct response to the priorities that have emerged from the BMI National Communications, CALEADS and Action Plan for Flood Protection and River Management.

## Climate Change Adaptation

Strong country ownership is also demonstrated by significant government co-financing commitment. Co-financing supports the development of the NAP and will be used in the implementation phase to finance the GCF project for structural measures and additionally through US$26 million of the public funds to be provided by both the Government of BMI and the World Bank.

## Project Development

To initiate the development of this project, a UNDP-GCF technical mission was undertaken in March 2018. Since then, the GCF Secretariat has been working with national and regional authorities, including BMI, GCF NDA have been conducted. The NDAs as well as other relevant institutions in charge of environment and water/food management expressed their interest to participate in the project development process and to contribute to the outcomes of the existing Vittoria River Project.

## Validation Meeting

BMI is about to initiate development of its first GCF country program document (CPD) for the NAP project. The validation meeting was held on 18 September 2019, with a letter of support from BMI's Minister of Environment and Water Resources. A draft of structural measures, equipment, and modeling/FEWS systems.

## Further Consultations

Further consultations were conducted with key stakeholders in June and September 2018 when the final Note was presented and finalized at joint workshops. The Project Idea was also presented in detail to the Project Boards and other relevant institutions in charge of environment and water/food management, as well as the Ministry of Environment and Environment.

## Efficiency and Effectiveness

The project is expected to result in cost savings from reduced flood damage due to climate-induced floods through the provision of technical capacities, policies and legislative enhancements, and by investing in structural and non-structural measures. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the NAPs' goals for reducing vulnerability to climate change impacts on food security.

## Conclusion

By addressing the capacity to undertake risk assessment and monitoring and to generate missing climate information, the project is expected to improve the ability of BMI to implement its adaptation strategies in the future. In addition, by addressing the legislative and policy gaps, the project is ensuring long-term sustainability of projects and investments as the authorities will have a more integrated approach to address root causes, which will have long-term effects and effectiveness benefits.

### 231. Most damaging floods in BAH (2010 - 1% of GDP and 2015 - 1% of GDP) have had devastating impacts on the most vulnerable groups including the rural poor, war returnees and displaced persons. The proposed project has the potential to reduce flood risk by up to 30%, which would be a significant contribution towards achieving approximately (26%) of the entire population of BAH and 44% of the population of the 69 municipalities covered by the proposed project. The proposed project will also provide direct benefits for vulnerable groups, including segments of population, particularly women (51.7%), children (15.2%) and the elderly (13.7%) of the affected population.

### 232. Based on detailed economic analysis (Annex A), the project has an estimated internal rate of return (ERR) of 10%. The sensitivity analysis of the project was carried out in accordance with the Guidance for the Economic Analysis of Projects of United Nations Development Program. The economic efficiency of the investment was determined by comparing the net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR). The ERR is calculated as the economic internal rate of return (ERR). The economic analysis was conducted at both the national level, and than for each municipality covered by the proposed project. The results show that the proposed flood risk reduction investment project is economically efficient as measured by NPV and IRR. The analysis also shows the proposed intervention will provide significant benefits to the communities in terms of increased agricultural productivity of the Vibas sub-basin which has an ERR slightly lower than 10%. These different types of sensitivity analyses were conducted and demonstrated that the economic efficiency of the project as a whole is robust. There is thus no doubt about the economic viability of the proposed project at both national level, for each municipality covered by the target area, and as a whole.

### 234. Overall, the proposed solutions are based on successful best practices, community ownership, and synergies between different sectors. The proposed interventions will be cost-effective and have a high return on investment and cost-effectiveness. The proposed activities and interventions will reach 902,806 people or 25% of the population at risk from flooding and will indirectly benefit 100% of the population through better economic resilience and stable GDP in the face of food insecurity.

### How the project/programme will contribute (Description)
The following project activities will contribute to the paradigm shift through scaling-up FIRM solutions:
- Activity 1.1: Upgrade and expand the coverage of the FIRM system in BIM, including flood risk assessment, climate variables in Category I catchments and torrential streams contributing to both geographic and technological scaling up of the FIRMs in BIM.
- Activity 1.2: Enhance climate-induced flood hazard, risk and vulnerability information for decision-making through improved data collection and sound decision making for climate induced flood management including early warning systems. This will enhance the coverage of flood risk from torrential streams, and HPFs, thus extending the technical scope/scope of hazard, risk and vulnerability information.
- Activity 1.3: Develop an integrated impact-based centralized and coordinated flood risk management system in BIM. The project will develop impact based FIRM for men which envisages

## E

### Sustainability

- **Flood risk management in B&amp;H has been undertaken mainly through donor funded projects and programs with relevant ministries. In some cases, the established flood risk management systems are limited due to the limited technical and geographic coverage of these systems and lack of sustainable government financing to sustain the systems over time.**
  - Long-term QAM financing for implementing climate-resilient structural and non-structural measures is lacking in B&amp;H. There is a need for capacity generation, sharing of climate risk information and data, institutionalization of regular update of technical standards and guidelines is lacking.
    - **Activity 1.1 Upgrade and expand the coverage of the hydrometric network to include additional climate variables: Under Sub-activity 1.1.4 the project will set up a new hydrometric station in B&amp;H, and under Sub-activity 1.1.4 will develop long-term financing mechanisms for the operation and maintenance of this network. This will ensure the sustainability of the rehabilitated hydrometric monitoring network on which all FIRM activities will rely in the future.**

- **Low**
  - Long-term financial and institutional capacities for implementing climate-resilient structural and non-structural measures are lacking in B&amp;H.
    - **Activity 1.2 Enhance climate-induced flood risk hazard, loss and damage assessment and decision making for climate induced flood management solutions: Under Sub-activity 1.2.4 the project will develop a method to assess flood risk hazards, losses and damages which result in sustainable solutions. It will also embed the method, tools and technologies developed into the FIRM system for generation, updating and availability of climate risk information.**
    - **Activity 1.3 Develop institutional capacity for data generation, data management and communication for climate-resilient flood risk management: Under Sub-activity 1.3.4 will provide the basis for institutional collaboration and data sharing on FIRM in the future.**

## Regulatory frameworks and enabling environment for resilient development pathways in a climate-driven manner

### Lack of climate risk assessment and adaptation policy: CC-related national policies, sectoral policies, and disaster risk reduction policies. Flows are largely lacking.

#### Policies, regulations, technical guidelines developed and adopted (Sub-Section 2.1)
- Climate-induced flood risk management for agriculture, livestock, fisheries, and forestry
- Technical guidelines for climate change adaptation in agriculture, livestock, fisheries, and forestry
- Technical guidelines for climate change adaptation in hydropower, agriculture, and fisheries
- Technical guidelines for climate change adaptation in infrastructure design (Sub-Section 2.1)
- Critical infrastructure technical guidelines

#### ESA technical guidelines
- Incomplete hydrological network with only 20% of the river basins covered by automatic observation networks.
- Limited climate-induced flood risk assessment and mapping using modern methodologies, models, and technologies (EUFO modeling tools are used in two basin teams only).
- Rating map: Repeated and complex hydrologic observation networks, automatic observation stations and gauging stations.
- Enhanced EUFO hazard and risk models and maps. These are used and used in decision making in those 6 basins.
- Voluntary flood warning systems using updated data products and providing real-time flood risk information in operational mode (Sub-Section 2.1)
- Impact-based FFWEs operational for all six river basins (2 of 6 basins only).

## Co-benefit 3: (Gender) Female practitioners trained in all aspects of FRM guidance and risk responsive risk assessments and FRM framework established

### Activities Description Sub-activities Deliverables

#### Activity 1.1: Upgrade and expand the coverage of the hydrometric network for enhanced monitoring of climate change impacts on catchments and torrential streams
* Develop optimized hydrometric network specification, develop ICT strategy and plan for implementation.
* Procure and install equipment to increase coverage of the hydrometric network and expand monitoring to include additional stations.
* Rebuild the biggest catchments (category 1) with average depth of 50-70 cm; 20 torrential streams with average depth of 30-60 cm; 40 Meteorological stations, 23 Hydrological stations.
* Develop long-term financing mechanisms for the maintenance of the hydrometric network.

#### Sub-activities
1.1.1. Develop optimized hydrometric network specification, develop ICT strategy and plan for implementation.
1.1.2. Procure and install equipment to increase coverage of the hydrometric network and expand monitoring to include additional stations.
1.1.3. Rebuild the biggest catchments (category 1) with average depth of 50-70 cm; 20 torrential streams with average depth of 30-60 cm; 40 Meteorological stations, 23 Hydrological stations.
1.1.4. Develop long-term financing mechanisms for the maintenance of the hydrometric network.

#### Deliverables
Hydro-meteorological observation network with 50 new monitoring stations: 24 rehabilitated piezometers, 18 new piezometers, 6 new boreholes; 30 torrential streams with average depth of 30-60 cm; 40 Meteorological stations, 23 Hydrological stations.

### Action 1.3: Develop an Integrated Impact-based combined and community based flood forecasting and early warning system (FEWS)

Under the Project, the Vitrus prototype will be used as a basis for developing a more intensive basin of Nenetsia and Tundra, and other basins in BAH with updated data.

The project will implement FEWS products and develop capacity building activities on impact-based flood hazard risk modeling approaches and FEWS application into appropriate institutions in BAH

| 1.3.1: Develop and implement National impact based FEWS system (integrated and combined) |
| --- | --- |
| 1.3.2: Implement GEWIS for high priority systems |
| 1.3.3: Review, identify and develop sector specific FEWS products for agriculture and livestock |
| 1.3.4: Identify and implement a capacity building program on impact-based flood hazard risk modeling approaches and FEWS into appropriate institutions in BAH |

- Strategy and programme of updating specifications for the inclusion of upstream monitoring and mapping
- Review of existing models/training programmes and training needs
- Training of experts/models trained in Flood hazard and risk modelling
- Development of institutional, organisational, financial and human resources for implementation and operation at MMS
- A flood hazard database with meteorological and hydrological data
- The tools and services for online and offline access to the flood hazard database, quality and quantity control, database management and maintenance

- Flood hazard risk modeling and FEWS application institutionalized in BAH

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

F.1 Mitigation measures (see 3 pages)

255. Risk factors associated with the project implementation include institutional, policy, financial, technical and operational aspects of establishing an integrated climate-resilient flood risk management and introducing an impact-based adaptation approach to reduce risks from floods and droughts. The project will also introduce a portfolio of climate-resilient nature-based solutions and construction of protective infrastructures against flood risks. The latter may have the potential to displace communities, which could lead to social conflicts and other negative impacts that may affect the level of commitment from communities where restoration activities, alternative livelihoods and EWS are planned. The proposed project includes several mitigation measures to address these risks.

Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |
| Description | Political situation becomes unstable due to local upheavals or regional conflicts |

Mitigation Measure(s):

The project will develop and implement environmental and social management plan in line with UNDP CO's crisis management requirements. This may reduce the level of impact from risk to medium to low level

Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical/Operational | Medium | Medium |

Description: Extreme weather event resulting in damage or destruction to hydrometeorological and/or flood defense infrastructure, damage to beneficiary communities and disruption of project implementation, requiring reprogramming of government FIRM budgets or implementation delays.

Mitigation Measure(s):

The project will develop and implement environmental and social management plan in line with UNDP CO's crisis management requirements. During the planning, design and construction of relevant infrastructure, disaster risks will be taken into account to ensure that the infrastructure is designed and constructed in a way that minimizes these risks. Importantly, the project is designing and implementing climate proofing of infrastructure which should ensure that even if extreme weather events occur, they do not result in damage or destruction of the infrastructure. These risk reduction measures will reduce the level of impact and probability that the infrastructure will be destroyed to minimum level.

Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

Description: Climate adaptation, FIRM and DRR do not remain a government priority and therefore political, financial and technical support to the project is reduced

Mitigation Measure(s):

The project will leverage (and strengthen) existing relationships with strong partners at high-level government representatives and will carry out lobbying and advocacy campaigns in support of CC adaptation, FIRM and DRR. This will reduce the impact of the risk to the minimum level.

## Description

### Prohibited Practices Risks

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

UNDP applies a zero tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. The guiding principles of UNDP's commitment to the fight against Fraud, Corruption and other related Anti-Corruption and Compliance (FCC) issues are set out in the UNDP’s Tackling Corruption and Other Related Anti-Corruption and Corrupt Practices (the “FCP Policy”), which applies to all activities and operations of UNDP, including projects and programmes. The FCP Policy is aligned with other relevant international policy frameworks relating to fraud, corruption and related anti-corruption issues.

Under the terms of the FCP Policy and related policies, UNDP has a commitment when developing a new programme/project that it will ensure that adequate and effective measures are in place to mitigate risks associated with fraud and corruption. These measures must be considered in the programme/project design and processes and that adequate and effective measures to mitigate such risks are put in place prior to the start of any project or programme. UNDP is committed to ensuring that all programmes/projects require UNDP to carry out a thorough due diligence of the risks in relation to the programme/project it intends to execute, including but not limited to, an assessment of the capacity of the implementing entity and other entities involved with the programme/project. Such capacity assessments include, among other things, a screening of all entities being considered for inclusion as partners or sub-contractors under the programme/project against the UNDP’s List to which UNDP, by virtue of being an organ of the United Nations and therefore being part of the same entity, is also subject. The assessment will be conducted in accordance with the relevant provisions set out in paragraph 41 interest. Based on this capacity assessment, UNDP then builds an internal control component into the design of the project and ensures that the financial management of the programme/project contains adequate safeguards to prevent, monitor and address any risks and acts of fraud, corruption or AML/CFT that may be identified.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### 238 The ESIA has been prepared in accordance with UNDP's Social and Environmental Screening Procedure (SESP), to identify any potential social and environmental risks that could arise from the project implementation. The ESIA identifies and assesses these risks, as well as mitigation measures for each risk identified. It also provides a detailed description of the potential risks in more detail (see Annex E). The ESAA has been prepared using publicly available information, market research, and expert judgment. The ESAA is based on the project design at the time of preparation by project team members, and will be updated by a safeguard specialist to representative sites.

#### 239 The environmental risks identified in the ESIA are categorized as follows:

- **High (H)**: These risks are considered unacceptable or unmitigable. They require further assessment and/or mitigation measures.
- **Medium (M)**: These risks are considered acceptable but may have significant impacts on the environment if not managed properly through the application of mitigation measures. The ESIA includes a description of these risks, along with proposed mitigation measures to reduce their impact or eliminate them entirely.
- **Low (L)**: These risks are considered acceptable and manageable through the application of mitigation measures. The ESIA includes a description of these risks, along with proposed mitigation measures to reduce their impact.

#### 240 The majority of environmental risks identified in the ESIA fall into one of three categories:

- **High (H)**
- **Medium (M)**
- **Low (L)**

### Environment

Environmental benefits will primarily be associated with construction activities for the new works, and access to land for these works and associated activities. The majority of the construction impacts will be temporary in nature, such as localized soil compaction, vegetation damage, and sedimentation. These impacts are expected to be mitigated by the implementation of appropriate mitigation measures.

### 239 Environmental benefits will primarily be associated with construction activities for the new works, and access to land for these works and associated activities. The majority of the construction impacts will be temporary in nature, such as localized soil compaction, vegetation damage, and sedimentation. These impacts are expected to be mitigated by the implementation of appropriate mitigation measures.

#### 240 The project is addressing climate-induced flood risks by introducing FIRM and CCA measures. In general, the project will contribute to reducing the vulnerability of communities in BWHs to climate change through adaptation and protection of people, property, and the environment from major hazards. In the long run, the project will also contribute to improving the resilience of communities against climate-induced natural disasters such as floods and droughts, thus enabling its population to better protect national assets, including environmental assets.

#### 241 Environmental co-benefits mainly relate to EDA strategies such as riparian plantings and agro-forestry which will provide a range of ecosystem services. These include: improved water quality (e.g., increased groundwater recharge, maintenance of base flow); natural hazard mitigation (e.g. flood prevention, peak flow reduction; soil erosion control); enhanced biodiversity conservation; and improved livelihoods through the provision of food security, preservation, and restoration. Environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due to the implementation of appropriate mitigation measures.

#### 242 The project will not have any significant adverse environmental impact on communities in target areas (see Section D).

### Social

The project is expected to directly benefit approximately 26% of the population. Project interventions will be undertaken in areas of BWH where communities are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The project will contribute to reducing poverty and improving livelihoods by providing access to improved water management and intervention measures at high level and will safeguard vulnerable communities and their economic assets against climate-induced natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and landslides. The project does not require any land acquisition and/or resettlement.

#### 243 The project will contribute to improving the resilience of communities in target areas by planning, design and managing rural infrastructure that is climate-resilient. By enhancement of the existing legislative and regulatory framework in water resources management, disaster risk reduction, and environmental protection, as well as through critical infrastructure and spatial planning, the project will directly protect and save lives, increase food production safety, and flood risk informed local development and land use.

## Gender and social inclusion

### gender

247. The project will directly benefit the most vulnerable parts of the population, particularly women who are disproportionately affected by flooding and disasters in general. In fact there are significant gender inequalities fuelled by traditional female roles. The project has been designed with attention paid to gender and social inclusivity throughout its implementation.

248. The project will ensure that women are primary stakeholders and will therefore need to be involved in decisions on the project’s design, implementation and monitoring. Women will also be empowered through their participation in climate disasters with particular attention to women, and other vulnerable groups. The Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) will be developed as part of the project.

249. The project will ensure that women are empowered to benefit from the structural interventions that will mitigate flood risks and reduce disaster impacts. This will be achieved through a gender-sensitive approach that addresses inequality on gender equality. With regard to EAM approaches, the project will implement agro-forestry, which will enable smallholder farmers to diversify their livelihoods in addition to increasing resilience against climate change impacts.

250. Among other things, under the revised municipal investment plan, the project will formulate and implement gender sensitive community preparedness plans in selected municipalities (10 out of 12): Una-Sana and Bihor basins. The project will also ensure that women are involved in all aspects of the project’s implementation including awareness actions including access to knowledge (trainings), information and resources that will increase capability of both men and women to respond effectively to climate change impacts.

## Vulnerable Groups in Bosnia and Herzegovina

### Main Vulnerable Groups

- **Socioeconomically Disadvantaged Communities:** Socioeconomically disadvantaged communities, including low-income households, marginalized groups, and those living in informal settlements or substandard housing.
- **Rural and Agricultural Communities:** Rural communities, particularly those engaged in agriculture and subsistence farming. These communities are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to limited access to services making it challenging for these communities effectively prepare for, respond to, and recover from flood events.

### Elderly Population

The elderly population faces heightened vulnerability to flood risk due to physical limitations, limited mobility, and underlying health conditions. These factors can hinder their ability to evacuate, seek shelter, or access emergency services during a flood event. The elderly may face challenges in recovering and rebuilding their lives after a flood.

### Children and Young People

Children and young people are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to their dependency on caregivers, increased health risks, and potential disruptions to education and social support networks. Displacement or loss of family members can have long-lasting impacts on their well-being, development, and future opportunities.

### People in Remote Areas

People living in remote areas, especially those who are isolated from essential services, particularly those living in remote or marginalized areas, may face disproportionate vulnerability to flood risk. Limited access to resources such as clean water, food, and medical care can exacerbate the impact of flooding on these communities. Barriers to access due to cultural and language barriers may also impede effective communication and coordination during emergency situations.

### People with Disabilities

People with disabilities may face additional challenges during floods, such as limited mobility, sensory impairments, or reliance on assistive devices. Barriers in physical infrastructure, lack of inclusive disaster preparedness plans, and limited access to information can further increase their vulnerability and reduce their ability to safely evacuate or access necessary assistance.

### Conclusion

Environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due to the nature of the interventions and the ability to manage impacts through implementation of mitigation measures, while social benefits are significant and can provide long-term improvements to the lives of communities in the target areas. Budgeting for environmental interventions and the application of mitigation measures to enhance positive impacts for vulnerable groups is a key component of the project at both local and national levels. The end result of this expenditure will be that there will be reduced river sedimentation and erosion, stronger community resilience, improved access to services, increased economic opportunities for communities, and overall improvement in the quality of life of the population as an investment in the future of the people of Bosnia and Herzegovina. This project is expected to have a rapid payback times over through reduced long-term operation and maintenance costs of implementing the project.

## Gender assessment and action plan (500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will ensure that women are fully involved in all aspects of the project, including planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. Women's participation is essential for the successful implementation of climate change adaptation strategies and actions. As a result of the project implementation more lives, property and productive assets will be secured from the impacts of climate-induced disasters. While all BH population will benefit from

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 111

The project, the gender gap will decrease as women's benefits will be relatively bigger given greater dependences of their livelihoods on natural resources. During the proposed development individual gender-specific impacts and needs have been examined through the lenses of vulnerability, capacities and voices and this will continue throughout implementation. The Gender Action Plan (Annex B) outlines specific actions to ensure that women’s voices support most vulnerable social groups among which women in BIA are specifically recognized. By enabling equal participation of men and women, the project ensures that climate change adaptation is more effective and sustainable.

### 250. **Gender equality**

250.1 The following paragraphs provide an overview of the gender equality situation in the country. It has been made in the application of the principle of the gender equality, which is a result of activities taken by implementing agencies and partners to ensure that women’s voices are heard.

250.2 The Gender Action Plan (Annex B) outlines specific actions to ensure that women's voices support most vulnerable social groups among which women in BIA are specifically recognized. By enabling equal participation of men and women, the project ensures that climate change adaptation is more effective and sustainable.

250.3 The vulnerability of women in BIA is caused by social and economic reasons which are deeply rooted in national policies and practices. Women’s voices are not heard at all levels of decision making. Women hold few positions, at all levels, remains unsatisfactory. Hence there is reduced participation of women in decision-making including representation on boards and committees. Women who have higher education or who have access to secondary/tertiary education are also more likely to be unemployed than men with the same education. Out of total number of employed people, 70% are women and out of total number of unemployed people, 85% are women. The prevalence of traditional socio-cultural gender norms supports male dominance. Men account for over 70 percent of employment in the formal sector while women dominate informal sector. Women’s voices are not heard at all levels of decision making including representation on boards and committees.

250.4 Social gender stereotypes that restrict women's involvement outside of household and family are even more deeply rooted in rural areas.

### 251. **The existing barriers to equality**

251.1 The existing barriers to equality (e.g., limited engagement of women in planning and decision making) and traditional distribution of gender roles in families and communities still call for tailoring and targeting of the project interventions. These barriers are also reflected in the current gender gap in access to resources, which effectively involve women in FIRM processes the project has dedicated special attention to gender issues under several components. The project will ensure that women’s voices support most vulnerable social groups among which women in BIA are specifically recognized.

251.2 The Gender Action Plan (Annex B) outlines specific actions to ensure that women's voices support most vulnerable social groups among which women in BIA are specifically recognized. By enabling equal participation of men and women, the project ensures that climate change adaptation is more effective and sustainable.

### 253. **Project implementation**

253.1 At Project inception, additional gender analysis will be undertaken to ensure that the baseline data set on sex- and age-disaggregated and adequate for assessment of the gender impacts of the Project. Progress reports will provide information on the progress made in addressing gender equality issues. The project team will conduct a mid-term review mission will review and monitor the gender impacts. The Gender Action Plan includes a number of recommendations to ensure that the project:

- narrows gender inequality
- avoids any risk of adverse gender impact
- ensures women's participation, promotes their leadership qualities; and
- ensures equal access for all groups including women, girls, men, and boys.

### As part of the implementation of the Gender Action Plan the project will

1. Carry out gender responsive vulnerability assessment informed by gender analysis.
2. Ensure that the project team is composed in such a way as to ensure an equal representation of women, elderly people, disabled or family members of disabled, IDPs, community leaders and government officials.
3. Ensure collection of sex- and age-disaggregated data for project indicators: Gender responsive and sex-disaggregated data will be collected at all levels of the project implementation and will be used through the multi-hazard disaster risk information and knowledge system to be designed by the project.
4. Enhance gender considerations in disaster preparedness activities based on gender analysis: Men and women are equally vulnerable to disasters, but their needs differ significantly. The project will ensure that planning and implementation Community consultation groups with at least 30 percent representation of less represented sex and age groups will be involved in all stages of the project including preparation, response, recovery, occurrence, threats to life, possible impact on livelihoods, houses, crops and livestock and on protecting

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately / page)

The financial management and procurement of this project will follow UNDP financial rules and regulations and the UNDP Procurement Rules and Regulations. The overall guidance is outlined in the financial resources management section of the UNDP Programmes and Operations Policies and Procedures.

### G.3.1 UNDP has comprehensive procurement policies in place as outlined in the "Contracts and Procurement" section of UNDP's Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (P&P). The policies outline formal procurement strategies, including a detailed description of the procurement process for goods and services. For more information on UNDP's P&P, please see <https://www.undp.org/content/undp/en/home/librarypage/policies-and-procedures.html>. Procurement is based on the following principles: Cost Efficiency, Best Value for Money, Fairness, Integrity and Transparency.

The procurement of goods and services will be done in a cost-efficient manner by applying the UNDP's Best Value for Money approach. The selection criteria for vendors will include equality, quality, reliability, and competitiveness. Vendors will be selected on an open, transparent basis to ensure that all interested parties have equal access to information about UNDP procurement opportunities.

### G.3.2 Financial Sustainability

The project will be implemented following the Direct Implementation Modality (DIM) following DIM guidelines.

The project will be audited in accordance with the UNDP audit policy and procedures. An annual audit is scheduled for performance, during the project cycle as per UNDP assurance plan, on the basis of a risk assessment. The audit will be conducted by an independent auditor who has experience working with international accounting standards. A scheduled audit is used to determine whether the funds transferred to the implementation partner are being used in accordance with the work plan. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal control audit.

The government's co-finance covers O&M costs during the 6 years of the project's implementation duration, and the government and HPP partner are committed to cover O&M costs of equipment

# Secretariat's assessment of FP216

## Proposal name:
Scaling up climate-resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme

## Country(ies):
Bosnia and Herzegovina

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

#### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The direct benefits will be extended to 799,445 people, accounting for 25 per cent of the total population, with a focus on the most vulnerable population. Scaling up successful innovative flood risk management practices and implementing a nationwide, impact-based Flood Forecasting and Early Warning Systems (FEWS), which is aimed at improved warning times and reduced losses. | Political instability could pose a risk that requires careful management. |
| Engaging the private sector in climate information services and FRM activities and facilitating the increased use of climate risk information in its operations. GCF investment of US$ 14.4 million will leverage an additional US$ 20.5 million in co-financing from both public and private sectors. The government of Bosnia and Herzegovina will contribute to the project through co-financing. | The project's strong inclination towards scaling up and replicating proposed innovations and knowledge products beyond the project area will require great attention to ensure that these are appropriate, particularly innovative FRM technologies and FEWSs to incorporate the needs and vulnerabilities of communities and institutions outside the scope of the project. |

#### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms and conditions linked in the respective term sheet and addendum XXXI titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

### II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment


---
**Page 126**
---

2.1 Project background

Bosnia and Herzegovina, identified as a middle-income country, has an estimated population of 3.8 million and spans an area of 51,292 km². During the 1992-1995 conflict, the country underwent severe destruction. In subsequent years, it has been navigating a complicated landscape of post-war rebuilding, along with economic and social revitalization.

The conflict left behind a legacy of extensive damage to infrastructure, including roads, bridges, and buildings. This has had long-lasting effects on the population's well-being, inflicting direct harm on flood control infrastructure and hydrological monitoring stations, while also affecting the country’s ability to adapt to climate change. The floods in 2014 and 2015 were particularly catastrophic, with flash floods in Bosnia and Herzegovina being the most severe in Europe that year. These floods are estimated to have cost the country approximately 3 per cent of its gross domestic product.

A quarter of the population of Bosnia and Herzegovina faces a high risk of flooding exacerbated by climate change, which has led to increased rainfall intensity and unpredictability. This has resulted in significant damage to infrastructure such as roads, property, and essential infrastructure. Key economic sectors such as agriculture, water management, and tourism are also severely impacted.

The project is designed to establish an integrated and innovative, gender-responsive, climate-informed approach for flood risk reduction that will be based on the final frameworks for the sustained implementation of comprehensive FRM strategies, which include a blend of structural and non-structural measures. The project will focus on improving the utilization of climate information in decision-making processes at all levels to strengthen the resilience and adaptive capacities of communities at risk.

The initiative is aligned with the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the EU’s Adaptation Strategy, developed in March 2023. The project outlines two outcomes and three outputs:

Outcome 1: Improved flood risk management through a combination of structural and non-structural Risk reduction solutions; and

Output 1: Fully integrated impact-based FRMs facilitates timely preparation and response to floods.

Outcome 2: Vulnerable communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina including women, children, persons with disabilities and older persons are better protected from and less exposed to climate-induced flood risk through a combination of structural and non-structural Risk reduction solutions; and

Output 2: Climate-proof flood protection measures are scaled-up through new investments and improved institutional capacity, thereby strengthening the resilience of the most vulnerable groups to climate-induced flooding.


---
**Page 127**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

The project objective will be achieved through three complementary outputs that comprise the overall project design.

#### Component 1: Climate-informed impact-based FEWS and an increased generation and use of climate data to reduce vulnerability to flood-related disasters (total cost: USD 14.6 million, GCF cost: USD 10.58 million)

The project aims to enhance the hydrometeorological monitoring network by covering a broader array of hydroclimatic variables impacted by climate change. Special focus will be on placing and operating new weather stations in areas with high flood risk. The project will also offer to integrate hydropower plants' hydrometric networks into the national grid, making sustainable use of these data for flood hazard monitoring and management by both the public and private sectors.

This component aims to refine existing hazard and risk models to include a more comprehensive set of variables. Activities will include extending modeling to cover whole basins, including category II tributaries, and improving hydrological modeling. The goal is to create an effective framework for flood forecasting and early warning systems (FEWS) in the region.

The project will incorporate hydropower plant operations into flood hazard and risk modeling as a basis for developing climate-informed FEWS for the hydropower sector, especially focusing on the Neretva and Trebišnjica River Sub-basins.

Building on existing FEWS projects, this work will advance information and communications technology (ICT) infrastructure to support the development of climate-informed FEWS that cover hydro-energy intensive river basins such as Neretva and Trebišnjica, and update existing models for flood hazard and risk modeling. The project will also enhance hydrometeorological forecasting capabilities based on numerical weather prediction models.

To ensure the sustainability of these initiatives, the project will deliver extensive training and capacity-building activities to build the institutional capacity within different agencies to avoid duplication and inefficiencies and establish standard operating procedures (SOPs), including flood risk management plans. The project will also develop operational protocols, protocols and SOPs defining the forecast thresholds for forecast-based financing. The project will also develop operational maintenance of the hydrometric network. It will assess and further develop climate-informed FEWS in coordination with the national adaptation plan project and implement a climate-resilient FIRM in cooperation with the national adaptation plan project.

#### Component 2: Non-structural flood risk reduction measures and nature-based solutions maintained within national policies and plans, and effectively contribute to protection of people and livelihoods from climate change impacts (total cost: USD 10.58 million)

The overall objective of the project is focused on elevating both FIRM and FEWS. This will be accompanied by enhancing existing legal and regulatory frameworks to incorporate climate-responsive FIRM across various sectors, such as agriculture, forestry, hydropower and spatial


---
**Page 128**
---

planning. At the state level, a holistic, cross-entity strategy will be adopted, particularly for shared basins. This integrated approach necessitates the participation of key stakeholders, improved coordination, and the ratification of relevant regional and multilateral environmental agreements. The project will also support the development of flood risk management strategies based on climate-induced floods. Through these activities, local governments will be enabled to demand better planning tools, policies and strategies, grounded in comprehensive impact assessments and cost-benefit analyses.

14. This project component will incorporate community-based ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) solutions into flood risk management strategies for the Neretva River Basin. The project will expand upon the successful EBA methods previously piloted by the UNDP/GEF funded project “Adaptation to Climate Change in the Neretva River Basin” in Bosnia and Herzegovina for its existing torrential flood hazard maps and evaluated cost-effective EBA strategies, will be prioritized for the implementation of high-impact ecological measures. The project will also support the development of a national network of forest roads, initially carried out during wartime for illegal logging. Though current legislation largely prohibits their use, this network has been identified as an important asset that could lead to significant material run-off, affecting river networks usually the mainstream of the River Neretva.

15. The project aims to establish a comprehensive framework in Bosnia and Herzegovina with applying climate-resilient EBA approaches to flood-risk management. It will adapt well into the existing national and international frameworks for climate change adaptation. These insights will be consolidated into countrywide guidelines, technical specifications, standards and practices, as well as capacity building activities. The project will also strengthen institutional capacities in identifying and implementing these EBA approaches to flood risk management.

16. The project will support the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina in transitioning from ad hoc project-based awareness and education to a more systematic and sustainable approach for climate change adaptation. A national framework for flood risk management will be developed and executed. This will include nationwide training programmes focused on flood risk management, as well as capacity building activities for local government and first responders. The project will also formulate a sustainability development plan, enhancing training in FRR and climate risk information within existing educational institutions. This strategy will be supported by the establishment of an inter-ministerial task force with academic institutions, and establish mechanisms for long-term capacity-building, building on the groundswell of interest generated through this project.

Component 3: Climate proof flood protection measures scaled up through new and improved national and local investment frameworks increasing resilience of the most vulnerable groups to climate change impacts in Bosnia and Herzegovina

17. Component 3 aims to bolster the resilience of community livelihoods by amplifying flood protection measures, particularly for the most vulnerable communities. This will be achieved by formulating an Investment Framework designed specifically to reduce and manage climate-induced flood risks, which will involve both public sector and private sector participation in climate change adaptation. The project will support the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina with these financing and risk transfer mechanisms after conducting thorough assessments of socio-economic impacts and vulnerability analysis at the municipal level, as well as through the national insurance sector.

18. Building on the climate-risk-sensitive budgeting mechanism already developed at the municipal level under the existing disaster plan project, this component will explore avenues for integrating such budgeting practices into Bosnia and Herzegovina’s existing public financial management framework. This will leverage current strengths while addressing any bottlenecks


---
**Page 129**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

25. The initiative aims to mitigate flood risks for about 798,445 people, or 25 per cent of the nation's total population, who are currently vulnerable to climate change. By 2050, it is estimated that this number will increase to more than half of the country’s entire population. This project is particularly aimed at safeguarding the most flood-vulnerable populations in Bosnia and Herzegovina, including those living in urban areas.

26. The implementation of enhanced flood risk management is expected to have a high impact, preserving lives and livelihoods for vulnerable communities across three key river basins: Bosna, Neretva, and Una. Each strategy will bolster both community resilience and ecological safeguards within targeted river basins.

27. The project is geared towards fortifying resilience and improving the livelihoods of at-risk demographics, specifically women, children, and senior citizens. Women make up 51.7 per cent of the total population in Bosnia and Herzegovina, while children account for 30.4 per cent and senior citizens represent 13.7 per cent, respectively. Economic analyses suggest that the initiative will yield an estimated net present value (NPV) of USD 289 million, with a benefit-cost ratio of 5:1. These gains seen in the protection of households assets. Geographically, the Bosna and Neretva River Sub-basins stand to benefit the most. The resilience of physical infrastructure across three key river basins will be enhanced through the introduction of flood zoning policies.

28. In addition to local benefits, the project also aims for broader climate resilience through the adaptation of technical solutions in line with the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF) investment framework in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The introduction of flood zoning policies, attuned to the specific vulnerabilities of local communities, aims to reduce their flood risk exposure significantly.


---
**Page 130**
---

29. Multisector consultations have informed the project’s design, with particular attention paid to the unique vulnerabilities of women, ethnic minorities, the persons with disabilities and older persons. The collection of gender-sensitive socioeconomic data will be an ongoing process. The project will build on existing initiatives in Bosnia and Herzegovina's commitments to gender equality and international protocols on gender-responsive disaster risk management. It will also draw from lessons learned and insights from prior initiatives, like the UNDP Wira project, and will include metrics and tools for monitoring progress in gender equality.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to High

The project aims to revolutionize existing flood risk management procedures in Bosnia and Herzegovina by enhancing institutional effectiveness at various levels, disseminating reliable climate information and early warning services widely accessible. It intends to broaden climate risk information for FBM to extend the reach of existing flood risk management systems (FRMS) beyond urban areas into rural communities, thereby enriching the spectrum of hazard, risk and vulnerability information. The innovative introduction of a large-scale, comprehensive, and integrated early warning system will enhance the efficiency of response efforts and potentially reduce disaster losses.

27. This will mark the first instance of a comprehensive, large-scale FFWEIS being implemented in Bosnia and Herzegovina. It is expected that new technologies and digital platforms like Internet of Things and data analytics will be used to empower local communities to undertake their own disaster risk reduction measures. This complements the national strategy for flood risk management and aims at strengthening collaboration with the private sector to create specialized climate services for sectors like hydropower and agriculture, thereby enhancing business continuity planning and private sector engagement.

The plan underlines the importance of knowledge dissemination and collective learning. It will foster a culture of innovation through interdisciplinary coordination and offer policy guidance on flood risk management, driving participatory decision-making processes. The project will also include the creation of training programs on flood risk management for various stakeholders, including decision makers at all levels, to ensure that they have access to best research and development in flood risk management via academic collaborations.

25. The proposal is fully aligned with the United Nations Secretary-General’s EMWAI initiative, which aims to strengthen national disaster risk reduction systems through detection, observation, monitoring, analysis and forecasting; warning dissemination and communications; and preparedness and response capabilities.


### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project is poised to deliver multiple co-benefits including enhanced ecosystem services, gender-responsive livelihood enhancement and job creation, and improved resilience

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale High**

Bosnia and Herzegovina is a middle-income nation with approximately 3.5 million residents. It is still grappling with the devastating effects of the 1992–1995 war, which decimated its human, economic, and social resources. These challenges are exacerbated by an ongoing political crisis, characterized by weak institutions and poor quality of governance. Bosnia and Herzegovina's economy is in a transitional phase, heavily reliant on experts from abroad to address the country’s complex needs. The lack of a single national government and poor financial coordination have stifled economic growth and deterred foreign investment, making the economy one of the least competitive in the region.

The project's interventions offer detailed sector-specific climate risk information, which is critical for effective adaptation strategies and optimization in sectors like hydropower. This targeted approach not only ensures the sustainable development of the hydropower sector but also facilitates a more efficient use of public funds by reducing disaster recovery costs while safeguarding crucial infrastructure like roads and bridges will bolster economic development and contribute to poverty reduction.

The project's gender strategy is specifically designed to benefit vulnerable populations, with a focus on gender equality. By actively involving women – who are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts – the project aims to counter existing gender disparities in employment and social roles in Bosnia and Herzegovina.

Environmentally, the project deploys EBA strategies and nature-based solutions like riparian buffer zones, green roofs, and permeable pavements. These interventions will enhance water retention, regular hydrological flows, mitigate natural hazards like flooding and soil erosion, and improve biodiversity. The proposed investments in hydropower and other interventions will significantly boost the country's environmental resilience to climate-induced flood disasters, thereby adding in the protection of valuable natural assets such as land and forest.

The Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS) identifies five priority sectors for national adaptation planning: water resources management, agriculture, human health, energy, and the economy. The potential benefit-cost ratio of return on investment could be up to 500:1 for flood-prone areas and 2300:1 for heat waves in urban centers (Tilgenkeller and Weber, 2008). The proposed project would provide a more comprehensive understanding of climate risks and linkages to the other hazards of the GFCS priority sectors. The proposal would benefit from a closer integration with the GFCS framework and the development of an early warning system.

The proposal could benefit from more detailed elaboration of how these interventions could lead to better decision-making in terms of risk management, disaster preparedness, and forecasting. It is recommended that UNDP should consider laying the foundation for the establishment of a national climate service center (NCSC) in the relevant GFCS priority sectors through the GFCS user interface platform. We request the national hazards forecasting system could follow overall value chain of risk information sectoral risk management.

### Country ownership

**Scale: High**

The project demonstrates a strong national ownership and has established the appropriate/relevant systems and processes (political, institutional and legal frameworks) for implementing its commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The country has demonstrated political will to implement the Paris Agreement, for the period until 2030. In October 2015, the development of its nationally determined contribution (NDC) was completed and submitted to the UNFCCC. This contribution provides guidance on the country’s climate change adaptation and contribution to climate change mitigation measures. The NDC includes a sectoral approach for addressing sectors being agriculture and water resource management. It outlines the role of detailed planning of short, medium and long-term adaptation measures, development of climate risk financing mechanisms, identification of key stakeholders, capacity building, institutional strengthening, and modeling of climate risk. The proposal is also aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly target 13 and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (in particular target G).

The proposal is based on these national strategies and the application of international best practices. It includes a strong commitment from all relevant government and local institution co-financing commitments. Co-funding support for the project will be provided by the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development Investment Bank (EBRD) in the amount of USD 21.5 million for structural measures and additionally through the EBRD’s Climate Change Adaptation Program (CCAP). The other relevant authorities composing the country – the Governments of the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina and Republic of Srpska, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, the GfM of structural measures, hydrometric equipment, and modelling and FFRS.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

**4.1 Project background:** The project will upgrade and expand the coverage of the hydrometric network and develop an integrated impact-based centralized and community-level flood forecasting and early warning system. The project will also implement structural and non-structural measures to improve resilience against flooding, droughts, and heatwaves while building institutional capacity for flood risk management. In addition, it proposes to scale up flood protection interventions in the project area by 2035.

**4.1 Environmental and social safeguard category:** The confirmed and assigned environmental and social safeguards are consistent with the GCF’s environmental and social policies at or above the accreditation level of the accredited entity (AE). This is rationalized by structural and non-structural flood reduction interventions that will be promoted by the project and are likely to have more significant positive impacts on the environment than negative ones. The project will be managed with mitigation measures. Activity 2.2 non-structural measures include gabion walls, terraces, and check dams. Structural measures include construction of embankments and other measures like rehabilitation of river embankments, and canals and establishing dykes.

**4.1 Additional considerations:** The project is consistent with the GCF’s environmental and social policies in terms of the coverage of the hydrometric system and capacity-building such as training and development of gabion walls, terraces, check dams, and other structural measures.

#### Safeguard instruments

The AE provided an environmental and social assessment report (ESAR) and environmental and social management framework (ESMF) with the finding that the project is consistent with GCF’s environmental and social policies. The ESAR provides a description of the national content for environmental and social risk assessment, potential environmental and social risks likely to be associated with the project activities, including medium risk site-specific


---
**Page 134**

### Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards (ISS) standards.

#### ESS 1) Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The ESAR was prepared based on the environmental and social risks identified through the project implementation, including the identification of the significance level of each risk that was identified under each activity. The ESAR provides an overview of the environmental and social assessment undertaken for the project to manage risks and impacts and the site-specific environmental and social assessments and management measures proposed in the ESAR. For example, the flood protection measures, for which outlines of the documents are annexed into the ESAR and ESMP. The main outcomes of the environmental and social assessment is also presented in the ESAR and ESMP.

#### ESS 2) Labour and working conditions

There is a risk that work sites may flood during the implementation of flood protection measures. This will be mitigated by timing the works appropriately and monitoring of upstream rainfall and river levels prior and during the works. Construction machinery and vehicles will not be used on roads or bridges until they are in compliance with relevant national legislation and in compliance with UNDP Social and Environmental Standard 7. Appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) will be provided to personnel who need it.

#### ESS 4) Community health, safety and security

The project is expected to involve minor construction activities that may have an impact on the hydrometric network. Other construction-related risks include noise and dust from heavy construction machinery and haul trucks. Some of the proposed mitigation measures are sourcing alternative routes for haul trucks, limiting access to the site during periods of extreme dust and restricting use of machinery and vehicles to normal working hours during the day.


---
**Page 135**
---

Additionally, there is the possibility of mines and unexploded ordnance (UXO) disturbing the earthworks for which the project will update the magnetometry survey to confirm the presence or absence of UXO and the contractor will be required to detail procedures to safely remove any UXO found.

Conflict sensitivity analysis is included in the ESAR and ESMF which assesses the conflict dynamics in the country and the project activities' potential to cause conflict. Most risks are classified as low, but there may be a risk of displacement due to physical interventions occurring or being exacerbated due to the project interventions is slight. Recommended conflict mitigation measures include stakeholder engagement and the use of governance mechanisms available for the project.

### ESS 5 Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.
Activities involving physical interventions will occur in areas where people are living, which may activate their ability to move or access resources. The AE will be required to ensure that when this standard is met, the project will undertake a comprehensive environmental and social assessments and management plans whether economic displacement is temporary or permanent. This will include an assessment of the potential for adverse impacts on internally displaced persons who may be more vulnerable. The project will not result in physical displacement. Sites for physical interventions have been selected only where land tenure is clear.

### ESS 6 Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources.
The project will cause habitat loss due to earthworks, which could potentially cause habitat loss and disturbance of fauna. This will be mitigated by revegetation where vegetation is removed by the project, avoiding in-stream works during the growing season, and ensuring that areas disturbed by construction activities are restored will be revegetated using native and locally endemic species that have high habitat value. Native and locally endemic tree species will be planted on land disturbed by earthworks. A biodiversity action plan may be prepared for the Krupa River site as case there likely are impacts.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7 Indigenous Peoples.
The AE confirmed there were no Indigenous Peoples or internally displaced persons affected by the project and that the ESAR and ESMF had been completed in accordance with the GCF IP policy. The same conclusion, however, had come to the same conclusion. Accordingly, the AE concluded that the Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP) does not apply.

### ESS 8 Cultural heritage.
Screening for the project indicated that cultural physical resources were unlikely to be at risk as a result of the project's implementation. Nonetheless, there is potential for adverse impacts on cultural heritage due to earthworks and construction works. A chance finds procedure is answered into the ESAR and ESMF for this eventuality.

### Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding.
The AE has reviewed the project’s SEAH policy and procedures in relation to implementing new ecosystem-based flood risk reduction and climate change adaptation methods. The project will ensure that all contractors and suppliers are aware of the project's SEAH policy and procedures for reporting incidents. The project's ESAR and ESMF has triggered GCF relevant policies, safeguard standards and programming principles. The AE has reviewed the project’s SEAH Action Plan to address the potential risks and impacts of SEAH and will require the project's contractors, suppliers and other parties involved in the project for the project to complete a pre-employment screening with those who cannot conduct and agree to taking adequate action if faced with SEAH allegations. As part of the project’s SEAH Action Plan, the project will ensure the availability of confidential reporting channels and safe and ethical documentation of SEAH cases. The project will map out SEAH prevention and response actors in the project's adjoining communities, develop
