### Gender policy

#### 4.2 Gender policy

The GCF has provided a gender assessment and Gender Action Plan therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF’s Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.

The gender assessment describes the enabling environment and gender issues at the national level, including the availability of supportive legislation and policies to promote gender equality. It also identifies gendered challenges and barriers that leave women more vulnerable to climate-related disasters. Despite a strong policy


---
**Page 137**
---

and legal framework supporting gender equality and women are still largely excluded from political and decision-making processes; they have disproportionately limited access to employment and income, and many are subject to violence. There is particular concern with respect to Roma women in Serbia, Kosovo, Montenegro, North Macedonia, Albania, and Roma women in Bosnia and Herzegovina, who are even more severely disadvantaged and marginalized than other women in the region. The lack of gender equality and women's gender equality remains a serious challenge. The greatest obstacles to broader development and empowerment of women in rural areas are related to property rights, access to public services, finance, land tenure, and social norms and practices that restrict women from moving outside of their homes. Limited opportunities for employment and self-employment are perpetuated by the lack of infrastructure, such as roads, electricity, water supply, and access to information and training. A lack of affordable childcare and pre-schools in rural and smaller settlements also hinders women's participation in the labour market and contributes to negative economic consequences, and consequently, employment. There is also a limited number of women associations and cooperatives that provide support for women entrepreneurs and small-scale farmers. Moreover, discrimination against Roma women is evident from the level of their formal employment, which is lower than expected given their educational attainment relative to education. Every second woman has experienced some form of gender-based violence from the age of 15, while every fifth woman has most likely experienced this over the past 12 months. There are no national or local laws that specifically address gender-based violence and based on experiences following the floods in 2014, the Republica Srpska Gender Centre has developed a programme to address this challenge.
st. The Act has provided a programme-level Gender Action Plan, which includes activities, indicators, targets, timeframe, and gender expertise to ensure that gender issues are incorporated into the project and GAP activities. The gender analysis will be based on secondary data sources such as national statistics, representative socioeconomic data collection, and ensure women's participation and decision-making as well as information on the representation of women in the project implementation team. The project will ensure at least 30 per cent representation of women on the steering committee, as well as in national and local and national decision-making bodies established or facilitated by the project. The project will also ensure that women are actively involved in all stages of the consultations and decision-making. In developing an investment framework for climate-induced flood risk reduction and management, the project will include representatives from the gender mechanism and other stakeholders to ensure that women's needs and interests are taken into account. ### 4.3.2 Programme-specific execution risks (medium-high risk)

72. **Complex governance structure:** The AE has identified several governance-related risks in the funding proposal. The funded activities include a number of policy and regulation updates, which are subject to approval by the two entities responsible for land use zoning policy and regulation. The governments of the two entities need to confirm their acceptance of the policy and regulatory changes as well as the implementation of the changes. This may lead to delays in the current regulations in the field of insurance. The relevant legal and regulatory provisions will be updated, which may also affect the existing insurance financing instruments. The fragmented institutional structure of the governments and a lack of political consensus on the proposed changes may result in delays or even rejection of the updates/changes. The AE will leverage the existing relationship and relevant experience from past projects to mitigate such risks.

73. **GCF co-financing risk:** The rate of GEF funding to co-financing is 1:4. The sizable co-financing is critical for the funded activities, especially for activities that are implemented through public-private partnerships (PPPs). Any delay in the disbursement or reduction of co-financing will hamper project implementation. To mitigate this risk, it is recommended that the term sheet include a proportionate disbursement between GEF and co-financing partners. The AE has already secured 50% of the co-financing for this year. However, the details for disbursement and how to address the shortfall in co-financing will be further confirmed during the project preparation phase.

74. As part of risk financing and as a risk transfer measure, the project will provide technical assistance and training to financial institutions for the development of lending products and the use of different types of guarantees. The AE has already provided such support to commercial enterprises in Bosnia and Herzegovina. However, technical assistance and training will not automatically translate into an increase in the number of loans granted by or guaranteed by the financial institutions. A specific target or indicator to monitor the number of loans to be provided and/or engagement of financial institutions will enhance the impact of the funding proposal.

## UNDP confirmed that it has conducted a risk assessment in relation to prohibited practices risks. The probability of occurrence of a prohibited practice event has been assessed as low, whereas the impact severity of occurrence has been assessed as medium.

### UNDP stated that it applies a zero-tolerance policy in relation to fraud and corruption. The guidelines for preventing and detecting acts of fraud and corruption have been laid out in the UNDP Policy Against Fraud and Corrupt Practices (FCP Policy). The FCP Policy is applicable across all UNDP programmes and projects. The fundamental principles of UNDP’s FCP Policy serve as the basis for and are integrated into the policies, procedures, guidelines, financial management, internal control and accountability, and staff rules and regulations.

#### Under the terms of the FCP Policy and related policies, UNDP has a commitment when developing and implementing programmes/projects that all prohibited practices are fully identified and considered in the programme/project design and processes and that all potential risks associated with prohibited practices are mitigated. The processes and requirements for UNDP’s PCAT require UNDP to carry out a thorough due diligence of all entities that will be contracted by or partner with UNDP, as well as in relation to the potential partners who will be involved in the implementation of such programmes/projects.

##### Capacity assessments include, among other things, a screening of all entities considered to be contracted by or partner with UNDP against the Consolidated List (to which UNDP is also beholden) and an assessment of any history of fraud, corruption or other fraudulent practices and/or potential conflicts of interest. Based on this capacity assessment, UNDP then builds appropriate safeguards into its contracts and/or agreements. UNDP also ensures that its financial management contains adequate safeguards to prevent, monitor and address any risks associated with prohibited practices, including the risk of money laundering or terrorist financing.

#### UNDP confirmed that the project has developed a grievance redress mechanism to deal with any complaints or grievances that may arise because of the project. The GRM has been designed to ensure that an individual and/or group are not financially impacted by the process of making a complaint, including in relation to the implementation of projects in Bosnia and Herzegovina and UNDP. Furthermore, the GMR allows individuals and/or groups to also be involved in the monitoring and evaluation of the project. The Secretariat has determined that the GCF independent Redress Mechanism should have any concerns as to corruption, unethical behaviour or where they believe their complaint or grievance has not been adequately addressed.

##### Compliance risk rating: The Secretariat has conducted a review of the project in accordance with the GCF’s compliance risk matrix. The Secretariat has determined no issue or deviation with respect to compliance issues. Based on available information for this funding proposal, the Secretariat has determined a compliance risk rating of medium and has no objection to this proposal.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

The UNDP will be the AE and the EF of the project. It will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and project confirmation to be agreed with GCF.

The project will be implemented through a financial arrangement established under the Standard Basic Assistance Agreement between UNDP and the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina, as well as the rules and procedures outlined in the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures.

The project will be audited following the UNDP financial rules and regulations noted under Section 4.3.1 above. The audit will be conducted by an external auditor according to the current audit policies. UNDP will appoint the auditors. Scheduled audits will be performed annually, with a view to ensuring that the project is being implemented in accordance with implementing partner’s risk rating and UNDP’s guidelines. The project’s financial statements will comply with international accounting standards.

As of 7 pm on 2 October 2023, there remains a divergence of views between the GCF Secretariat and the AE regarding the co-financing covenant for the project, which accounts for more than 80% of total funding; therefore clear mechanism and undertaking by the AE on how this will be managed is still outstanding. The AE has not yet been able to reach its targets and implementation plan. Despite numerous rounds of reviews, the AE has not yet been amenable to including a covenant on co-financing. Given the GCF’s co-financing ratio of 1:4, the Secretariat has proposed that the AE should contribute 25% of total project costs towards co-financing. However, since no agreement on the co-financing covenant has been reached, we have to refer relevant information to the Board for further consideration and decision-making. The GCF Secretariat is set out a mechanism for co-financing to be contributed by all co-financers listed in the FP. We propose to include the proportionality of the GCF proceeds disbursed (per Annex 1) outlined in the FP. We propose to include the proportionality of the GCF proceeds disbursed at each year with the proceeds of co-financing, inline with the Budget.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

The original AMA was signed with the Accredited Entity on 5 August 2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016. The Accredited Entity’s first term of accreditation expired on 23 November 2018. The second term of accreditation commenced in April 2019 and is due to expire on 30 June 2021.

The project proposal will be implemented in Bosnia and Herzegovina, a country in which GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation. Therefore, the Secretariat recommends that the GCF be provided with the relevant privileges and immunities. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed about this matter.

### 4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board;

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP216

## Proposal name:
Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme

## Country(ies):
Bosnia and Herzegovina

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Overview

In its assessment, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) is guided by decision B33/12 adopted by the Board, which explains the principles for demonstrating the impact of climate change on a country and provides guidance to the ITAP in developing an assessment guide in considering these investment criteria and as endorsed by the Board.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has assessed the scientific literature related to climate change impacts. The findings from this review have reached several important conclusions. According to its findings, the country (and the wider region) is susceptible to a variety of climate-related risks, including a rise in sea levels.

One of the major impacts of climate change identified by the IPCC in Bosnia and Herzegovina is the alteration of precipitation patterns. It is projected that the country will experience more frequent heavy rainfall events with increased intensity and frequency of extreme weather events, such as heavy rainfalls and floods. This alteration in precipitation patterns poses a significant threat to water-related issues, including water scarcity, droughts and potential threats to agriculture.

Furthermore, rising temperatures are identified as a significant concern for Bosnia and Herzegovina. The country is projected to experience more frequent heatwaves with increased frequency and prolonged duration. This rise in temperature could have detrimental effects on the economy and public health, notably increasing the risk of forest fires and heat-related illnesses.

As such, recent research has indicated that climate change will likely exacerbate flood risks in Bosnia and Herzegovina through a combination of factors including more frequent heavy rainfall events with increased snowmelt, subsequently overwhelming drainage systems and increasing river flow. As Bosnia and Herzegovina is already prone to flooding due to its mountainous terrain and complex hydrological system, the country faces significant challenges from climate change. Increased frequency and intensity of floods may damage road bridges and buildings, resulting in substantial economic losses.

*Note: Website link provided for further reading.*

### Impact potential

#### Scale: Medium

a. The funding proposal highlights various anticipated adaptation impacts resulting from the proposed project across various GCF adaptation results areas. Specifically, the funding proposal references two main project outcomes:

(i) **Outcome 1:** Improved flood forecasting and more responsive impact-based Flood Forecasting and Early Warning System and increased generation and use of climate data to reduce vulnerability in Bosnia and Herzegovina.

(ii) **Outcome 2:** Vulnerable communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina, including women, children, people with disabilities, and older persons, are better protected from and less exposed to the adverse effects of floods through a combination of structural and non-structural flood-risk reduction solutions.


---
**Page 147**
---

### The funding proposal contains a detailed description of the methodology adopted for the flood risk assessment exercise. This involved scenario modeling and mapping and cost-benefit options appraisal that forms the basis of the calculation of these anticipated adaptation benefits. The results are presented in Annexes A to D.

#### Results of the flood modeling exercise need to be interpreted with caution. First, the TAP model is somewhat challenging to use for this type of analysis. Second, the baseline scenario used in the vulnerability study (Annex 2) refers to the calibration and validation of the underlying flood model but does not necessarily reflect the actual historical flood events that occurred during the period covered by the study and thus the confidence in the extent of future floods. Second, the estimated adaptation benefits are based on a simplified approach for the calculation of the reduction in flood risk in the Drina and Vrbas River Basins that was conducted as part of the baseline project. In the absence of detailed hazard information, this approach assumes that the climate change scenarios and impact of proposed land use changes will be similar to those observed during the period covered by the baseline project across five river basins (assuming that the climate change scenarios and impact of proposed land use changes are similar in Serbia, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Montenegro, Kosovo, and Treblijica). While this may well be a reasonable assumption, the approach taken is overly simplistic and thus the results cannot be accepted with certainty without consideration of alternative assumptions. Some insights related to land cover dynamics, among others, related to these other river basins


---
**Page 148**
---

13. Another methodological challenge is the choice of the Representative Proportionate Pathway (RCP) scenario(s) that will be utilized as the base scenario to inform the design of the proposed project interventions (as highlighted for example by the response of the accredited entity (AE) in Q&A 20). It would have been prudent also to model the design of project interventions based on alternative, and equally plausible RCP scenarios. The RCP 4.5 scenario is likely to be more relevant than the RCP 8.5 scenario for example (intense precipitation resulting in floods and landslides, and extreme heat waves). These climate change impacts are likely to have more significant negative impact on livelihoods and business operations over time and may require different interventions to support adaptation. The RCP 8.5 scenario (as a “high” baseline) is likely to be the most relevant for the project area as it represents an event triggered by much more significant temperature increases towards the end of the century (akin to floods, heat waves, droughts, and extreme precipitation events). The RCP 8.5 scenario may result in higher costs for the adaptation measures at the expense of other development priorities. This is a risk that needs to be managed through engagement among stakeholders (e.g., communities, businesses, insurance) that the project interventions will deliver or support. It also represents an event that could lead to maladaptation if it is not recognized as the event of catastrophic failure. This carries the risk of maladaptation. The ITAP recognizes the fact that the various scenarios track each other in terms of climate change impact in the medium term, and therefore, the project interventions should be designed with this in mind. However, and seeks to achieve compliance with the region’s acquiescent communicative – requires application of the RCP 4.5 scenario as a baseline for the design of the project interventions. The anticipated impact proposed project interventions will be considered under alternative climate change scenarios (e.g., RCP 8.5) in the context of the project’s risk management plan.

14. A major concern when evaluating the expected adaptation impacts of the proposed project relates to the lack of recent local stakeholder involvement in its design. Experience with similar projects has shown that the effectiveness of climate change adaptation approaches greatly depend on effective stakeholder consultation, and integration of different views from stakeholders. The ITAP recognizes this fact and has engaged in extensive stakeholder consultations in 2018 and 2019 (21 bilateral meetings with individuals, including experts, government officials, and representatives of civil society organizations). No further consultations have been conducted. In the Q&A, the AE stated that between 2019 and 2023 consultations with local stakeholders have confirmed via two UNDP-implemented projects (the GEF-funded project in Bosnia and Herzegovina and the Climate Change Adaptation Investment Planning in Climate sensitive sectors in BiH) and the Joint Swiss United Nations Development Programmes (UNDP) funded project in Serbia. No summaries of these consultations have been made available. There is also no indication of how any of this information has influenced the design of the proposed project interventions. In the ITAP view, this presents a significant risk to the project. Thus, the ITAP recommends that the AE should submit a summary of all stakeholder consultations conducted which would require the AE to conduct additional consultations covering the entire project region regarding the design of the project and how the local entity, national and regional institutions might be involved in its implementation. The AE should also provide an overview of the Secretariat’s results. The AE should submit a summary of the outcomes to the Secretariat (proposed conditions for approval).

15. Likely the most significant challenge for implementation and thus the achievement of the anticipated adaptation impacts is related to the political and institutional complexity of the government structure in Bosnia and Herzegovina. This country has two major ethnic divisions (the countries consists of two largely autonomous entities, Republika Srpska and the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina). These divisions are characterized by: i) strong ethnic lines, with separate government structures and decision-making processes); ii) complicated decision-making processes (for the governance structure of Bosnia and Herzegovina


---
**Page 149**
---

involves multiple layers of authorities, including a central government, entity governments, cantonal governments and municipal governments); iii) veto and blocking mechanisms (entities and ethnic groups have the power to veto decisions that go against their interests, resulting in delays or even complete failure of projects); and iv) a lack of fiscal autonomy (the entities in Bosnia and Herzegovina are heavily reliant on financial transfers from the central government for their revenues and expenditure and dependence). The funding proposal references these challenges, refers to UNDP's experience in entering into agreements with the relevant authorities in Bosnia and Herzegovina, and makes commitments that various Bosnia and Herzegovina entities have made as evidence that the project will have significant government buy-in and support. This highlights significant interest in the project from the relevant authorities and a willingness to engage in meaningful engagement.

In its ITAF view, however, the most effective mechanism to ensure that the project delivers sustainable results in this complex environment would be to implement the proposed project interventions in close partnership with the relevant entities and other institutions in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The ITAF notes that while the funding proposal is consistent with the approach by the AE, however, does not facilitate such extensive ownership of project implementation by relevant authorities as it could. In addition, the ITAF notes that the proposed structure of the funding proposal makes only limited reference to how the AE will involve institutions of Bosnia and Herzegovina in the project's implementation. The ITAF recommends that the Project Management Unit will be hosted by the UNDP Country Office rather than a partner institution. (In its working paper, the ITAF notes that the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of Republika Srpska is responsible for the implementation of the structural measures funded by the European Investment Bank (EIB) will be based at the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Management of Republika Srpska.) In its ITAF view, however, this structure does not fully address the potential responsibility within UNDP presents a material risk for the project and may undermine its potential to deliver sustainable results. To mitigate these risks, the ITAF recommends that the Board adopt a second condition (condition 2), to be fulfilled before commencing implementation of the project. The second condition would require the Board to update the sustainability plan, built on the updated stakeholder consultations, so as to outline the various roles and responsibilities of relevant authorities and other institutions in the production and maintenance of project results (see below for more information).

15. Finally, under output 3 the funding proposal suggests implementing activity 3.1: "Developing a climate risk management strategy and tools". The ITAF notes that this delivery includes climate risk management including provisions for private sector engagement in climate risk financing." The delivery is consistent with the AE's approach to developing a climate risk management strategy and tools to be developed by consultants. It remains unclear which partners the AE will work with on the development of these deliverables, and what the AE will ensure that these additional activities are implemented as part of the project. To mitigate this risk, the project proposes to improve the enabling environment (for example, for products related to national climate risk management strategy and tools) by engaging with relevant stakeholders and relevant regulators and government agencies, but where specific information on how such engagement is planned remains unclear. The ITAF recommends that the Board consider public-private partnerships being established and public-private sector climate risk financing mechanisms being developed as part of this activity (the AE should identify who will be involved (aside from the co-financer) and who would ultimately utilize the various products and tools developed). In its implementation plan and reporting to GCF, the AE should provide further clarification on how these activities are planned for delivery and not just result in studies and tools that do not find practical application (recommendation).

16. Overall, the ITAF rates the impact potential for the project as medium, with potential for improvement if the issues highlighted above are adequately addressed.

**Page 150**

1.3 Paradigm shift potential

The project aims to transform the current flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina (BiH) in response to climate change by bolstering institutional capabilities at all levels, enhancing comprehension of risk through improved climate information, incorporating non-structural ecosystem-based solutions, involving the private sector, and ensuring alignment with public and private investments in flood risk management that are subject to climate impacts. The project anticipates achieving a paradigm shift in flood risk management on five fronts:

(a) Introduction of technological innovations (implementation of fully integrated impact based Flood Forecasting and Early Warning Systems based on state-of-the-art observations and the use of advanced numerical modeling and prediction technologies);

(b) Capacity development and enhancement (capacity to identify, plan and implement long-term flood risk management solutions in BiH);

(c) Mainstreaming nature-based flood risk management solutions in Bosnia and Herzegovina (encompassing EBA approaches as well as non-structural solutions);

(d) Development of long-term climate risk financing (including the development of municipal-level disaster insurance schemes);

(e) Introduction of innovative approaches to flood risk management (including forecast-based financing approaches).

The project is expected to leverage BiH’s potential to substantially improve current flood risk management practices in Bosnia and Herzegovina. For a comprehensive paradigm shift to occur, however, it will be necessary for the various partners of BiH to demonstrate their commitment to the various commitments that relevant partners of Bosnia and Herzegovina have made with regard to, for example, financing long-term operation and maintenance (LROM) expenses for the hydrometeorological infrastructure. The project is expected to provide a demonstration of commitment. Many of the co-financing commitments included in the funding proposal are subject to the completion of certain conditions and/or milestones and are contingent and rescheduled to GCF; prior to project commencement (recommendation).

In the Q&A, the AE indicates that it will take a comprehensive approach to ensuring reliability of flood risk management solutions. The AE also provides an approach as how the IFAP believes is crucial. The funding proposal, however, lacks clarity in terms of how the project will ensure that the various activities it undertakes and that will require sustained attention will be completed on time and within budget and will have a positive impact for the country. The AE should generate a detailed sustainability/handover plan, specifying which institutions it will engage and work with to ensure “institutionalized operationalization” (e.g., how the AE will ensure that the project’s activities are institutionalized, including specific tasks such as developing standard operating procedures) and continuity of the various project components. The sustainability/handover plan should be completed and submitted to the Secretariat prior to the second disbursement. This should include all project activities. Thus, the plan to be submitted to the Secretariat should

## Social co-benefits: In the discussion of the Theory of Change (section B.2.a) for the project, it is stated that the project will have social benefits that the project is expected to engender. The improved health and reduced health risks of the project's beneficiaries are considered a social benefit. This includes reduced mortality related injuries and exposure to waterborne diseases. In general, the TAF agrees that such health-related co-benefits can be expected from the project. However, they have not been implemented and sustained over time. However, no specific data on the project region is presented in this proposal to support this supposition; it is not possible to quantify these potential benefits.

### Gender-sensitive development co-benefit: The funding proposal includes an evidence-based gender assessment (annex 6), the results of which have been incorporated into the TAF. These results are used as a basis for specific proposed project activities. For each activity, the funding proposal highlights gender mainstreaming measures and/or gender equality considerations by incorporating analysis on existing inequalities into project design across all levels.

### Overall, the TAF rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as medium-high.
## Needs of the recipient: Scale: Medium-High

Following the war (1992–1995), Bosnia and Herzegovina faced several economic crises. The conflict had devastating effects on the country's economy, leading to large destructions of infrastructure, the collapse of industries, and the displacement of the country's population. In this post-war context, the country has been working towards recovery and rehabilitation since 1995. The TAF rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as medium-high.

The TAF rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as medium-high.


---
**Page 153**
---

Bosnia and Herzegovina’s economy, with increasing risk of floods and associated damage being just one manifestation of how climate change may impact the country negatively in the years and decades to come. From that standpoint, the assistance potentially made available by GCF for this project is timely and relevant as it aims at addressing the country’s growing vulnerabilities to the impacts of climate change, specifically in the context of increasing flood risks.

The IAIP did not include, as part of the funding proposal, an analysis of alternative sources of financing that might potentially be available. The TAP whether acquired through other EU funds or through a separate loan facility, would provide Bosnia and Herzegovina with an accession candidate and does receive support from the regional block to achieve compliance with the EU’s acquis in the areas of water management and flood risk management and management of flood risks (which compels EU member States and candidate countries to apply the NAP). The TAP is therefore a good example of how Bosnia and Herzegovina tries to satisfy its status as a potential EU accession country. The AE responded that the EU already makes assistance available to the project via the EIB’s concessional financing facility, which would be used in conjunction with the proposed funding under the Instrument for Pre-accession Assistance.

The reliance on external financing aid and since the end of the Bosnian conflict has also created a dependency that the country must reduce to achieve sustainable economic growth. While the proposed project has the potential to significantly improve flood risk management, it is not clear how this will be achieved in the context of the Instrument for Pre-accession Assistance Modality carries the risk of further aggravating such dependency if the relevant entities and other responsible parties do not have the capacity to manage the project’s “soft” and “hard” flood risk management infrastructure that is being funded by the project. As noted earlier, Bosnia and Herzegovina has a long history of reliance on external financing within the country during project implementation and the lack of a proper handover (or exit) plan is concerning in this TAP.

Given the above, TAP rates the potential for the project to serve the needs of the recipients as medium-high.


### 1.6 Country ownership

Coherence with existing policies: The proposed project is well aligned with national climate change strategies and plans of Bosnia and Herzegovina. Specifically, the funding proposal references a number of commitments made in the 2015-2020 that specifically references the need for strengthening adaptive capacity in the context of climate change-induced flood risks. The funding proposal also refers to the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (CCAS) adopted by the country’s Council of Ministers in 2013 (updated in 2020) that highlights increasing flood risks and associated damage as one of the main challenges facing Bosnia and Herzegovina at the time of the funding proposal. Finally, the funding proposal devotes itself to the NAP concluded in 2021 and that specifically addresses climate change adaptation measures for improving flood risk management in response to increasing climate change hazards. The IAIP would also like to highlight that the country has a number of other commitments made with international institutions in Bosnia and Herzegovina which signify buy-in and ownership (even though many of these commitments, as previously pointed out, are now dated and will have to be renewed).


---
**Page 154**
---

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scope: Medium

The funding proposal highlights the following three aspects to demonstrate the economic soundness of the project:

(a) A significant share of co-financing is from other public and private sector sources (GCF funding represents only 19.8 per cent of the estimated project cost).

(b) The results of the economic analysis show an economic internal rate of return of 19.3 per cent, which indicates that the proposed project interventions are economically efficient (with the exception of one), and a cost-benefit ratio of 2:1.

(c) A cost of USD 0.50 per direct beneficiary for climate-sensitive flood risk protection and early warning systems is considered to be "comparable" to other projects of similar nature.

Overall, the ITAP agrees that the proposed project performs satisfactorily with regard to the principles of economic soundness and efficiency. However, the ITAP would like to make the following observations:

Regarding the co-financing share, the ITAP commends the large share of co-financing committed to this project from other sources, particularly from Bosnia and Herzegovina's partner. This signifies buy-in and ownership. The ITAP notes that the funding commitment is equivalent to a grant of approximately USD 21.2 million at the exchange rate at time of signing (the actual amount will be determined by the final disbursement in the grant form). The ITAP notes that none of the interventions co-financed by GCF will result in any ongoing financial obligations for Bosnia and Herzegovina, as they are technical assistance projects. Furthermore, the government of Bosnia and Herzegovina is not in a state to raise additional external debt at this time. Bosnia and Herzegovina (with a 2023 gross domestic product per capita of EUR 7,566 (projected)) is considered an upper-middle-income country by the World Bank, and it is part of the International Monetary Fund's Low-Income Country Status.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP216)

## Proposal name:
Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina

## Accredited entity:

### Impact potential
Paragraphs 12-13: UNDP acknowledges the ITAP’s observations regarding the methodology for the flood risk vulnerability modeling and mapping that forms the basis of the calculation of the anticipated adaptation impacts.

We are confident in the rationale behind the choice of scenario RCP8.5 for the estimation of impact of future climate change on flood risk, as this scenario represents a high level of increased frequency and intensity of flooding with the highest flood risk, highest flood levels and highest flood duration. The results from the modelled 100-year floods under RCP8.5 are likely to result in substantial damage to infrastructure, agriculture, and communities.

The project’s approach to adapting to these projections of future flooding risks is to invest in flood defences and other adaptation measures as described below.

The modelled 100-year floods (using RCP8.5) levels for Vrbas basin were found to be similar to the flood levels observed in the 2014 flood event. Given that the 2014 flood impacted all target communities, we are confident that the results from this scenario can be extrapolated the RCP8.5 flood impact modelled in Vrbas basin, to other basins. We are therefore confident of the accuracy and reliability of these projections for use as a future climate change scenario for the assessment of flood risk (identification of population and assets at risk), but also for the identification of adaptation measures that will reduce or mitigate the impact of these adaptation measures. As noted by ITAP, this approach also follows the EU flood directive and was requested by stakeholders who they had already in the present day experienced flooding events.

It is important to note that since RCP8.5 gives the highest flood levels (of those tested) and that we expect it to produce the greatest level of damage, the protection measures implemented under this scenario, the protection measures are expected to provide protection under both RCP3 and RCP4.5.

Whilst flooding under moderate (RCP4.5) and intermediate (RCP8.) climate change scenarios are likely to result in smaller magnitude, lower intensity events floods, they may occur more frequently and with greater frequency than those experienced today. This will require more response time for authorities and communities to prepare, respond, recover, and respond. These types of events are likely to be less disruptive but still costly. For these types of flood events, sustainable land use (through flood zoning, flood resilient agri., and forestry etc.), early warning systems (including CEMS) and capacity building in terms of preparedness and response will be most appropriate.

The project is currently implementing a number of activities that are aimed at reducing the economic impact and management of smaller more frequent floods), awareness raising and capacity building are most appropriate and are being implemented by the project. Their

# Design and Implementation

During year 1 and year 2 of implementation, the WBF hazard maps for all basins will be evaluated in terms of their accuracy and completeness using mapping techniques. Based on the evaluation results, all relevant climate change scenarios will be considered when using calibrated hydraulic models for all basins; the results from which will be used to inform detailed design and implementation of flood risk reduction measures. The hydrological modeling will include RCP4.5 in the hydrological simulations, but unfortunately RCP6 is not available for this project. This is because RCP6 is only available at a coarse resolution (10 km) and is therefore unsuitable for the hydrological modeling. For the reasons noted above, RCP5 is likely to remain most relevant for the design and implementation of protection measures, while the lower impact RCP4.5 scenario will be used as a reference.

Paragraph 12: UNDP fully agrees that regular and effective stakeholder consultations and engagement are critically important for the success and sustainability of the flood risk reduction measures in the project area. This has been achieved through continuous consultations with the institutional stakeholders and local communities which have been ongoing throughout project preparation. Most recently, all institutions involved (the National Flood Protection Agency, Ministry of Environment, water agencies, civil protection unit) were consulted through the GCF NDA in the process of securing funding for this project. Consultations with the local communities to be targeted by this project were carried out during the project design and construction monitoring in 2021-2022 via two UNDP implemented projects on the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the GCF.

As noted above, UNDP agrees with the TAP suggestions to reinforce stakeholder and community consultation processes, which are planned during the start of the project. The national flood protection agency will be responsible for coordinating these activities starting from the project inception phase as part of the planned baseline assessments and then regularly during implementation in line with the stakeholder engagement mechanisms outlined in the stakeholder engagement plan. Please refer to the detailed feedback on the TAP condition 1 in the Overall remarks section.

Paragraph 12: We are in agreement with TAP highlighting that the sustainability of the project interventions is a key concern for this project, and we agree with the importance of the project interventions in close partnership with the relevant entities and institutions in Bosnia and Herzegovina. The project will be implemented by UNDP in its capacity as one entity (Republika Srpska) and focuses on the implementation of infrastructural measures to reduce flood risk in the project area. These measures are a combination of policy, regulatory, institutional, capacity building, structural and non-structural investments. Hence, the project will be implemented across entities and secure coordination across various institutions and workstreams.

UNDP's Direct Implementation Modality offers ample opportunities and concrete instruments to ensure that all relevant stakeholders in Bosnia and Herzegovina are engaged throughout the country, while also mitigating the political and institutional risks to implementation noted by the TAP. UNDP has a long-standing experience of working with the GCF and has established operational practices facilitating engagement of the partner institutions in the project's implementation, which will apply to the GCF project as follows:


---

GCF funds will be used to provide relevant technical expertise and input to the project, including the development of a strategy for mainstreaming non-structural solutions and the mainstreaming of EBA into FBM policy and practice. The following is a general overview of the activities:

* **Policy Framework:** The entity government will take the lead in developing the policy framework that integrates EBA into flood risk management which may involve the Ministry of Natural Resources, Ministry of Agriculture, etc. Key ministries, all project board members, will collaborate to ensure a holistic approach and implementation of the policy.
* **Raising Awareness and Capacity Building:** Awareness campaigns, workshops, and training sessions will be conducted on the importance of non-structural measures and EBA in enhancing climate resilience and reducing risks (Activity 2.3).
* **Gearing Up for Implementation:** The implementation of 21 non-structural and 5 EBA measures (Activity 2.2) will provide the basis for the development of methodologies, and best practices for the mainstreaming of EBA into flood risk management in Russia. In addition, the project will collaborate with research institutions to gather scientific evidence on the cost-effectiveness of EBA solutions and non-structural measures and provide evidence-based research to support policy advocacy efforts, and with environmental agencies to ensure that the project is aligned with national and local ecological aspects of EBA solutions and can advocate for stronger EBA policies and regulations and hold the government accountable for their implementation.
* **Quantify Benefits and Co-Benefits:** Using the socio-economic modeling tool to be developed in Activity 2.3, the project will assess the cost-effectiveness of non-structural measures and EBA, including flood risk reduction, improved water quality, carbon sequestration, etc., and provide evidence-based recommendations on how implemented measures the project will provide evidence of the cost-effectiveness of EBA solutions compared to traditional structural approaches through socio-economic analyses comparing NBS to traditional structural approaches and highlighting the potential for reducing maintenance-and operational-costs over time. This will help demonstrate that non-structural measures, including EBA and EBA solution can achieve broader sustainability goals.
* **Linking with Local Development Goals:** The project will ensure that all 26 NBS measures will be included in the investment planning of high-risk municipalities as part of their overall flood risk management strategy. This will help to ensure alignment with local development goals, such as improved water management, sustainable agriculture, enhanced biodiversity, and resilient infrastructure.

# Annex B - Gender Assessment and Gender Action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### I. Introduction

The GCF project will address the increasing vulnerability of BiH communities and livelihoods to intensified climate-induced hydro-meteorological flood-related disasters (pluvial, fluvial, and torrential) due to a projected increase in precipitation events and lack of quality climate data and flood forecasting and early warning systems. As a result, the BiH authorities are unable to make informed decisions and the affected population cannot effectively cope with floods. Lack of climate information and risk knowledge increases the vulnerability and costs from flooding.

The GCF project will build resilience of BiH communities and livelihoods to climate-induced floods by catalyzing a necessary wide shift from ad hoc, reactive response approaches to holistic a project-wide disaster management and prevention approach based on enhanced climate information, risk knowledge, flood forecasting and early warning.

The project will scale-up successful prototypes tested under the UNDP supported GEF financed project "Technology transfer for climate resilient flood management in Vrbas River Basin".

This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan has been prepared for the submission to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) of a project proposal for the implementation of the project in BiH.

The assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Bosnia and Herzegovina, identify gender issues that may be relevant to the project context, to develop recommendations for advancing gender equality and women's empowerment through the project activities, and to inform the development of the project’s gender action plan. Individual needs and contexts in a disaster management and prevention approach are considered as well as the role of men.

The analysis follows social vulnerability approach and further defines whose voice is seen as the default one, and who else is the most visible perspective. The assessment was based upon available data from various sources including those provided by the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina, international organizations, civil society organizations and any other sources as UNDP Technology transfer for climate resilient flood management in Vrbas River Basin.

1. Undertaking a desktop review and aligning approaches in this proposal with the national priorities of Bosnia and Herzegovina.
2. Incorporating information and lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender equality and women's empowerment by the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina, the United Nations, civil society organizations, and multilateral development agencies.
3. Conducting stakeholder consultations, including consultations with the Agency for Gender Equality of Bosnia and Herzegovina and the two gender centers (of Republic of Srpska and Federation of BiH) on women's issues/roles/youth affected by the project and incorporating all points raised; and
4. Integrating gender considerations in the project indicators, targets, and activities, identifying specific actions to address gender equality.

## UNDP prioritizes gender mainstreaming as its main strategy to achieve gender equality and women's empowerment. Gender mainstreaming is the process of ensuring any planned action in all areas and at all levels involves, responds to, and benefits both men and women equally, while addressing women’s, as well as men’s concerns and experiences an integral dimension of the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of projects so that women benefit equally. Gender mainstreaming is a strategy for achieving gender equality by ensuring that policies, programs, and practices make them profoundly responsive to gender; and, when realized, it ensures that both women and men benefit equally from the development process. It involves much more than simply adding women's perspectives to existing strategies and projects. The gender gap in many areas of life is often required to compensate for the existing gaps and inequalities that women currently face.

The UNDP Gender Equality Strategy 2022-2025 is aligned with the 2030 Development Agenda and UNDP’s Strategic Plan. The strategy recognizes gender equality as a human right as well as instrumental in achieving other development goals. It aims to ensure that women and men are active agents of change and development, not simply beneficiaries and vulnerable groups and it recognizes that gender equality is achieved when there is equal access to resources, opportunities and attitudes and achieve gender equality. The gender assessment and action plan developed for the submission to the Green Climate Fund with the proposal "Scaling up climate resilient flood risk management in Bosnia and Herzegovina" to meet the requirements and standards in UNDP’s Gender Equality Strategy which meets or exceeds the requirements set forth by the Green Climate Fund Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.

## Short background on Bosnia and Herzegovina and an EU Accession Process

Bosnia and Herzegovina is a potential candidate for EU membership. The Stabilization and Association Agreement (SAA) was signed with the European Union in 2011. In February 2016, the country applied for EU membership and in September 2016 the European Council invited the European Commission to submit its Opinion on the merits of Bosnia and Herzegovina’s application. The European Commission prepared a report entitled “Bosnia and Herzegovina: Stabilization and Association Policy and Enlargement Negotiations” handled over a comprehensive Questionnaire covering all EU accession criteria. The country's consolidated answers were finalized in February 2018 and the answers were submitted to the European Commission. In March 2019, Bosnia and Herzegovina prepared its "Opinion" report in 2019, based on the country’s answers to the Questionnaire and follow up with the EU member states and other stakeholders including representatives from international organizations and civil society. This interim report on Bosnia and Herzegovina provides an update on the situation in the country as well as on key developments on

## Overview on Bosnia and Herzegovina Government Construction

The current Constitution of Bosnia and Herzegovina came into existence in 1995 as an Annex to the General Framework Agreement for Peace in Bosnia and Herzegovina. While the General Framework Agreement was intended to provide a constitutional framework, the extent to which the constitutional arrangement defined by its provisions is adequate under present circumstances, is widely debatable. According to the provisions of the Peace Agreement the State of Bosnia and Herzegovina consists of two semi-autonomous political-territorial units of competencies: the Federation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (FBiH) and Republika Srpska (RS). Some years after, an arbitration decision on the disputed areas of the country made Brčko District the third administrative unit of BiH. It is a centralized entity whereas the FBiH is comprised of ten Cantons.

The State level comprises a tripartite rotating Presidency, A Council of Ministers (executive branch) and a bicameral Parliamentary Assembly consisting of a House of Representatives (lower chamber) and a Senate (upper chamber). The judicial branches established by Dayton consists of a State-level Constitutional Court. The High Judicial and Prosecutorial Council, as well as a State Court and Prosecutor Office, were established later.

An international presence under UN auspices—the Office of the High Representative (OHR)—has been in place in Bosnia and Herzegovina since 1995. The Peace Implementation Council Steering Committee was established by the Dayton Agreement to oversee the OHR's work. Since the decoupling of the European Union Special Representative’s mandate from the OHR, the EU has reinforced its role in the country in line with the relevant Council Conclusions. The enhanced

# Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## II. Gender Equality/Inequality in Bosnia and Herzegovina

Before starting to analyze gender inequality in Bosnia and Herzegovina, it is important to make clear and to agree on the meaning of gender equality.

Gender equality refers to the equal rights, responsibilities and opportunities of women and men and girls and boys. Equality does not mean that everyone will become the same but that women's and men's access to rights, responsibilities and opportunities will not depend on whether they are born male or female. Gender equality implies that the interests, needs and priorities of both women and men matter equally in all spheres of life. It also means that there is no discrimination against either sex. Gender equality is not a woman’s issue but should concern and fully engage men as well as women. Equality between women and men is seen both as a human rights issue and as a precondition for sustainable and people-centered development (UN Women, 2013). Gender Mainstreaming: Concepts and definitions

The fundamental principle of human development is for people to enlarge their choices, to realize their potential, and to enjoy the freedom to lead lives they value or have reason to value. Equal opportunities are a necessary condition for this but not sufficient in itself for achieving human development. However, those options remain unequally distributed within and across societies and unfortunately many are still largely unavailable to women. Disadvantage and discrimination, lack of access to resources (material, social, economic, political, cultural, informational, physical and emotional), reinforced by social norms and values, institutions, and public policies, exist in every sphere of women's lives, manifested differently in different country settings. They limit women’s capabilities and opportunities for participation in all aspects of life, including education, health, work, education, standard of living, empowerment, personal security, and other critical dimensions of human development. The fact that women are disadvantaged does not mean that they are less equal and humanity as a whole does not reap its potential (Issues papers, “Gender Equality in Human Development - Measurement Revisited”, Human Development Report Office, UNDP June 2015).

According to the latest UNDP Human Development Report (2020), Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI value for 2019 is 0.780 – which ranks it at 73 out of 189 countries and territories. Between 2000 and 2019, the country’s HDI increased by 45 percent. The country’s GNI per capita increased by 145 percent. Bosnia and Herzegovina's progress in each of the HDI indicators shows that between 1990 and 2019, Bosnia and Herzegovina’s life expectancy at birth increased by 6.5 years, mean years of schooling increased from 8 to 13 years, and expected years of schooling increased from 7 to 14 years. Between 1990 and 2019, Bosnia and Herzegovina's GNI per capita increased by about 73.7 percent between 1990 and 2019. Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI value of 0.780 in 2019 places the country in the high human development group and below the average of 0.791 for countries in Europe and Central Asia. The Inequality HDI (IHDI) introduced in 2010, is basically the HDI discounted for inequality. The IHDI takes into account the distribution of income within a country by dividing both the HDI and the IHDI and can be expressed as a percentage. When discounted for inequality, Bosnia and Herzegovina’s HDI falls to 0.667, a loss of 14.5 percent due to inequality in the

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Distribution of the HDI dimension indices.

This is above the average loss due to inequality for high HDI for Europe and Central Asia which is 1.9 percent.

With regard to Bosnia and Herzegovina, there can be no dispute that significant advances have been made in the realization of the principle of the equality of women and men, which is a result of activities taken by all parties (Gender institutional mechanisms, as well as citizens through civil society organizations) in order to achieve gender equality in Bosnia and Herzegovina. Nonetheless, in spite of these positive changes, there are still a lot to be done.

Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GGI). GII is an index that measures the gap between women's and men's achievement in three areas: health, education, and the labour market while with a separate dimension for reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market while with a separate dimension for reproductive health, empowerment, and the command over economic resources. The GGI considers the same dimensions as the GII but measures the gap between women and men.

The Gender Development Index (GGI) based on the sex-disaggregated Human Development Index, defines the ratio of the female to the male HDI is 0.937 in Bosnia and Herzegovina (female HDI = 0.753 and male HDI = 0.803). This is below the average for Europe and Central Asia, which is 0.953, and placing it in Group 3 – which comprises countries with medium equality in HDI achievements. The GGI measures gender inequality by assessing the gap between women's and men's inequalities in three dimensions - reproductive health, empowerment, and economic activity. Reproductive health is measured by maternal mortality and adolescent birth rates; empowerment is measured by female participation in secondary and higher education by each gender; and economic activity is measured by the labour force participation rate of women and men.

Bosnia and Herzegovina has a GII value of 0.149, ranking it 38 out of 162 countries in the 2015 Human Development Report. The GGI shows that 70 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 89.3 percent of their male counterparts. For every 100,000 live births, 10.0 women die from pregnancy-related causes as opposed to 5.2 men. The female labour force participation rate for ages 15–64 is 35.4 percent compared to 58.1 for men.

The Multi-dimensional Poverty Index (MPI), which identifies multiple overlapping deprivations suffered by individuals in 3 dimensions: health, education, and standard of living. In Bosnia and Herzegovina, 2.2 percent of the population (73 thousand people) are multidimensionally poor while an additional 10.4 percent are classified as vulnerable to multi-dimensional poverty (135 thousand people). The MPI is a measure of the intensity of deprivations experienced by individuals in Bosnia and Herzegovina, it is 37.9 percent. The MPI, which is the share of the population that is multidimensionally poor, adjusted by the intensity of the deprivations, is 8.04%. Compared to income Poverty, the multidimensional

## Political participation and Public Life

According to the 2013 Law on Amendments to the Election Law of Bosnia and Herzegovina, equal representation of genders exists when one of the genders is represented by at least 40% of the total number of candidates in all elections. In practice, however, this is not the case. The percentage of women in legislative and executive authorities at all levels remains unsatisfactory (even the situation is better comparing 2014 and 2018 elections) and the newly introduced 40% representation quota has not been applied.

The last General Elections were held in 2016 in Bosnia and Herzegovina (State, entities, cantonal level, and Brčko District level). The Governments are still not fully established, but the available figures give an overview of the gender reality in BiH. There are two (1.4%) female representatives in the House of Representatives of the Parliamentary Assembly of Bosnia and Herzegovina out of 42 members; and 3 (50%) female delegates in the House of People of the Parliamentary Assembly of Bosnia and Herzegovina.

Local/municipal elections were held in 2018. The electoral cycle was marked in particular by the minimal participation women as candidates for mayorial positions. In the end, four women out of 425 mayoral candidates in total (out of which 29 were women mayoral candidates) were elected as majors, mostly in small municipalities, which is a drop of over 50% in comparison to 2013 local elections.

### Education and Economic participation of women

In general, Bill has adequate laws and regulations in place to ensure gender equality in the education sector. The right to equal education is provided by the Constitution of Bill (part III.3) and the National Policy on Gender Equality and Equity in Education (2010). The Framework Law on Elementary and Secondary Education in Bill and the Northwest Large Pre-School Upbringing and Education in Bill provide a legal framework for education. These laws empower women to access education at all levels, including primary, secondary, and tertiary.

Here we first look at the working age population of Bill (15–64), consisting of employed, unemployed and inactive persons, in accordance with the Labor Force Survey standards, to identify the gender gaps in education.

#### Working aged women have lower educational attainment than men in Bill

- 39.5 percent of working age population has no formal education.
- 51 percent has secondary specialization or education.
- 0.5 percent has higher education.

A considerably higher proportion of women than men only have a primary education (40 percent compared to 29 percent), with a small number having no formal education at all (38.5 percent for women and 37.1 percent for men). The gender gap in higher education is considerably smaller, at only one percentage point in favor of men.

#### The FHIB has the largest gender gap

In the RS and BDBH, this gender gap is in the 16- to 10-point range for primary and 14-and 13-point range for secondary education, respectively.

#### Education achievements of working-age women in the entities and the Brice District are similar to Bill (see Table 2b)

In the RS, 58.7 percent of working age women have only a primary education and 41 percent and 44 percent, respectively, have a secondary education. The FHIB has the highest ratio of women with higher education (9.7 percent) and the RS, the lowest (7.7 percent). In BDBH, 50.8 percent of working age women have only primary education (31.2 percent) and the lowest with secondary and tertiary education (14.9 percent and 7.8 percent, respectively).

By contrast, in the labor force (employed + unemployed), there is a significantly higher percentage of women than men with a university education (20.7 percent of women and 12.3 percent of men), while it is the opposite for secondary education (60.4 percent of women and 75.7 percent of men). In tertiary education, 89.5 percent of men have higher education compared to only a quarter of all employed women.

## Compared to one in seven men, if at the same time, a greater percentage of women than men have only a primary education (three percentage point difference).

Considering educational achievement and unemployment rates, except for primary education, women with secondary and university educations are also more likely to be unemployed than men with the same education. Among those with secondary education, women tend to be more often unemployed compared to men in all age groups. However, among those with a university education, the situation is slightly more favorable—the gender gap in favor of men is five percentage points[1].

For emphasis, note there figures do not account for men and women who are inactive.

Men with higher education are less likely to be employed than women and women are more likely to be inactive. In the BFBH, 25 percent of women with secondary education were unemployed (up to 42 percent of women with higher education). Men with higher education are more likely to be inactive than women by nine percentage points. However, women with higher education are more likely to be unemployed compared to men in all age groups. One in six highly educated women is unemployed compared to one in ten men.

Comparing the labor force (employed + unemployed), the FBBH has found that the FBBH has the best educated female labor force. In the FBBH, 23.4 percent of women in the workforce have a university education and 60.1 percent have secondary education. The BFBH has the lowest percentage of women with primary education (58.9 percent) and the highest percentage of women with higher education (72.6 percent). In the BFBH, 43.6 percent of unemployed women are employed only a primary education compared to 10.2 percent in the FBBH.

The FBBH also has the lowest percent of workforce with primary education (16.5 percent). The FBBH and RS have almost equal proportions of workforce with secondary education, 60.1 percent; however, the BFBH has a higher proportion of workforce with university education than either the FBBH or RS. The FBBH also stands out compared to the BFBH and RS in terms of the percentage of employed women with primary education (26.9 percent) and the lowest with secondary education (54 percent) [LFS, 2013: Pages 7.1 – 7.3]. Looking at employed women only (LFS, 2015: Tables B.8.1–B.8.3), over half of them in the FBBH have a secondary education and one-third have higher education. The percentage of employed women with higher education is 27.9 percent and 26.6 percent, respectively. In the RS, 17.3 percent of employed women have higher education compared to 85.0 and also in the BFBH, 43.6 percent of employed women only a primary education are unemployed (which is lower than the 52.6 percent). Women with secondary education also account for the highest percentage of unemployed women among all groups in the FBBH.

The BFBH stands out compared to RS and FBBH by having considerably higher rates of unemployment among women with university education, compared to considerably lower rates in the RS and 17.3 percent in the FBBH.

Women's and men's choices in education have an impact on their earnings later in life. The "World Bank study on Bosnia and Herzegovina: Gender Disparities in Endowments, Access to Economic Opportunities and Earnings" [LFS, 2015] shows that women with higher levels of information on the gender wage gap. Their estimates show that the hourly-wage gap for the population aged 36–64 years of age working for salaries or wages is nine percent. This means that the difference between men and women's earnings is nine percent, which is considerably lower than in many other countries. The gap varies across education levels and age groups, but it is always in favor of men, with the exception of the 15–24 and 55–64 age groups[2]. When controlled for factors such as age, industry,

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

occupation, gender, and education, the latter being the main difference in observable characteristics between men and women; the wage gap persists[1].

There is clear evidence that gender norms translate into preferences and choices when it comes to the fields of study chosen by male and female students. In a number of instances, these choices have led to a ‘gender divide’ and occupational segregation, creating “feminization of professions”. Overall, women are more likely than men to choose studies in social sciences, humanities, and education, which translate into lower lifetime earnings and social benefits. Lower paid[2] women’s jobs exist in both public and private sectors; these jobs tend to be dominated by women and are primarily associated with lower earnings including education, health, culture, and agriculture.

There is a serious problem of gender-based inequalities and discrimination in women’s access to the labour market, combined with structural stereotyping and the problem of unpaid work, which affects women disproportionately. All these combined factors are reflected in the low number of employed women in the agricultural sector (18 percent), construction industry (20 percent), industry, and services sectors respectively[3]. It is also alarming that there are twice as many women than men with university degrees who are unemployed. There is a serious problem of gender-based inequalities and discrimination in women’s access to resources, combined with structural stereotyping and the problem of unpaid work, which affects women disproportionately. The current employment strategies do not effectively address the issue of female participation in the labour market; the improvement of which would contribute to poverty reduction.

## Access to resources

Women and men have equal legal rights to property ownership, management, and use, as well as equal legal status in relation to access to housing and credit facilities[4]. However, local traditions and customs may affect women’s ability to exercise these rights. Women are not always given the right to a male relative or simply are not included in the deeds for land or housing [Interviews]. Nationally, men account for over 70 percent of landownership and own 74 percent of dwellings. Women own only 26 percent of land and have joint ownership with their partners in 15 percent of dwellings that have joint ownership[5]. Women hold joint ownership in 11 percent of male-headed households, compared to 38 percent for female-headed households. One possible explanation is that female-headed households are more likely to include women without partners. Across political entities, women own 16 percent of dwellings in the RS and BDHII and 14 percent in the BDHI; men hold joint ownership with their partners in 20 percent – compared to three percent in the RS and one percent in the BDHI.

There is considerable difference in dwelling ownership among men and women in urban and rural settings. Men own 80 percent of dwellings in rural areas and 66 percent in urban settings. For women, the trend is reversed – dwelling ownership among urban women is higher at 19 [WB, 2015]

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

**Green Climate Fund Full Proposal**

The document discusses gender assessment in relation to urban versus rural areas within the context of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). It highlights that while approximately 16 percent of women are heads of households, this figure is higher for non-poor women compared to poor women. In urban areas, 13 percent of women hold income-generating jobs, whereas in rural areas, only 8 percent do so.

The document explains that the lower percentage of women holding income-generating jobs in rural areas could be due to limited access to financial inclusion and fewer opportunities for women to support themselves and their families. It also notes a higher prevalence of traditional gender roles among women in rural areas compared to urban settings, which may influence their decision-making power.

The level of financial inclusion is similar between Eastern Europe and Central Asia (EECA) average lag considerably behind upper-middle income countries. Financial inclusion has been broadly recognized as an important tool for promoting broad economic development and reducing poverty. According to FINDEX data, 53 percent of the BB population had an account at a financial institution in 2014 compared with 67 percent in EECA and 89 percent in upper-middle income countries.

The percentage of account holders has declined by three percentage points in comparison with 2011, with a similar trend in EEAC and upper middle-income countries. The rural population in BB has higher ratios of access to financial institutions than in EEAC – 52 percent compared with 36 percent in EEAC.

A considerable gender financial gap exists in BB. Only 47 percent of women have an account at a financial institution, compared to 59 percent of men. However, in comparison with 2011, the decline is more significant for women than for men – eight percentage points versus six percentage points for men. While still low, men have higher ratios of savings than women - 12 percent compared to six percent. And women have slightly higher rates of borrowing than men - 19 percent compared with 17 percent.

Nationally, financial institutions are sources for 14 percent of loans, and 7 percent come from family and/or friends. Looking at all sources of funding as a group, 275 percent of women borrow money to start or expand a business (FINDEX, 2014). Other sources of finance include informal lenders, family and friends, shops, and employers.

Women and men borrow for different purposes. According to FINDEX, women tend to borrow more for education or school fees (23 percent compared to 1 percent of men), health (45 compared to 16 percent of men) and other immediate needs; while men borrow more in order to start, operate, or expand a farm or business (27 percent of men compared to 2 percent of women).

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Women in Rural Areas

The agricultural sector employs slightly more men than women - 165 percent and 140 percent, respectively, according to the World Bank (WBG) data. The Republic of Korea is an exception as it has the highest percentage of their workforce employed in agriculture and the highest percentage of women in agriculture. In the BIDHR, 28.7 percent of women are employed in agriculture compared to 34.6 percent in the BIDRH, and 10.6 percent in the FRH. Also in the RS, 95 percent of women are employed in agriculture, compared to 18 percent in the BIDHR, and 9.1 percent in the FRH.

Men and women tend to engage in different types of farm-related activities. Women more often are engaged in lower levels of agricultural activity, own smaller businesses, and are more likely to be engaged in subsistence farming. They also have less access to land, credit, inputs, and a range of activities, have different levels of ownership and are more likely to be engaged in larger, formal markets. Farming performed almost exclusively by women includes making, picking fruit, and activities that require greater processing or preparation time (e.g., cleaning, sorting, drying) compared with men who perform more tasks related to machinery, transport of goods, marketing, and negotiating contracts.

In the RS, women in rural areas have very few (if any) opportunities for non-work or house social interaction. In preparation for developing a new strategic program for agricultural development by 2030, the World Bank conducted a baseline survey on women’s empowerment in rural areas, administering 860 surveys to agricultural households. One issue raised by women that stood out and came as a surprise was the lack of need for opportunities, services, and activities aimed at empowering rural women. Women expressed their desire to see more coalescence around different interests. This was surprising given the assumptions that in the age of globalisation, women would be more open to new ideas and opportunities outside the home. This finding provides additional validation to the existence of still-deep social stereotypes that restrict women’s involvement outside of household and family. In response to this finding, the new strategic program will include a specific focus on addressing this issue.

Engagement in this area is a continuity of the 2010 rural development strategy and the RS Action Plan for the Advancement of the Position of Women in Rural Areas. The biggest limitation is lack of resources to address it.
