# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

A significant mechanism for establishing partnerships between institutional mechanisms for gender equality and non-governmental organizations is the Financial Mechanism for the Implementation of the Global Gender Action Plan (GGAP). The GGAP’s main objective is to strengthen capacity of gender institutional mechanisms and relevant institutions to integrate activities from the GAP into regular plans and programs, as well as to support non-governmental organizations in their efforts.

In 2011, through FIGAR funds, 36 non-governmental organizations from all over BiH where supported to implement projects in total value of BKM 710.000. Implemented projects addressed issues of prevention and supporting victims of domestic violence, strengthening women’s entrepreneurship and self-employment, access to education and employment programs, as well as strengthening the role of women in political and public life.

The actual number and types of women's associations and NGOs that focus on gender equality in BiH is not known, but good reference is Women's Network of BiH which is an informal group of civil society organizations and individuals who represent and work on women’s rights and encourage women to participate more actively in the political process. The network focuses on violence non-violence and anti-militarism, through acceptance of feminist values of action – peace, solidarity, equality, respect for human rights, democracy. The network currently consists 40 member organizations, all from urban areas, of which 9 are registered in RS and 31 in BiH. The network is supported by Royal Norwegian Embassy.

(Women (and men) in less developed and poorer municipalities face greater challenges than women living in more developed areas. For example, in the northern part of the RS, the sectorial organization for women is the Women's Association of the Federation of Agricultural Cooperatives (RS). More women are co-owners of land and houses, while in the more culturally traditional southern part, ownership is negligible. Similar differences exist in terms of access to social services, especially in rural areas due to inadequate services and inadequate transportation. In the FBiH, issues of access to services and gender norms tend to be more pronounced than in RS. Women's associations are particularly important in mountain areas, and often exacerbated by natural disasters, such as the devastating landslides of 2014.

The greatest obstacles to broader gender development and empowerment in rural areas faced by women are related to property rights, access to public services, finance, training, advisory services and lack of information. Women tend to be more socially active than men. Only 12 percent of rural women own their dwellings compared to 80 percent of men and only eight percent of women have joint ownership, compared to 13 percent of urban women? This firmly holds true for other types of property as well (land, livestock, vehicles, etc.). Women are also more likely than men to be illiterate or semi-literate. # Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Property rights makes it practically impossible for women who would like to undertake new entrepreneurial activity to secure credit at reasonable rates. However, the situation seems to be improving among the younger women in terms of asset inheritance, co-ownership of land and dwellings, and access to financial services (Interviews and unpublished findings).

Women in rural areas have lower levels of employment and self-employment than women in urban areas. The UNDP 2013 report called "Rural Development in Bosnia and Herzegovina: Myth and Reality," indicates that self-employment rates and employment rates are lower for women in rural areas compared to those in urban areas (Interviews). The LFS data also confirms that the women’s inactive rate in rural areas is much higher than in urban areas.

Limited opportunities for employment and self-employment are perpetuated by low levels of understanding of such possibilities, which is linked to inadequate (or non-existent) opportunities for information or training. As part of the preparations for the new RS agricultural strategy, the RS Ministry of Agriculture has launched a project aimed at increasing women’s participation in agriculture. However, this project does not specifically target women as a separate group but rather focuses on women for whom men are the primary breadwinners in the households. Eighty percent of female respondents said that they never took part in educational or training activities about business (Interviews and unpublished findings). It is reasonable to assume that the situation is probably not much different in the FBiH, but this kind of information is currently not available.

Rural women have overall lower levels of education than women in urban areas, as well as limited facility with and access to the Internet. This limits their knowledge about what training opportunities are available for them. The lack of transportation facilities makes it difficult for rural women to be able to access them due to the distance from their homes, times they are being offered, and the limited time they have for such activities because of their household responsibilities.

Lack of affordable childcare and pre-schools in rural and smaller settlements is one of the central obstacles to women’s participation in education, employment, entrepreneurial activity, and consequently, employment. The Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey (MICS) shows that 20 percent of first-graders in urban areas attended preschool, compared to 13 percent from rural areas[6], and 22.8 percent of children aged between three and five years old were enrolled in pre-schools, compared to 7.9 percent of rural children[7]. Part of the reason for such a rural-urban gap may be the difference in awareness of the importance of pre-school education, or the fact that the level of the mother’s education is lower among women living in rural areas than those living in urban areas. However, a portion of the difference can likely be attributed to inadequate availability of affordable and accessible childcare and pre-school education facilities in the rural areas.

Women in rural areas have limited access to appropriate information and services. There is very limited number of women associations and cooperatives which exacerbates the lack of access to relevant information and services. Women are also less likely than men to engage in, own, and share information on more equal footing. Prevailing patriarchal/conservative social norms

## Women’s participation in agricultural activities and decision-making varies by region. Regional differences are along the levels of economic development, with less developed areas/regions having lower levels of women’s participation in agriculture and decision making.

## Marginalized groups and Social Inclusion

Marginalized groups considered in this section include Roma, persons with disabilities, and internally displaced persons, although the list of marginalized groups varies across agencies and institutions.

International human rights law directly applies through the Bill Constitution, but the Constitution does not protect equal rights for all groups and individuals. Human rights and fundamental freedoms for all individuals are enshrined in the International Conventions on Civil and Political Rights, on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR), and on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities and Fundamental Freedoms (ECHR), directly applicable in Bill. Beyond clear-cut, direct discrimination issues confirmed in ECHR judgments, gender-based discrimination, especially in the case of women with disabilities (women with disabilities, Roma women with disabilities, etc.) is difficult to prove in court.

Persons with disabilities in BiH find themselves in an extremely difficult situation. Due to fragmented policies, persons with disabilities face discrimination on the basis of their place of residence. Authorities and the private sector ignore affirmative measures related to their employment and social inclusion. The most vulnerable groups are Roma women and people with disabilities in Bosnia and Herzegovina “are in the areas of social protection, health, education, housing, access to justice, labor and employment, and in all the fields of organized action for people with disabilities. Even where laws exist to protect this area, there is an evident record of discrimination in practice.”

Women with disabilities face daily discrimination both as women and as persons with disabilities. They often lack adequate health care and equal access to services and are often socially isolated. According to the 2013 census, at least 294,856 (or about 3.5% of the population of BiH (8.3% of the population) are diagnosed with some form of a disability and that 39% of the population is either directly or indirectly affected by the consequences of disability. Women make up 50.6% of the population and 54.7% of all people disabled.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

As the State and Gender Equality Agency recognizes, women with disabilities are especially vulnerable to being victims of multiple discrimination in Botswana society, particularly in the areas of labour and employment. Although Bill has ratified the UN Convention and adopted the EU standards on equal treatment for persons with disabilities, there is no legislation that addresses their individual condition, not the cause of disability. However, this has never been implemented.

The rights of people with disabilities are differentiated differently in Botswana’s two entities, and within one of them (the Federation of BII), in each of the ten cantons. Both entities have adopted strategies for the promotion of gender equality and non-discrimination, but only the Federation of BII has implemented them. In the Federation of BII, 248 people with disabilities have started to work in 2015. Only 26.6% (66 out of 248) of these were women. In Republica Supika, 547 disabled people were employed and only 39.5% (217) of them were women; among these, 20.5% (43) were BII women. The State has no special programs for employment, retraining or rehabilitation related to women with disabilities, nor does it have relevant statistics, data, or special measures for protection and care for pregnant women.

Almost two-thirds of all disabled persons in Botswana live close or are below the poverty line. One of the major obstacles is a lack of statistical data and information on the extent, nature, and characteristics of people with disabilities. There is also no central registry of people with disabilities. The majority of women with disabilities have never been married. Women with disabilities are also more likely to be victims of domestic violence. Health care is a very problematic field, especially reproductive health protection and protection and care for pregnant women.

In addition to the lack of appropriate examination tables for women in wheelchairs, the majority of health care workers are not trained for examinations or delivery when it comes to women with disabilities[25]. It is mentioned in a report issued by NGOs on the Application of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities in Botswana and Herzegovina that women with disabilities have problems conceiving children due to a diverse range. There is concern that the child should be with the “healthy” parent, ignoring the child’s best interest, even in cases where the father was violent, and taking for granted that women with disabilities are not able to raise a child.

The most severe and most common forms of violating the rights of people with disabilities in Botswana and Herzegovina are found in the fields of social protection, health education, rights of access to information, labour and employment and in the field of organized action for people with disabilities.

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Levels of discrimination against Roma are not the same for men and women.

Roma women experience multiple vulnerability factors compared to Roma men and are even less politically represented and economically secure than Roma men. The 2009 report of the ECHR to amend the BH Constitution, which was adopted by a two-thirds majority in the National Assembly and by the House of Peoples of BiH, has not yet been implemented. Even when the direct discriminatory provision gets removed, Roma women will still face indirect obstacles in running for office at any level. Among other things, they are less likely to be employed or to have access to education than men; women, despite campaigning by the Roma Women’s Network before the 2016 local elections.

Discrimination of Roma women is very evident when one considers the levels of their formal employment (Human Rights Ombudsman Institution, 2013). The Alternative Report on the Implementation of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in BiH, which was submitted to the UNCED Committee in 2014 and which included data from a survey conducted by the Human Rights Ombudsman Institution in 2013, shows that while 85.7 percent of Roma men were surveyed, almost 82 percent were unemployed, only nine percent were working in the informal sector, and seven percent were begging for subsistence. Additionally, in 2006, only two to three percent of Roma women were employed (Human Rights Ombudsman Institution, 2013). The 2011 census report did not include employment data broken down by ethnicity; it is not possible to cite more recent data on the employment of Roma women.

The discrimination towards Roma, especially toward Roma girls, is also reflected in their rights-to-education. According to the Alternative Report on the Implementation of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in BiH, 2013, in 2011–2012 varied by sex for Roma children: Gender differences were larger in the FRBiH than in the RS. In the FRBiH 78.6 percent of Roma boys attended school compared to 66.9 percent of Roma girls; in the RS, 74.3 percent of Roma boys attended school, compared to 71.3 percent of Roma girls (ibid.). The number of girls who dropped out of primary education was greater than the number of boys.

However, there are still some positive developments that can be observed regarding Roma girls and employment of Roma women and their access to health and social protection:

Roma women experience very high levels of violence. Over 43 percent of surveyed Roma women self-reported being subjected to some form of physical violence*. The same survey also identified that 25.7 percent of Roma women had been subjected to sexual violence, while 65.6 percent know a woman who has been subjected to sexual violence. In addition, there is an increasing number of Roma girls vulnerable to trafficking for forced marriages and a high percentage of arranged or paid marriages.

Twenty-two years after signing the Dayton Peace Agreement (DPA), 98,124 internally displaced persons in BiH are still of whom 50,763 were victims of war, and some 47,000 minority retirees in Bosnia and Herzegovina are still in need of solutions*. The revised strategy for implementation of annex VIII of DPA was supposed to be implemented by 2014.

## Gender-based Violence

Bill ratified the Council of Europe Convention against Violence Against Women and Domestic Violence in 2013 and the entities have their own strategic documents. In the RS, acts of domestic violence can be prosecuted as a criminal offence; however, economic violence is recognized in the Household Code of Obligations. The RS has adopted a National Strategy for Prevention and Fighting domestic violence. In the FBiH, the acts are processed in accordance with the Criminal Code of Bosnia. In this criminal code, each entity has a referral mechanism for responding to domestic violence.

Every second woman has experienced some form of gender-based violence since the age of 15, while every fifth woman has probably experienced it over the past 12 months. Working with perpetrators of violence against women, especially in cases of domestic violence, is not yet at a standard level and there are still many obstacles to overcome. The number of shelters is close to a closure of two out of nine safe houses. Victims of conflict-related sexual violence (CRSV) still lack socio-economic rights and legal protection. Victims of sexual violence are not treated equally in the two entities, with the RS having more developed mechanisms for dealing with trafficking; however, it still does not fully meet the minimum standards for doing so.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Mechanisms to address Gender Inequality in Bosnia and Herzegovina

The introduction of the principle of the equality of women and men in all areas of public and private life does not only constitute the realization of one of the core standards required to achieve gender equality, but also a necessary precondition for the sustainable social and economic development of a country, with special emphasis on poverty reduction and the improvement of living quality.

Over the past twenty years significant efforts have been made in Bosnia and Herzegovina to develop a national legal, institutional, and political framework for the implementation of the principle of gender equality.

Institutional gender mainstreaming mechanisms have been established within the system of legislative and executive power, at all levels, as part of an overall effort to improve the status of gender equality in public life. These include:

- The Bill Law on Gender Equality (LoGE) was adopted in 2003 (BilOg 16/97) and amended in 2009 (BilOg 102/09), including improved definitions and improved implementation provisions.
- An integrated text was published in 2010 (BilOg 32/10).
- The law specifically addresses education, health care, labor market, social security, family policy, housing, culture, sports, public life, and media. The Law provided the framework for the gender institutional mechanisms which implement it and monitor its implementation.
- The Law has established the Agency for Gender Equality (AGE) as a central body of the Government of Bosnia and Herzegovina and Gender Centre-Gender Equality Centre of the Government of Republika Srpska), which is under the direct control of the Agency. The entity gender centre in public life, i.e., governmental bodies.
- The BH institutional mechanism on gender equality forms a network which includes institutions on the municipal, cantonal, entity and state level, both in the executive and the legislature.

Unfortunately, these institutions on municipal and cantonal level were established through different mechanisms and with different purposes. As a result of this fragmentation and after the projects over the gender mechanisms did not continue with their active role.

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

At the state (legislative) level there is a Committee for Gender Equality of the Parliamentary Assembly of BiH. At the entity level, there are Commissions for Gender Equality of the House of Peoples of the National House of Representatives of the Parliament of Bosnia and Herzegovina, as well as the Equal Opportunities Committee of the National Assembly of Republika Srpska.

All institutional mechanisms have precisely defined mandate, which shows governments' commitment to achieve gender equality and inclusion of gender issue in all segments of social life. It is important to note that these institutions are not only responsible for defining the real drivers of the initiatives and measures, and the key implementers and monitors of the gender mainstreaming in BiH. These three institutional mechanisms for gender equality are crucial instruments for ensuring the implementation of the LGiG. The LGiG places them at the forefront, as they are the most prominent place considering their activities, projects, and measures which they have undertaken to strengthen and ensure sustainability of institutional mechanisms for gender equality.

The LGiG prescribes that victims of discrimination may seek judicial protection in accordance with existing procedures. Special proceedings for protection from discrimination are possible in compliance with the Law on Protection of Discrimination (official Gazette of BiH).

BiH has adopted two laws from the Council of Europe: Violence against Women and Domestic Violence Act in 2012 and 2013, respectively. The laws replicate the definitions and elements of violence from an international framework. Domestic violence (DV), therefore, includes physical, psychological, or sexual pain and suffering as well as economic damages against a family member.

The first BiH Gender Action Plan (GAP) was adopted for the period of 2006 – 2011 and financed through the main financial instrument for the Gender Action Plan (GAP) which aggregated funds from various donors.

The new GAP for the period 2018–2022 builds on important international documents: CEDAW Convention and Recommendations of the UN CEDAW Committee, National Plan of Action based on the Beijing Platform for Action, BiH Constitution, BiH law on gender equality, BiH laws and regulations as well as a number of other binding and recommended UN, CoE and EU documents. GAP also refers to domestic documents such as the state, entity, and cantonal constitutions as well as the Federal and State Gender Equality Strategies. The new GAP builds upon the previous GAP and places significant focus on strengthening the gender machinery as an important strategic pillar for monitoring and reporting on the implementation of the GAP.

GAP is a policy document that guides gender equality policies at lower levels of government and includes specific recommendations for the BiH level, as well as for each entity in it. In accordance with the LGiG, many local self-governance units were adopting local action plans (during the period 2013 – 2017) which operationalized the GAP’s high-level priorities, bearing in mind local circumstances and capacities of the local authorities. The first BiH local action plans for gender policies for women's empowerment developed by the municipalities were only the first steps in changing the mindset of local authorities and were often of lower quality due to low capacities for gender analysis and limited sources of sex disaggregated sufficiently representative data.

## Institutional capacity to implement normative framework for gender equality remains to be one of the most serious challenges in BiH. The research about the institutional capacity to implement the Gender Equality Act (BiH, 2016) has shown that there is a serious limited capacity of public institutions to implement the established legal framework. The mailed questionnaire has been sent to 22 public institutions and only 8 have responded[2]. Among those who replied, only 40% replied that they are familiar with the Law on Gender Equality (BiH, 2016). It is important to note that among those who replied, only one person had been involved with gender equality issues. Since it is reasonable to assume that responses were selective, in terms that those who were not familiar with the problem were also the ones who did not respond[3]. The research has shown that there was a lack of institutions’ policies related to gender equality in BiH. In addition, research revealed that great majority of responses has shown that there was a lack of institutional regulations related to gender discrimination and sexual harassment at work. This is an important finding because it shows the gap between normative and institutional aspects of gender equality in BiH which needs to be addressed by concrete set of measures and recommendations which will be fully contextualized in present day Bill society.

## Civil Society organizations (women/gender/DRB)

Prior to the 1990s, the associations, organizations, ovela, and trade-unions that existed at the time were mainly part of the governmental machinery and thus could hardly be viewed as elements of civil society. This means that there were only a few organizations in former Yugoslavia which could be considered as elements of civil society (e.g., Women’s Union of Socialist Government; their work was also not directly related to the needs of the country's population. These organizations generally included trade unions, chambers of commerce, sports and professional associations, and women's associations, which acted in accordance with the interests of the ruling socialist system.

However, certain feminist initiatives were launched during the 1970s in the capitals of the former Yugoslav republics, in universities, academic circles and among intellectuals[4]. The first NGOs in

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

The region, including BiH, were established at the start of and during the 1991-1995 war; they were aimed at calling for peace and organizing protests to support the cause of peace, to address the developing humanitarian crisis and to provide aid to refugees and the victims of war. Starting in 1996, a number of civil society organizations (CSOs) were established in BiH as an attempt to rebuild confidence in the divided country, take part in the rebuilding of the country and its institutions and to provide services and support to the population. The majority of these CSOs were established by men; however, there were women's organizations. These organizations were the first to cross the entity separation lines and to start working on gender mainstreaming and gender equality.

The civil society sector in BiH is characterized by a high level of fragmentation within society in regard to the effects of inequality and they organized activities related to the participation of women in politics, combating violence against women, the provision of assistance for the protection of women's rights, etc. The CSOs were also involved in other areas such as education with a view to establishing groups and associations across the country, etc. Some years later, women's NGOs even began to be established in the remote and rural areas of the country.

Some civil society interventions include fragmented approaches to gender equality, but there are several different types of organizations that work on gender equality. However, this is not an analysis. Implementation is rarely monitored using gender sensitive indicators and sex-disaggregated data. Finally, advocacy initiatives rarely take a gender transformative approach. One of the interventions that have been implemented in BiH are those related to women's rights, gender mainstreaming and gender equality. (Centar za ekologiju i energiju, Tuša, Gender Centers of RS and FBiH) On one hand, there is a large number of organizations where second-class men are primarily the leaders of the biggest CSOs which do not specifically deal with gender issues (e.g., UNDP, BiH, Gender Centres RS and FBiH). At the other end, there are gender equality organizations where nearly all the leadership positions are held by women. However, the work of women's organizations is not properly deployed in the largest civil society initiatives for capacity building and gender mainstreaming. On a positive note, organizations are increasingly working with men on gender equality in public and in private life.

## Gender issue in addressing the increasing vulnerability to intensified climate and flood related disasters (pluvial, fluvial, and torrential) in Bosnia and Herzegovina

The project objective is to address the increasing vulnerability of BiH communities and livelihoods to intensified climate-induced hydrometeorological flood-related disasters (pluvial, fluvial and torrential). This will be accomplished by adopting an integrated approach to flood risk management resulting from a combination of structural and non-structural approaches. The project will implement long-term flood risk management strategies, including a combination of structural and non-structural measures, as well as the development of early warning systems and other flood approaches. In addition, strengthened early warning and emergency response systems will provide forecasts and early warning to at-risk populations. For achieving its objectives and targeting the GCF assistance the project employs social vulnerability approach. The various elements of this approach are discussed below in the context of gender equality and gender mainstreaming.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Climate change, climate variability and weather events pose a threat to the eradication of extreme poverty and should serve as a spur to halt efforts not only to reduce greenhouse gas emissions but also to tackle other underlying risk drivers such as unplanned urban development, vulnerable livelihoods, and weak governance.

Families are displaced and victims lose shelter. This is complicated further by immense shortages of food and drinking water. Severity of impact of natural hazards are often measured by the degree to which functioning of individuals, groups or organizations is disrupted. The disruption can be physical (e.g., loss of life), economic (e.g., loss of income), social (e.g., separation from family and community, damage to vegetation and infrastructure, epidemics of communicable diseases, loss of land, home, crops, livestock, productive assets, and other property, loss of livelihoods) or psychological.

### Vulnerability

Vulnerability describes the characteristics and circumstances of a community, system or asset that make it susceptible to the damaging effects of a hazard. There is many aspects of vulnerability including physical, social, economic, political and environmental. The vulnerability is determined by the material and social assets that individuals and communities possess. The effect of possessing resources to a different degree is evident at all phases of disaster cycle i.e., detection, protection, impact, recovery.

The vulnerability of households depends on their composition. Gender age and of household members, number of dependents and persons with disabilities, economic status, human, social and political capital: all have an effect on protection from, response to and impact or recovery from natural hazards. The gender dimension is important in understanding the vulnerability of a female headed, consisting of only a woman or a man. In all phases of disaster cycle it is important to consider composition of households and the ratio of dependents, these being children, seniors, and persons with disabilities. Gender also has an impact on the capacity of individuals and households: "the capacity of a system to maintain its basic functions and structures in time of shocks and perturbations" (Birdman, 2006,[15])

Social vulnerability approach to disasters emphasizes the role of social, economic, and political relations in shaping the distribution of risks: as “some groups in society are more prone than others to damage, loss, and suffering in the context of differing hazards” (Blair et al., 1994). In dealing with natural hazards, their impact on the social conditions that underlie different outcomes should be taken into consideration. Households rather than individuals should be taken as a unit for this interpretation.

Gender interacts with other characteristics such as poverty, disability, age, seniority, and displacement – which rarely act alone – to exacerbate inequality and vulnerability.


## Gender

During the third week of May 2014, heavy rains fell in Russia and Herzegovina (BH) causing sudden floods, flooding and landslides over one third of the country. A similar flood event also occurred again in 2019. Urban, industrial, rural areas and roads were submerged; thousands were displaced and victims lost shelter. Many houses and businesses were destroyed or damaged, and the effect on agriculture and production was severe. More than one million people have been affected.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

A government-led Post Disaster Needs Assessment was conducted in 2014 by the World Bank, EIU, and UN to identify damages, losses, and needs, and to provide an estimate of national recovery costs[1]. UN Women responded to the emergency by contributing a humanitarian grant covering 50% of the total cost. The PDDA estimates not only the replacement cost of damaged assets but also the cost to “build back better”, to increase resilience and mitigate the effects of future disasters.

Having in mind all earlier mentioned, throughout the Gender Analysis as well as the PDNA 2014, it is clear that Gender inequality in Bosnia Herzegovina has significant implications for disaster recovery. Women are more likely than men to be displaced by natural disasters; women who have been affected will experience greater difficulty compared to men in obtaining assistance. Therefore the PDNA 2014 Report among others, recommends: provisions of training in gender-sensitive disaster risk reduction and response; ensuring that women’s needs are taken into account when preparing of gender-responsive disaster plans; compliance with existing requirements for disaggregated statistics at all levels of government; ensure transparency in the distribution of recovery funds; provide adequate support to women who have been displaced by natural disasters, including access to education and health services; ensure equal participation of women and men in decision-making processes related to housing, land property and agriculture etc. Also, the PDNA recognizes that “Funding for women’s economic and political empowerment, and for equal participation in all disaster-related discussions and decision making is critical.”

While the country is one of the most advanced in terms of legislation and policy on gender equality, women are largely excluded from political and decision-making processes; they have disproportionate access to resources and services. Women are also more likely than men to be victims of violence.

There is particular concern with respect to women HPSs, disabled people and children who many are subject to violence. There are even more severely disadvantaged and marginalized.

However, there is one promising example: Gender specific issues in state of natural disasters are recognized by the Government of Bosnia Herzegovina. In 2014 the Center has developed a Gender Action Plan for conditions of natural disaster. The Plan, among others, anticipates constant communication with relevant institutions in order to introduce gender perspective into all phases of disaster management cycle. It also includes: continuous and defined monitoring of disaster impact through defined gender-sensitive indicators, constant communication between Women’s NGOs and local communities in order to assess needs of women, men and children during disaster raising public awareness and information exchange to support population in need etc.

Resilience-building against future shocks will require attention and funding directed toward reducing the severe economic and decision-making disparities between women and men.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Summary of gender differences in vulnerability and adapting to disasters

### Disparities that increase risks for women in disasters:
- Higher levels of poverty
- Extensive responsibilities of caring for others
- Domestic violence
- Traditional women's occupations

### Gender experiences that can increase capacities for managing disaster situations: Women:
- Social networking
- Caretaking skills
- Extensive knowledge of communities
- Management of natural and environmental resources
- High levels of risk awareness

### Disparities that increase risks for men in disasters:
- Occupational segregation
- Internalized norms of masculinity
- Roles in the family and in the house

### Gender experiences that can increase capacities for managing disaster situations: Men:
- Professional and work contacts
- Technical abilities
- Limited childcare responsibilities

## Prevention and protection:

Early warning system works as a potent protection mechanism against natural hazards. But as receivers of warnings are humans, for warranting the desired response a number of human related factors should be considered. Information on hazard risk can be delivered in time, but still the problem of understanding it, believing it, and acting according to provided recommendations remains. Hence saving life, health and property is not guaranteed if the messages on multi-hazard risk information are to be tailored to the needs and capabilities of vulnerable groups. This includes people with physical disabilities (PWDs), people living in poverty (PLPs). Education level, knowledge of language in which warning is communicated problems of hearing and seeing constitute serious barriers for adequately grasping the threat.

Men appeared to be more confident in managing an emergency situation including the perception that they were better prepared than women. Women reported lower levels of preparedness and response. Additionally, women had fewer opportunities to maintain a high level of social networking in the community, which may lead to them being less informed. Effective targeting requires utilization of both sources.[10]

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Response to disasters:

**Effectiveness of response greatly depends on a well-planned emergency behaviour, preparedness and early warning system.** Therefore, outlined below factors should be reflected in emergency planning. Timely evacuation is a challenging issue for small children, seniors, and persons with disabilities, especially with problems of moving and of persons with poor health. The elderly are more vulnerable to the effects of natural hazards (dialysis, cancer treatment) are faced with life threatening circumstances in disaster.

**Women’s, childrens’, and elders’ vulnerability is greater due to the mobility constraints. Both car ownership and having driver's license is less frequent among women than men.**

It is important to have list of CSOs dealing with vulnerable groups in the flooding area and include them in the phases of disaster management cycle.


## Impact of disasters and coping:

Psychological reactions following a disaster can be: withdrawal, distressing, angry, disturbed but also increase in family bond and social cohesion. Experience of natural hazards may result in stress, anxiety, depression, and other mood disturbances. Effect usually is not lasting, only 25 percent of people experience long-term effects. However, women are more vulnerable than men. Coping strategies of disaster affected population could include: raising of public awareness, information exchange and communication with citizens, improvement of preparedness, development of evacuation plans and preparation of evacuation centers, development of gender sensitive flood response plans (prior to floods), in training area.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Overexploiting resources in order to survive (e.g., cutting down trees for wood), liquidation of assets (e.g., livestock), reducing food intake (during the flood).

As women, more so than men, depend on natural resources for livelihood (23% of women employed in agriculture comparing to 19% men[6]), disaster has a more severe effect on women. It also exacerbates existing gender inequalities and reinforces traditional roles and access to scarce resources. As senior women considerably outnumber men, they are more likely to experience physical limitations that matter so much in emergencies. The burden of domestic work and care giving to children and the elderly falls disproportionately on women. Women’s ability to recover and thriving in aftermaths of disasters becomes more challenging for women. As men can decide to migrate due to property or employment loss, family income decreases but also, more and more women are left behind to provide for families; this is an issue as female-headed households increases in EU countries. The migration of family member also effects senior citizens as they have to look after grandchildren.

Limited power over production, representation, low involvement in consultation processes of key stakeholders make women more vulnerable as their perceptions and needs are not reflected in planning of recovery process. Gender stereotypes negatively reflect on men. Men are often overwhelmed by the need to provide for families and this can lead to increased alcohol related consequences and often stimulates substance abuse, domestic violence, gambling, and engagement in risky behaviours.

### Recommendations

The analysis above shows that in order to set up effective national and community based early warning systems, disaster informed planning and improved resilience, gender consideration need to be integrated into the project implementation. The existing gender inequality factors (e.g., limited engagement of women in planning and decision making) and traditional distribution of gender roles are barriers for women’s participation in recovery processes. Gender equality is a key issue-to-outreach beneficiaries of both genders equally. Based on the analysis of the gender aspects of vulnerability to climate-induced natural disasters a number of recommendations for the proposed project implementation have been developed. The following recommendations and the following Gender Action Plan are aimed at ensuring that the GCF project:

- narrows gender inequality;
- addresses the needs and constraints of women, girls, men, and boys;
- reveals risks and vulnerabilities under impacts;
- ensures women’s participation; promotes their leadership qualities;
- ensure women are included as planners, co-implementers and agents of change.

As a result of the project implementation more lives, property and productive assets will be secured from the impacts of climate induced disasters. Beneficiaries of this outcome will be all the population, but women's benefits will be bigger as for the livelihood women more than men

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

depend on natural resources. They also are poorer than men and hence proportionally looser more. Moreover, after flood, majority of women is left with household and some farm works since their husbands are going out for cash. Women again have to be in charge of all the remaining self-feeding activities such as planting seeds, weeding, harvesting or replanting vegetables for some small cash. Food security, psychological stresses, households' debts, and more labor-intensive works have degraded the wellbeing of women in the post-flood scenario. Therefore, a gender action plan has been developed to address these issues. The plan was designed to ensure that both men and women have full and equitable access to the Project's resources and benefits with specific actions and responsibilities aimed at ensuring the full participation of women in all aspects.

All Project activities, including additional analysis will be undertaken to ensure that the baseline data set is sex- and age-disaggregated and adequate for assessment of the gender impacts of the Project. At this time, the Gender Action Plan will be presented to the Project Implementation Team, including Government staff and consultants. In particular, the Project team leaders will ensure that the Gender Action Plan is integrated into all aspects of the Project implementation. The Plan upon mobilization. Progress reports will provide periodic updates on the effect of the Project on women, and regular UNDP review meetings and the midterm review meeting will review and monitor the progress.

## Carry out gender responsive vulnerability assessment

Ensure that risk assessments are informed by gender analysis.
- Enhance the Gender data to be additional needs, providing sex- and age-disaggregated data on social vulnerability across regions, assessing employment, education, health problems connecting with adequate functioning, disability, physical abilities, access to land, water, and other resources, access to markets, credit, and farming opportunities, ratio of dependent persons in households, household composition-single member male and female, women headed households, livelihoods, unpaid care and domestic work responsibilities.

Map/mapping of recent social assistance allowance assistance by analyzing social assistance data across chosen regions.
- Carrying out group discussions and in-depth interviews with key stakeholders, ensuring sex equality and representation of women over 65 disabled or family members of disabled, IDPs, community leaders and government officials for mapping hazards and risks, collection of existing community coping strategies, identifying local businesses and institutions that provide social assistance to vulnerable groups, including social safety nets, responses to separate coping mechanisms of women and men, elderly, disabled and poor.

Ensure collection of sex- and age-disaggregated data for project indicators

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Enhance gender considerations in disaster preparedness activities

- Disaster risk reduction and response plans should be based on gender analysis and include gender considerations.
- Men and women of different age groups, as well as those from vulnerable communities, should be included. At least one representative from each gender-sensitive group with at least 60 percent representation of less represented sex should be established.
- Ensure that information on hazards, their character, probability of occurrence, threats to life, property, livelihoods and the environment is shared equitably. Include measures in both women and men through appropriately tailored channels.
- Increase preparedness of educational institutions. Provide information to teachers, pupils and students about disaster risks and how they can be reduced. Ensure that they have active immediate response to them. Create emergency plans for kinder-gardens, schools, vocational institutions, and Universities. Inculde parents on them. Ensure they are practical and include gender considerations.
- Include Gender Mechanisms representative in disaster preparedness activities.

## Warrant universal access to disaster warnings

- Tailor warning to the gender-differentiated needs and capabilities of specific population groups such as children, senior citizens, IDPs, the sick and persons with disabilities.
- Set up community-based early warning systems and make sure that they are tailored to effectively serve both women and men based on prior gender responsive assessment of proposed solutions.
- Use multiple methods for targeting messages for reaching broadest group of people, including TV, radio, Internet, sirens, flashing lights, registration-based alert systems sending SMS or email, mobile phones and other emergency communication equipment. Use network of CSOs.
- Include pregnant women, IDPs the elderly and disabled in emergency planning. Include 50%.

## Mainstream gender considerations and engagement of women in resilience building activities

- Target at least 60 percent representation of less represented sex in capacity building and training activities supported by the project.
- Make sure that women have equal access to livelihood generating activities facilitated by the project.

# Annex 8 – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

- Disseminate information and stimulate involvement of population of the hazard prone regions in insurance schemes, among them insurance of crops.
- Disseminate information on risk zones with recommendations for construction of disaster resilient infrastructure.
- Support creation of employment guarantee opportunities for women and poor; ensure that women are part of employment guarantee schemes planned in the framework of the community preparedness plans.
- Strengthen social care infrastructure to provide services to redistribute burden of unpaid care work that falls on women. Provision of social care infrastructure can also generate jobs for women and men as part of disaster recovery.

## Conduct gender mainstreaming trainings and build capacity of national stakeholders targeted by project and responsible for various disaster risk reduction functions

- In 2016 UNDP Istanbul Regional Hub (IRH) developed and piloted a three-module training course on Gender Mainstreaming in Disaster preparedness and response (GMIDPR). The course is designed for UNDP and other organizations working in disaster preparedness and disaster response in the Europe and CIS region. In 2017 the tool was updated to include new modules, including gender equality and inclusion. However still there is a lack of knowledge within the governmental institutions, as well as CSOs on organisations on GMIDPR. There is a need to organise a series of training and capacity building sessions for national stakeholders involved in disaster risk reduction, aimed at targeting various stakeholders and practitioners (government, CSOs, community leaders, youth groups, women’s groups, farmers’ groups), as well as Gender institutional mechanisms at the national and local level.

## Engage women in decision making

- Ensure at least 40 percent representation of less represented sex and their active participation in project stakeholder consultations, local and national decision-making bodies set up and/or facilitated by the project. This includes ensuring that women are properly involved in the development and implementation phases of community preparedness plans and that the women of incorporated is all products are truly designed to promote and advance gender equality and social inclusion.
- Secure at least 40 percent of Gender Institutional Mechanism representatives from all the governmental level
- Line with the UNDP LOT 1025: Strengthening Women CSOs and women’s groups. Women’s advocacy will be involved in the process related to advocacy and awareness raising as well as development of risk financing and transfer mechanisms products, including insurance schemes, for women and girls.
- Women’s associations know their real needs, barriers and main risks, which can support and guide the project in its efforts to ensure that women are not left behind. Under equality advocates will be engaged with development and finance institutions, and governments, civil society and affected communities themselves – to develop mechanisms that bring transformative benefits to women and girls.

# Annex B – Gender Assessment and Gender action plan

## Green Climate Fund Full Proposal

### Affirmative Measures

* The recent UNDP’s Intersectional Analysis of the COVID-19 and Inflation on Women confirms that 55.5% of women experienced a worsened financial situation while Report on Mapping the Gender Component in Data and Legislation in the area of Climate Change, Environmental Management, and Disaster Risk Reduction (DRR) recommends to increase land/asset ownership in order for commercial banks to approve credit funds. Therefore, affirmative measures will be taken by awarding additional scores to projects that employ at least 50% women and companies that have achieved parity in managerial positions/boards, by awarding additional scores to such companies by virtue of being embedded in the project.

* Furthermore, feasibility studies on micro-insurance, regional disaster risk insurance programs, and other innovative financial instruments will be conducted. These studies focus (taking into account local dynamics and whether insurance products are sold through middlemen or directly to consumers) on understanding men’s and women’s needs for these products and could purchase them more easily), inform targeting and identify women centered solutions (for example, feasibility of women-specific micro-level risk insurance schemes.

### Requirements to the project staff

* Gender-responsive social vulnerability approach can be realized only by the gender and social vulnerability sensitive staff of the project. Therefore, project staff should be composed of both women and men represented at all levels of decision-making. Staff members should have a good understanding of gender equality and its importance and on social vulnerability approach. Also, it is suggested that staff members participate/complete a specific Gender mainstreaming in disaster preparedness and response training. Prior employing new staff, the ToRs can consist of request of knowledge on gender mainstreaming

### Impact Statement
Increase resilience and enhanced livelihood of the most vulnerable people including women and girls, communities, and regions through the implementation of gender-responsive climate risk and vulnerability information developed and used in decision-making.

### Outcome Statement
Gender-specific and gender-responsive climate risk and vulnerability information developed and used in decision-making: Reduced loss of life and assets of the most vulnerable including women, girls, disabled, old people through a strengthened country-wide early warning system (EWS) and disaster risk insurance scheme.

Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity for gender-sensitive climate response planning and decision-making: Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity to reduce exposure of the most vulnerable in particular female-headed households to climate risks through the development of a national flood and drought risk insurance scheme and other risk financing mechanisms; through the development of a national flood and disaster risk insurance scheme and other risk financing mechanisms.

Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity for gender-sensitive climate response planning and decision-making: Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity to reduce exposure of the most vulnerable in particular female-headed households to climate risks through the development of a national flood and drought risk insurance scheme and other risk financing mechanisms; through the development of a national flood and disaster risk insurance scheme and other risk financing mechanisms.

### Output 1: Fully integrated impact-based Flood Forecasting and EWS facilitates timely preparation and response.
Output Statement: Gender-specific and gender-responsive climate risk and vulnerability information developed and used in decision-making: Reduced loss of life and assets of the most vulnerable including women, girls, disabled, old people through a strengthened country-wide early warning system (EWS) and disaster risk insurance scheme.

### Output 2: Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity for gender-sensitive climate response planning and decision-making.
Output Statement: Gender-specific and gender-responsive climate risk and vulnerability information developed and used in decision-making: Reduced loss of life and assets of the most vulnerable including women, girls, disabled, old people through a strengthened country-wide early warning system (EWS) and disaster risk insurance scheme.

### Output 3: Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems and capacity for gender-sensitive climate response planning and decision-making.
Output Statement: Gender-specific and gender-responsive climate risk and vulnerability information developed and used in decision-making: Reduced loss of life and assets of the most vulnerable including women, girls, disabled, old people through a strengthened country-wide early warning system (EWS) and disaster risk insurance scheme.
